## Article #1

**Title:** Creating Business Value with AI (Part 1) - What I Learned from Cornell (Republished Version)

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/creating-business-value-ai-part-1-what-i-learned-from-lim-ywfmc/

**Content:**

Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program (Under Lutz Finger's instruction) transforms how business leaders and managers approach artificial intelligence implementation.
This first program module "Creating Business Value With AI" cuts through technical complexity and industry hype to focus on practical applications that is aligned with business objectives to create value. This systematic approach begins with identifying the right business problems, determines whether AI suits those problems, and identifies which AI product/solution approach generates the most value. The course design combines theoretical content with value-driven applications, including insightful speaker sessions with industry experts like Dr. Guy Berger, Ph.D., former Principal Economist of LinkedIn who provided valuable and practical perspectives on leveraging labor data analytics in the industry.
Differentiating Between AI Areas and Applications
One key learning was how to differentiate between AI areas and applications. The course outlined how Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI), deep learning, generative AI, and foundation models all serve different purposes. Rather than viewing newer AI technologies as inherently superior, I learned to match the approach to the specific business problem. For instance, rule-based GOFAI might be more appropriate for automated compliance checks, while deep learning excels at image recognition. This distinction helps avoid the common "hammer looking for a nail" approach to AI implementation.
Symbolic vs. Sub-Symbolic Logic: A Paradigm Shift
The evolution from symbolic (rule-based systems) to sub-symbolic logic (pattern-based systems) represents a fundamental shift in AI capabilities. Symbolic systems follow explicit rules that humans can understand, while sub-symbolic approaches like neural networks develop their own internal representations that can be difficult to interpret. This evolution has enabled the development of generative AI, which creates content rather than just analyzing it. The course beautifully illustrated how these different logical paradigms influence AI product development decisions.
The Agentic AI ROI Fundamental Ratio For Leaders and Business Managers
Business leaders and managers need a fundamental ratio approach to evaluate and measure the returns from agentic AI investments effectively.
This fundamental ratio enables quick assessment of intelligent risk-taking in a given business decision and determines whether a business decision is reversible ( or quickly corrected) - like a 'two-way door' (taking a leaf from Amazon's "Day 1" culture or innovation principle).
At its core, this example of a fundamental ratio compares value generated to its costs (or invested capital):
Agentic AI ROI Fundamental Ratio = (Net New Revenue Streams + Quantified Value of Efficiency Gains) / (Development Costs + Operational Costs + Risk Costs)
Organizations must account for the full spectrum of investment gains and potential liabilities, all of which must be carefully weighed against projected revenue generation and efficiency improvements to determine if the investment truly delivers actionable business value:
Net New Revenue Streams are additional streams of revenue (estimated at present value for multi-year projections using your organization's cost of capital to enable accurate investment comparison across the AI project and other projects) generated directly and specifically from agentic AI deployment/integration that would not exist otherwise - such as new product offerings, expanded market reach, or premium services enabled by AI agents' capabilities.
Efficiency Gains are positive financial gains per unit labor per unit cycle time (also estimated at present value for multi-year projections) from process improvements enabled by the agentic AI, including process acceleration, quality improvements, capacity reallocation, customer experience enhancements, and comparative advantage through specialization (where AI agents handle specialized, repetitive tasks within workflows, allowing for division of labor between human and AI resources).
Development Costs can include data scientist salaries/wages, model training infrastructure, and algorithm development
Operational Costs can include direct, variable costs of inference, cloud computing resources, maintenance personnel, and model retraining (without assuming any cost allocation methods to identify true costs of sale or costs of goods sold)
Risk Costs can include decline in quantified brand equity value, regulatory fines, CAPEX for regulatory compliance measures, business disruption costs due to model performance degradation. For agentic AI specifically, additional risk costs include liability from autonomous agent actions, negative outcomes from unforeseen agent behaviors, costs of maintaining appropriate human oversight, and in safety-critical applications, potential harm to users or others—which could represent an effectively infinite cost that would immediately render ROI calculation meaningless.
Clearly, the use case, industry and vertical matter greatly. For instance, when implementing an internal AI tool to enhance document review processes, we might measure the ROI fundamental ratio more comprehensively: $500,000 in net operational efficiency gains annually (including process acceleration, error reduction, and capacity reallocation to higher-value tasks) against $150,000 in development costs plus $50,000 yearly for operations and fine-tuning.
Over a three years period, this ratio estimates an expected yield 5.0x return - far preferable to investing in other solution options where costs significantly outweigh potential gains. This approach allows organizations to measure improvements not just in cost reduction but in value creation through improved quality at economies of scale, faster processing times, and enhanced employee capabilities.
Conversely, when this fundamental ratio falls below 1, it indicates a poor or unfavorable return on investment where costs exceed the created value.
When measuring efficiency gains in AI deployments in the ROI fundamental ratio's numerator, you can consider metrics such as
Process acceleration value - Quantify the mean/median financial benefit of completing tasks in less time
Quality improvement value - Calculate positive mean/median savings (costs reduced or avoided) from reduced errors and improved compliance
Capacity reallocation value - Measure efficiency gains from shifting human effort to higher-value tasks (e.g., an agentic AI system that autonomously processes 80% of customer support tickets could free up service representatives to focus on complex issue resolution and process improvement, generating $250,000 in incremental revenue per full-time employee (FTE) through reduced escalations and faster resolution times without increasing headcount; This differs from Net New Revenue Streams which captures additional revenue streams directly generated by an AI product)
Customer experience improvements - Quantify retention and satisfaction benefits in financial terms
Scalability without proportional cost - Value of handling volume increases without equivalent cost increases (e.g., capacity or maximum output that an organization can realistically achieve given current operational constraints)
This approach helps avoid Lavers' Law fallacy (where existing solutions are overlooked in favor of AI), by encouraging methodical comparison via decision trees or efficiency metrics.
Additionally, considering the time value of capital and competitive positioning creates a framework for intelligent risk-taking in AI investment—treating it as an infinite game where strategic positioning or outpacing Returns on Assets (ROA) relative to competition often matters more than short-term returns.
Also consider Anthropic's Model Context Protocol (MCP), MCP represents a significant advancement in connecting AI systems with data sources.
As an open standard like OpenAPI, MCP allows developers to build secure, bidirectional connections between AI-powered tools and the systems where data resides, including content repositories, business tools, and development environments. This protocol addresses a fundamental challenge in AI deployment: breaking down information silos to provide models with the context they need to produce more relevant responses.
Rather than maintaining separate connectors for each data source, developers can now build against this universal standard.
As early adopters like Block and Apollo demonstrate, MCP enables AI agents to retrieve relevant information more effectively, enhancing their ability to understand complex tasks and produce more nuanced, functional outputs with fewer attempts.
This standardized approach not only simplifies implementation but also creates a sustainable architecture for context-aware AI that will significantly enhance the value of our AI investments while reducing development costs (e.g. retraining or finetuning) and integration complexities.
This potentially adjusts for new value of the ROI fundamental ratio.
Conclusion: From Theory to Strategic Planning & Decision Making
Throughout Cornell University's eCornell "Creating Business Value With AI" program module, the emphasis remains clear: Effective AI implementation is not about chasing the latest technology, but about identifying the right problem, selecting the appropriate AI approach or technology, and measuring business value through comprehensive frameworks like the AI ROI Fundamental Ratio.
As organizations navigate the rapidly evolving AI landscape, success will increasingly depend on this disciplined approach—balancing innovation with practical application, technical capability with business need, and short-term gains with long-term strategic positioning. Advancements like Anthropic's MCP further illustrate how the ecosystem continues to mature, creating new opportunities for value creation while potentially improving that crucial ROI fundamental ratio value.
For business leaders and managers, the path forward requires continuous learning and thoughtful experimentation.
Rather than asking "How can we use AI?", the more powerful question becomes "Which specific business challenges can we solve more effectively with the right AI approach?" By maintaining this problem-first mindset and leveraging frameworks discussed in this course, organizations can move beyond the hype to deliver measurable, sustainable business value with artificial intelligence.
#ArtificialIntelligence #BusinessStrategy #AIBusiness #FirstPrinciples #AIImplementation #CornellAlumni #CornellJohnsonAlumni
If you missed the other parts of this learning series about eCornell's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program, you can find all articles here in this newsletter.

## Article #2

**Title:** Creating Business Value with AI (Part 2) - What I Learned from Cornell

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/creating-business-value-ai-part-2-what-i-learned-from-lim-nzljc/

**Content:**

As I continue my journey through Cornell University's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program on the eCornell platform (Instructed by Lutz Finger), I am excited to reflect on new additional insights from the first module focused on "Creating Business Value With AI."
This article builds on my previous article exploring the fundamental ratio for evaluating AI investments.
The Heart of AI Implementation: The "So What?" Question
What struck me most about this module was the emphasis on asking ourselves the fundamental question: "So what?" Every AI implementation must answer this simple yet profound challenge. If we cannot articulate why our AI solution matters and what specific action it enables, we likely should not proceed with the business decision for development. This perspective shifted my thinking from technology-driven solutions to value-driven implementations - a crucial distinction in today's AI-saturated and 'noisy' landscape.
Value Creation in the AI Era
The first module "Creating Business Value With AI" thoughtfully explored how AI initiatives must generate tangible business value that exceeds their implementation costs. With the emergence of foundation models, we are witnessing a democratization of AI capabilities, making sophisticated solutions accessible to organizations without the vast resources of tech giants.
AI Product Development Framework for AI Product Managers: Lutz Finger also taught a highly-comprehensive and flywheel-like Al product development framework for developing AI products with actionable value - starting with defining the business value and concluding with implementation and feedback loops.
This systematic AI product development approach ensures that AI solutions are built with clear purpose and measurable outcomes.
Industry Perspectives That Broadened My Horizon
Lutz Finger had also invited industry leaders to feature conversations that provided valuable real-world context to understanding the business value of AI:
Dr. Guido Appenzeller, who has held leadership positions at Intel and VMware and is currently at Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), shared fascinating perspectives on how foundation models are creating a new building block for system design. His prediction that this shift could lead to trillion-dollar companies in the coming years was particularly thought-provoking.
John Buckman, CEO and founder of Decent Espresso Machines, demonstrated how data science and AI technologies are transforming even traditional industries like coffee-making. His innovative approach showed how AI can create unexpected value in specialized domains.
Measure to Improve: The Metrics Behind Successful AI Implementations & Investments
"If you can't measure it, you can't improve it."
While often attributed to Peter Drucker, this saying perfectly captures the approach to AI implementation taught by Lutz Finger in the program.
Beyond the AI ROI fundamental ratio I discussed in my previous article, organizations also need to establish quantified metrics for data quality, model performance, output quality, and bias & fairness evaluation.
The semantics of the English language (or any language) makes generative AI (GenAI) output quality hard to assess subjectively.
Taking large language models for example, the subjective nature of language comprehension makes establishing concrete metrics for GenAI evaluation both crucial and complex.
The English language is perceived and comprehended differently based on one's experiences, biases/priors, age, generations, cultures, socioeconomic norms, and geographies - even amongst native speakers.
This 'Tower of Babel' phenomenon that often afflicts business communications, intercultural communications and stakeholder alignment will be apparent when humans assess GenAI output quality in a subjective manner.
In the case of image and video generation models, the difference in semantic comprehension also apply to visual images or videos (excluding obvious quality features such as noise, grain, pixel-level variations, flickering, jerkiness, floating, color, distortion, and motion) as different viewers may interpret the same visual content differently based on their cultural context, personal experiences, and cognitive biases.
Even during my undergraduate days at the National University of Singapore, there were abundant, pre-existing and peer-reviewed research literature which showed that languages are used differently across social groups, can evolve over time and vary across regions (Note: I will consider adding citations to give examples of these research literature at a later time).
To address this challenge, organizations should establish grounded and continuous evaluation & measurement frameworks using GenAI metrics including (but not limited to):
Accuracy (factual correctness)
Recall (comprehensiveness)
Area Under the Curve or AUC (statistical measure of model discrimination ability across different thresholds; Ideally optimized for above 50% threshold)
Relevance (alignment with user intent)
Faithfulness (adherence to source material)
Confusion matrix analysis (error type categorization)
Toxicity (model's propensity to generate harmful, offensive, or malicious content)
For the more advanced adopters: I would even recommend a Composite Quality Score (CQS). This Composite Quality Score (CQS) uses an organization-specific weighted combination of the aforementioned metrics using logistic regression to create a binary "acceptable/unacceptable" classifier based on strategically-defined threshold values)
The Composite Quality Score (CQS) metric approach can be strategically relevant and valuable to organizations because:
It allows organizations to encode and formalize their consensus and acceptance criteria across different metrics
It is easier to measure, track and continuously refined as more evaluation data becomes available - reducing execution friction and development tax
It transforms multiple complex metrics into a clear decision point
It enables systematic tracking of quality over time and across model versions & AI projects
You create an information asymmetry about your organization's strategic metrics and efficiency gains about your AI implementation - changing the 'rules' of the game for your competitor players in game theory.
Most importantly, these measurement metrics must anchor in organizationally-agreed AI labels of quality output and standards that are aligned with strategic relevance and user/customer needs. Here, the AI label of a GenAI system can be one or multiple data samples that are agreed to be of good quality. These "gold standard" labels also double as benchmarks - representing outputs that the organization has collectively determined meet or exceed quality thresholds across multiple dimensions of evaluation.Credit: Image by Sabrina Jiang © Investopedia 2020Credit: Image by Sabrina Jiang © Investopedia 2020Credit: Image by Sabrina Jiang © Investopedia 2020
AI's Competitive Advantage Through Specialization
“The question is, ‘What would Carl Sagan do?’ He would take it back to first principles.” — Randall (Leonardo DiCaprio), Don't Look Up (2021)
In my own perspective using First Principles thinking, one of the most compelling drivers of AI deployment is how it creates efficiency through specialization (or learning rate) in your organization.
In business & economic terms, efficiency is a function of specialization or learning - provided your organization or enterprise is measuring specialization or 'blended learning rate' of your whole organization.
In this AI supercycle, we can recognize that specialization can source from human users and/or the weights of your AI model/neural network. For now, we will ignore the sub-symbolic and probabilistic nature of activating a neural network (or neural net) with a large language model (LLM) architecture, and steering latent embeddings (We will discuss this in later articles).
Anecdotally, I have observed from my past careers how a higher 'learning rate' drives higher success for individual project or a team - compared to other teams in the same company. While they are associated concepts, this 'learning rate' in the context of an individual, team or entire organization is not the same as the learning rate in machine learning terms.
In an example of my career at Amazon Web Services (AWS), we undergo a routine of learning, testing and certifications for a specific technology every month - encompassing business use case(s), documented business impact, historical customer metrics, core technology concepts, architectural designs and implementation. I would not be surprised if AWS is measuring and optimizing a company-wide 'net blended learning rate' from these enablement initiatives across its global organizations.
Efficiency is a Competitive Advantage Driven by Specialization from AI: Specialization has long been recognized as one of the formal competitive advantages in business. Specialization is "a method of production whereby an entity focuses on the production of a limited scope of goods to gain a greater degree of efficiency". This concept of specialization has strong foundations in economic principles related to comparative advantage and division of labor.
AI accelerates specialization or learning by enabling human workers to focus on higher-value tasks while automating routine work. This creates and captures higher overall value for the organization. Value can be quantified as % net new revenue growth, % expansion revenue growth, net revenue retention rate, Return on Marketing Investment (ROMI) ratio, Customer Acquisition Cost (CAC) per logo, SaaS Magic Number or even a customer experience score ratio (e.g., Net Promoter Score) from AI-attributed activities.
Your efficiency in a select dimension is only good or bad relative to competition: As taught by Dr. Gautam Ahuja (who is one of the world's most cited scholars in advanced business strategy) at Cornell Johnson Graduate School of Management, specialization can be represented on an efficiency curve of at least two (or more) dimensions. This efficiency curve framework provides clarity on your firm's positioning relative to competitors, revealing specific areas where you can establish competitive advantage.
The goal is not to be "middle of the pack" on this efficiency curve. You want to clearly differentiate through an advantage by optimizing tradeoffs and maximizing for a specific dimension (e.g., Walmart leads in cost efficiency) - while residing on the efficiency curve. Any point/position on the efficiency curve is known as the 'efficiency frontier'. When your AI investments operate on the 'efficiency frontier,' you are fully maximizing value creation at the lowest possible cost. This is your 'objective function' or 'loss minimization function' from your AI investments.
The efficiency curve example from Investopedia (Shown below) illustrates production possibility tradeoffs between Wine and Cotton based on business decisions. In the GenAI context, your efficiency frontier might involve balancing tradeoffs between quality metrics like relevance and faithfulness.
Considering how hallucination and creativity/innovation are 'two sides of the same coin' in GenAI, your strategic position on this curve reflects your deliberate choice between factual accuracy and innovative thinking based on your specific business needs.
Credit: Image by Sabrina Jang, Investopedia 2020
With proper measurement of business value and strategic tradeoff decisions, you gain clear visibility into your competitive positioning through quantifiable efficiency gains from AI investments. Voilà!
Beyond operational efficiency gains, businesses operating as AI software providers can unlock entirely new revenue streams, creating a dual pathway to value creation that compounds over time.
A Real-World Example: AI-Enhanced Content Personalization Engine for B2B Demand Generation
Let me illustrate what measurement looks like for a practical example by assuming an internal use case of an advanced content personalization engine for B2B demand generation.
For simplicity, I refrained from using an example of an agentic AI content creation system which will include more complex designs and metrics.
Agentic AI and its AI agent components can have complex designs: An agentic AI system would incorporate multiple coordinated AI agents working within a a more complex architecture that includes workflow orchestration, goal-setting capabilities, autonomous decision-making processes, closed-loop learning mechanisms, and 'robust' adaptation protocols. These agentic AI systems can execute multiple tasks by navigating and adapting to various environments - from handling interface changes in connected applications to responding appropriately when third-party APIs are updated or modified (unless there are radical changes in font type, size and pixels on the web browser or SaaS tooling user interface that challenge the generalization of the agentic AI system).
For readers interested in the distinction between AI agents and agentic AI systems, I recommend the recent research by Sapkota, Roumeliotis, and Karkee (2023), "AI Agents vs. Agentic AI: A Conceptual Taxonomy, Applications and Challenges." (Link to paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10468). Their work provides a comprehensive taxonomy that clarifies the divergent design philosophies and capabilities between these related but very distinct approaches to AI systems.
1/ Traditional B2B Demand Generation Approach: In conventional strategies, marketing teams create limited sets of content assets targeting broad customer segments - a process that is typically time-consuming, expensive, and often results in generic messaging. These materials undergo lengthy approval processes and frequently require expensive third-party agencies. This came from my own experiences having led B2B product marketing and GTM during my previous career.
2/ The Modern B2B Demand Generation Approach would mean implementing an NLP-driven content personalization engine with a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system. This modern marketing team could:
Connect to corporate branding guidelines, tone of voice documentation, approved messaging frameworks and even specific sales & marketing campaign briefs stored in data repositories
Generate hyper-personalized emails, landing pages, and other assets that speak directly to each prospect's unique situation by analyzing prospect data, website behavior, and industry-specific challenges
Scale content production dramatically without increasing headcount or agency spend - Effectively reducing the marginal cost of content creation
The implementation would involve:
Setting up a knowledge base with brand guidelines, product information, and success stories
Integrating with CRM, marketing automation platforms or a customer data platform (CDP)
Implementing feedback loops to track engagement metrics and continually refine the model
This approach would transform the software business's B2B demand generation from a one-size-fits-all model to a dynamic, personalized experience that speaks directly to each prospect's needs while maintaining brand consistency for any campaign run - accelerating the sales & marketing cycle and improving ROI efficiency on marketing investments.
Beyond quantifying the business value of this AI-powered B2B demand generation engine, we will also need a comprehensive measurement framework that addresses operational performance, technical robustness, and governance requirements. This includes metrics that facilitate troubleshooting, provide audit-ready documentation, and deliver transparent design explainability to both leadership and end users."
To build a truly comprehensive AI measurement framework, organizations must identify both leading indicators that predict future success and lagging indicators that validate past performance. This dual approach allows teams to both anticipate outcomes and verify results.
For leading indicators, consider metrics that provide early signals of AI system effectiveness. These predictive measurements help teams course-correct or troubleshoot before business outcomes are affected or escalations for SEV-1 incidents.
For lagging indicators, focus on metrics that demonstrate concrete business value, even if they emerge more slowly.
Beyond business metrics, technical performance indicators such as read-write rates, inference runtime, and output generation speed provide crucial visibility into system reliability and scalability. These operational measurements ensure your AI solutions can meet increasing demands while maintaining consistent performance.
Assuming our real world example of an AI Content Creation Engine for B2B Demand Generation, examples of leading indicators are system and engineering performance metrics such as:
System Performance Metrics: Retrieval precision and recall rates, Query processing latency (milliseconds), Context window utilization percentage, Vector database query throughput, Chunk relevance scores, Token processing rate, Cache hit ratio, Embedding quality metrics
Content Quality Metrics: Content coherence scores, Content relevance to user query, Hallucination detection rates, Source citation accuracy, Guidelines adherence score, Brand voice consistency rating
Examples of lagging indicators are business and product metrics such as:
User Engagement Metrics: Daily Active Users (DAU), Weekly Active Users (WAU), Monthly Active Users (MAU), Queries per user, Click-through rates on personalized content, Time spent with generated content, Content completion rates, Response action rates, Follow-up query frequency, User feedback scores
Business Impact Metrics: Conversion rate improvements, Content production velocity, Marketing team efficiency gains, Cost per personalized asset, Revenue attributed to personalized content, Customer satisfaction scores
Examples of operational metrics are engineering performance metrics such as:
Read-write rates for vector database performance
Inference runtime impacts on user experience
Speed of output generation for scalability
Retrieval latency (time to fetch relevant context)
End-to-end response time
Memory utilization during peak loads
Resource consumption per request
System stability under varying traffic
Warm/cold start performance differences
Token processing efficiency
Not all use cases are made equal or comparable for measurement: These measurement considerations can differ significantly between internal use cases like content creation and external, mission-critical use cases like fraud detection across high-volume financial transactions.
In the latter, error rates and processing speed become much more critical to business value than in marketing content generation, where creative quality and engagement metrics may take precedence.
Recalling my previous article where I introduce my own take on the AI ROI Fundamental Ratio:
ROI Fundamental Ratio = (Net New Revenue Streams + Quantified Value of Efficiency Gains) / (Development Costs + Operational Costs + Risk Costs)
The Risk Costs between the marketing content creation use case and financial fraud detection use case also vary significantly.
Conclusion
As I continue through Cornell University's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, I am struck by how this first module "Creating Business Value with AI" has transformed my approach to AI implementation, product development and investments.
The emphasis on defining actionable business value before rushing to technological solutions provides a crucial framework that will serve me well in future AI projects.
The insights gained from industry leaders like Dr. Guido Appenzeller and John Buckman have broadened my perspective on how foundation models are creating new building blocks for system design across industries. Their real-world applications demonstrate that AI's true power comes not from technology alone, but from its strategic application to specific business challenges.
In my own perspective using First Principles, one of the most compelling drivers of AI deployment is how it drives efficiency through specialization (or learning rate) in organizations. AI helps record learning in its weights and accelerates the learning rate of individuals, teams & organizations.
Perhaps most valuable has been learning to rigorously measure the business value of AI implementations. As the saying goes, "If you can't measure it, you can't improve it."
Through comprehensive frameworks like my AI ROI Fundamental Ratio and detailed metrics for system performance, content quality, user engagement, and business impact, we can move beyond technological fascination to ensure our AI investments realize investment returns.
The B2B content personalization example we explored demonstrates how AI can transform traditional approaches by enabling hyper-personalization at scale, but only when implemented with clearly-defined business objectives and concrete while robust measurement systems.
And as we examined, evolving this system into a truly agentic AI would require additional capabilities like autonomous decision-making frameworks, industry intelligence, execution autonomy, and sophisticated control mechanisms.
As I look forward to the next modules in this program, I am excited to build upon this foundation of business-first AI implementations and product development. The lessons learned about measuring business value, establishing meaningful metrics, and aligning AI capabilities with strategic objectives will prove invaluable to everyone who joins the eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program with the rest of the previous cohorts: https://ecornell.cornell.edu/certificates/technology/designing-and-building-ai-solutions/
What metrics do you use to continuously evaluate AI projects and investments in your organization? I would love to hear about your experiences in the comments below.

## Article #3

**Title:** Creating Business Value with AI (Part 3) - What I Learned from Cornell

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/creating-business-value-ai-part-3-what-i-learned-from-lim-zjm5c/

**Content:**

Asking the Right Questions: The Foundation of AI Success
The most profound lesson from Lutz Finger's instruction in Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program was that successful AI implementation is not about technical proficiency but rather about identifying real business value. Through engaging examples and hands-on exercises, Dr. Finger demonstrated that the specific technology used matters far less than the measurable outcomes produced.
The program emphasized beginning with clear business objectives before considering technological solutions. This value-first approach ensures that AI implementations create meaningful impact rather than just following technological trends.
The Value Proposition First Approach and the "So What" Litmus Test
The program's first module "Creating Business Value with AI" emphasized beginning with a well-defined value proposition before obsessing over technical implementation details right at the beginning.
Finger repeatedly stressed the importance of the "So what?" question as a litmus test before execution - ensuring that any AI implementation actually creates measurable business value of strategic relevance.
The most valuable framework I gained was the disciplined application of the "so what?" question to any AI initiative.
If an AI solution cannot answer this fundamental question about its business value, you either need to dig deeper or you should not have started in the first place.
This simple yet powerful lens helps distinguish between technologies that merely fascinate and those that truly create measurable value.
“The answer to the ultimate question of life, the universe, and everything is 42.”– Douglas Adams, The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy
In the book "The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy", they overengineered and built a supercomputer called Deep Thought to calculate the Answer to Everything — but forget to define the Question (which remains unknown by everyone in the plot). Sound familiar?
Hands-On Learning Through Real Applications
The most engaging aspect of the module was the guest session with John Buckman, founder of Decent Espresso Machines, who demonstrated how measurement and data analysis are transforming the coffee making industry.
This real-world coffee-making example brilliantly illustrated how scientific measurement, data collection, and pattern recognition can create value through measurement, actionable prediction and optimization - fundamental principles that apply across industries.
This first module "Creating Business Value with AI" in the eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program also included practical exercises where we evaluated AI product concepts across diverse domains like marketing strategies, credit risk prediction, deforestation prevention, suicide risk assessment, hospital flow optimization and even coffee preparation.
Each exercise reinforced the importance of defining actionable value before implementing any technology solution. The practical, application-focused nature of these exercises ensured the concepts were not only theoretical but also immediately applicable to real business challenges of today.
The New Economics of AI Implementation
New AI tools have fundamentally transformed the execution and marginal costs of a wide array of undifferentiated and low-value tasks - reinforcing the importance of intentionally and strategically defining business value and business objectives from AI implementations.
As I discussed in my previous article about AI's competitive advantage, low-code and no-code AI tools deliver efficiency gains to users by harnessing the specialized 'intelligence' embedded in large language model weights, unique retrieval-augmented techniques that incorporate existing data/context into input prompts and test-time compute in 'reasoning' models.
With low-code and no-code AI tools (e.g., code-generation, envisioning UI) of today, successful AI deployments have higher weights on assessing business value, making good business decisions, running efficient experiments before investing a significant amount of resources & capital in full-scale implementations.
A Real-World Example: Building an Applicant Tracking System with AI in 1+ Days
I experienced this paradigm shift firsthand as a non-professional developer through a recent AI & Machine Learning hobby project called TalentSol.
Yes, you read right. Using Augment Code Agent and VS Code as IDE environment, I completed the bulk of frontend development in just 1.5 days - a process that would traditionally take weeks.
If you would like to try it out with the rest of us, do check out @augment here >>> https://www.augmentcode.com/
TalentSol is a prototype applicant tracking system designed to "revolutionize how companies manage their recruitment pipeline through intelligent candidate matching and workflow optimization." Built with React 18, TypeScript, Vite, and Tailwind CSS, this project demonstrates how AI code-generation tools can dramatically accelerate development cycles.
The project served as a practical application of concepts from my eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, specifically around machine learning model development for recruitment use cases, as opposed to user interface development. v0.1 frontend features include candidate pipeline management, interview scheduling, messaging, and an analytics dashboard, with AI-powered candidate prioritization models planned for future releases.
What impressed me most about Augment Code Agent was its contextual awareness of overall software architecture - automatically updating dependencies and routing across the entire codebase when implementing new features. Augment Code Agent is also proven to be phenomenally reliable and accurate when troubleshooting errors - automatically resolving issue root causes during the extent of this project. My role is to review the Augment Code Agent's proposed changes and approve them - almost like a pseudo-engineering lead.
The chat interface functioned more than a code generator It also serves as a fractional CTO, offering creative insights and troubleshooting complex errors with remarkable reliability, especially when discussing architectural design decisions in my prompts.
This rapid development experience demonstrates how AI tools are reshaping software development timelines and capabilities. For a visual overview of what 1.5 days of AI-assisted development can produce, including UI screenshots and technical implementation details, visit the TalentSol GitHub repository [Full link: https://github.com/youshen-lim/TalentSol---Applicant-Tracking-System-Application].

Analytics ML Reports Page
Candidate Messaging Page
Candidate Document Management Page
Creating Business Value With AI: Further Insights from the Cornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program
Throughout Cornell's eCornell program and Lutz Finger's instruction, I have learnt that effective AI implementation requires balancing technical capabilities with clear business/organizational/societal objectives. The program's curriculum content and hands-on exercises - from evaluating deforestation prevention systems to optimizing hospital flow - provided practical frameworks for distinguishing genuine innovation from fleeting hype. Most importantly, I learned that the "So what?" question must drive every AI initiative.
As foundation models continue to democratize access to powerful AI tools, the competitive advantage shifts from technical implementation to strategic vision and business acumen. The future belongs not to those who merely deploy AI technology, but to those who leverage it to address meaningful problems with measurable outcomes. Whether you are a business leader or technology professional, success in the AI era depends on your ability to identify where automation creates genuine value and to build solutions that deliver it consistently.
Do join us all in your AI product development journey today on eCornell: https://ecornell.cornell.edu/certificates/technology/designing-and-building-ai-solutions/
Have you experienced how low-code AI tools are transforming product development in your organization? I would love to hear about your experiences in the comments!

## Article #4

**Title:** Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (Part 1) - What I Learned from Cornell's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/exploring-good-old-fashioned-ai-what-i-learned-from-lim-ynmqc/

**Content:**

Introduction to Symbolic, Rule-based AI in Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI)
Continuing my journey through Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program under Lutz Finger's expert instruction, I dove into the 2nd module "Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI)". This follows after the first program module which is "Creating Business Value with AI".
In this article, I will explore my key learning about symbolic, rule-based AI systems - foundational approach in the broad domain of GOFAI (also known as classical or traditional AI/Machine Learning).
This exploration of these early AI paradigms revealed valuable perspectives on AI approaches that continue to play crucial roles in modern applications.
Understanding Rule-Based AI Systems as the First Wave of AI
The first part of "Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI)" module covers symbolic, rule-based AI and its transparent, logical frameworks for decision-making - a refreshing contrast to today's often 'opaque' neural nets (e.g. large language models, CNNs, autoencoders).
I gained appreciation for the elegance of symbolic, rule-based AI systems built on the following fundamental components:
Clearly-defined symbolic representations
Logical predicates
IF/THEN rules
Logical connectives (e.g., Boolean operators).
This framework allows computers to perform logical operations based on human-defined or machine-learned rules - a process which I find simple yet powerful. At the same time, AI decisions in symbolic, rule-based systems are logical, transparent, interpretable and auditable.
Practical Learning Through Exercises
In this "Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI)" module, Lutz Finger had also designed particularly insightful exercises involving the development of rule-based systems to generalize to complex real-world scenarios (e.g., designing a robot waiter at a restaurant for every step/action/response).
This hands-on work perfectly illustrated why purely rule-based systems struggle with generalization. The airport example was particularly powerful - air travel seems automated until something goes wrong (like a missing bag), then human intervention becomes necessary because we could not automate every possible scenario.
Knowing the capabilities and limitations of purely rule-based approaches helps me understand why successful modern AI often combines traditional symbolic methods with other machine learning techniques.
Insights from Yann Lechelle
A highlight of this module was the guest lecture by Yann Lechelle, a serial entrepreneur who shared valuable insights on integrating AI into business ventures. Lutz Finger knew Lechelle from their INSEAD connection.
Lechelle's discussion about NetVestibule (a social networking platform for admitted students at business schools) demonstrated how even simple data analysis could provide valuable business insights - like predicting which admitted students would actually enroll based on their platform engagement.
He also emphasized the importance of domain knowledge alongside technical skills, advising students not to discard their non-technical backgrounds when entering the technology field.
Enduring Relevance of Symbolic, Rule-Based AI in Modern Systems
Symbolic Rule-Based Systems as the First Wave of AI: The Cornell program's exploration of symbolic, rule-based AI fundamentals sparked my curiosity about these approaches in today's rapidly evolving AI landscape. What I learned is that symbolic, rule-based AI - the very first wave of AI - remains surprisingly relevant even in modern AI systems, including the large language model-based systems and other generative model-based systems that we see today.
This approach, characterized by explicit symbols, logical predicates, and IF/THEN rules, provides the deterministic decision-making framework that makes today's AI systems reliable and trustworthy.
From Human Intuition to Deterministic Business Rules: One key insight from the module was understanding that while humans operate with intuition, business decisions require deterministic yes/no decisions for causality, consistency and auditability. Symbolic, rule-based AI provides this reliability by using explicit cutoff values to transform uncertain situations into discrete categories - exactly what businesses need for clear, auditable decisions that can be explained and defended.
Why Symbolic, Rule-Based AI Remains Indispensable in Businesses: Businesses and enterprise applications demand interpretability, reliability, and deterministic behavior, which rule-based symbolic AI provides, while neural networks contribute contextual understanding and natural language processing. The persistence of rule-based symbolic AI in modern systems is not nostalgic - it is necessary. The complexity of modern AI guardrails requires sophisticated solution design methods to manage multiple, sometimes conflicting requirements that pure neural approaches cannot reliably handle. Symbolic, rule-based AI provides the structured framework that ensures AI systems remain controllable and auditable. Modern AI systems increasingly implement hybrid architectures where symbolic, rule-based AI and neural components work together synergistically. These systems leverage the predictability and reliability of first-wave approaches for critical decision points while using neural networks for natural language understanding and generation tasks. This addresses real-world requirements for controllability, auditability, and deterministic behavior that make AI systems trustworthy in production environments. Enterprise applications specifically demand:
Interpretability: Clear explanation of how decisions are made
Reliability: Consistent behavior across scenarios
Deterministic Control: Predictable responses for business-critical decisions
Auditability: Traceable decision paths for compliance
Symbolic, rule-based AI delivers these requirements through explicit logic that humans can understand, modify, and trust.
Complementary Approaches between Rule-Based AI and Machine Learning: What surprised me most was learning that the most sophisticated AI systems today are not purely neural nets (or neural networks). As explored in the paper 'Neurosymbolic AI: The 3rd Wave' (Full link to paper: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2012.05876), the integration of symbolic reasoning with neural approaches represents the next evolution in AI development.
These hybrid AI systems are carefully designed combinations where:
Symbolic, rule-based AI provides reliability, control, and interpretability
Machine learning handles fine pattern recognition and probabilistic predictions
This hybrid approach also addresses real business needs for AI systems around nuanced pattern recognition, reliability, control, and interpretability.
Where Rule-Based Symbolic AI Powers Modern Systems: Through my learning from the program coursework, I learned about key areas where first-wave AI principles remain essential to this day:
1/ AI Safety and Control Systems: Modern AI guardrails use classic symbolic, rule-based logic, applying IF/THEN rules to monitor and control AI outputs within defined business and safety boundaries. Contemporary guardrail systems implement what researchers classify as neural-symbolic architectures—essentially evolved GOFAI systems that incorporate neural components:
Type 1 Neural-Symbolic Systems: Like Llama Guard and NeMo Guardrails, where "the input and output of a learning agent are symbolic"—pure GOFAI input/output with neural processing
Type 2 Neural-Symbolic Systems: Like Guardrails AI, which "consists of a backbone symbolic algorithm supported by learning algorithms"—GOFAI architecture enhanced by neural capabilities
2/ GOFAI Languages Powering Modern AI:
RAIL (Reliable AI Markup Language): Used by Guardrails AI as "a language-agnostic and human-readable format for specifying specific rules and corrective actions for LLM outputs"—classic GOFAI principles applied to modern AI systems
Colang Programming Language: NVIDIA's NeMo Guardrails employs this "executable program language designed to establish constraints and guide LLMs within set dialogical boundaries"
3/ Business Process Automation: When companies need consistent, explainable decisions - like routing customer requests, validating data formats, or enforcing compliance rules - they rely on symbolic rule systems rather than neural networks. This ensures predictable, auditable outcomes that businesses can trust.
4/ System Coordination and Workflow Management: Complex AI applications often involve multiple components working together. Rule-based workflow management—using the same symbolic logic principles from first-wave AI - orchestrates these interactions reliably and predictably.
5/ Modern Communication Protocols are also Rule-Based: The growing interest in AI agents has sparked development of communication standards like Anthropic's Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Google's Agent-to-Agent (A2A) protocol. While these are infrastructure and communication protocols rather than AI reasoning systems themselves, they demonstrate how rule-based structures remain essential for building reliable AI systems that can work together effectively. These protocols use the same symbolic representation and rule-based principles that also define symbolic, rule-based AI.
Setting the Stage for Machine Learning: Understanding symbolic, rule-based AI as the first-wave in this Cornell program is not just historical knowledge - it is fundamental to appreciating why machine learning emerged as a complementary approach. Where rule-based systems excel at control and interpretability, machine learning excels at pattern recognition and generalization from data. Rather than being replaced by neural networks, symbolic, rule-based AI has evolved to become the essential control infrastructure that makes modern AI systems reliable and safe. We will explore how these foundational symbolic approaches combine with machine learning techniques to create more adaptive and powerful AI solutions.
In my next article, I shall cover my learning and reflections about machine learning - the second wave of AI that learns patterns from data rather than following pre-programmed rules. Together, these approaches form the foundation of modern AI systems that are both intelligent and trustworthy.
Real World Example: Applying Symbolic, Rule-Based AI to TalentSol
Credit: Screenshot of the hypothetical TalentSol Applicant Tracking System for my AI/ML project
The technical implementation is available on my GitHub: https://github.com/youshen-lim/TalentSol---Applicant-Tracking-System-Application
Building on my previous article, TalentSol serves as my hobbyist AI/machine learning project developed with Augment Code - though I should clarify that while the technical implementation is real, the applicant tracking system itself is hypothetical rather than a deployed business solution. This project fulfills two key personal objectives:
1/ I was curious about using low-code, no-code AI tools like Augment Code Agent for highly efficient code generation and application development. Call it "vibe-coding" but you still have to understand application architecture designs for frontend and backend development. Brian Foote once said, 'If you think good architecture is expensive, try bad architecture." Nevertheless, it is very impressive to have Augment Code as a AI development partner and 'fractional CTO' to execute frontend and backend development. For a non-professional coder like myself and entry-level engineers, this democratization of code development is a huge leap in efficiency.
2/ I had to create an actionable user interface to serve my machine learning models that would otherwise remain command-line experiments using Python. Consider the benefits of ChatGPT's 'chatbot' user interface to serve OpenAI's o3 model. I am now dedicated to applying Don Norman's design principles to user interface design and full-stack development, which takes more effort and time beyond a Kaggle data project.
The technical implementation is available on my GitHub repo: https://github.com/youshen-lim/TalentSol---Applicant-Tracking-System-Application
Despite being a hypothetical system, TalentSol provides an ideal case study for implementing symbolic, rule-based AI principles because recruitment decisions inherently follow logical frameworks that align naturally with the deterministic, transparent nature of rule-based systems.
Applying Symbolic, Rule-Based AI to Recruitment: Even in this hypothetical context, the business logic remains compelling.
Recruitment decisions follow clear logical patterns like "If candidate has X years of experience AND possesses required skill Y AND meets visa requirements, then prioritize for review."
This natural alignment between business logic and IF/THEN rule structures makes TalentSol perfect for exploring how first-wave AI methods deliver transparent, auditable decision-making.
Unlike some of the other "black-box" AI approaches, symbolic AI systems provide the explainability that any real HR team would need - every decision can be traced through clear logical steps, ensuring regulatory compliance and building trust with candidates & hiring managers.
Practical GOFAI Implementation Approaches: I identified several implementation examples for symbolic, rule-based AI that leverage its transparent, deterministic nature.
These GOFAI solutions can provide immediate business value while maintaining the explainability that modern HR users, systems and workflows need.
Of course, these approaches still remain fundamentally limited in their ability to generalize to complex, nuanced real-world scenarios - a constraint that becomes apparent when attempting to manually encode every possible business (or recruitment) situation into explicit rules. We also have to consider the technical complexity and costs of managing, storing, and updating these rules.
1/ Hard Constraint Filtering of Candidates: We can implement highly-deterministic business rules as initial filters: automatic candidate disqualification for missing certifications, visa status mismatches, or salary expectations exceeding budget. These binary decisions - perfectly suited for symbolic AI - ensure only qualified candidates enter the review pipeline.
2/ Priority Ranking of Candidates Through Logical Rules: Create transparent scoring systems using explicit rule chains:
Senior roles require 5+ years experience: +10 points
Exact skill match found: +15 points
Internal referral: +5 points
Urgent position flag: +8 points
3/ Dynamic Rule Engine: Build a JSON-based rules engine allowing non-technical HR users to modify business logic:
{
"rule_id": "senior_dev_requirements",
"condition": "role_level === 'senior' && years_experience >= 5",
"action": "boost_priority",
"explanation": "Meets senior role experience threshold"
}
4. Audit Trail Generation: Every hiring decision for any given candidate generates clear explanations: "Candidate prioritized due to: 5+ years experience (✓) + Java expertise match (✓) + internal referral (+5 boost)." This transparency is exactly what businesses need for defensible hiring decisions.
Strengths and Limitations of Symbolic, Rule-Based AI: The TalentSol project has potential to leverage symbolic, AI's core strengths: deterministic behavior, complete interpretability, and alignment with established business processes. The rule-based framework excels at codifying explicit business knowledge and handling scenarios where domain expertise can be clearly articulated.
Generalization Limitation of Symbolic, Rule-Based AI: However, purely rule-based systems struggle with real-world complexity and nuanced pattern recognition as I learnt through the module's exercises - like designing a robot waiter for every possible restaurant scenario. In recruitment, this limitation becomes apparent when evaluating subtle indicators of candidate quality that cannot easily be captured in explicit rules: How do you encode "demonstrates leadership potential" or "shows cultural fit" as deterministic IF/THEN statements? Imagine the operational and compliance challenges for recruiters, businesses and auditors. These present the need for other AI approaches to identify such complex patterns and nuanced relationships that can only be discovered through statistical learning from data rather than being explicitly programmed as deterministic rules.
Inherent Risk of Encoding Human Biases and Assumptions: When domain experts or AI system designers create explicit rules (man-made constructs), they inevitably embed their own assumptions, biases/priors, and experiential limitations into the logical framework.
In recruitment, this becomes problematic - rules like "prioritize candidates from top-tier universities" or "require 10+ years experience for senior roles" may seem logical but can perpetuate systemic biases against diverse candidates or non-traditional career paths. Unlike machine learning systems that can potentially discover unexpected patterns in data, rule-based systems are constrained by the worldview and blind spots of their human creators.
The transparent nature of symbolic AI can institutionalize subjective human judgments as 'objective' logical decisions, directly compromising the AI system's output quality through systematically biased results while creating fairness and generalization challenges.
Looking Forward to Data-Driven Pattern Recognition: This recognition of both generalization and bias limitations in purely rule-based approaches points toward the value of supervised machine learning techniques, which can complement symbolic, rule-based AI by discovering patterns that human experts might miss - or revealing biases that manual rule creation might perpetuate. The combination of rule-based transparency with data-driven pattern recognition represents a powerful approach for addressing both limitations - a topic I will explore in my next article covering my learning about supervised machine learning as part of "Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI)" module.
This hobbyist project demonstrates how GOFAI principles—despite being the first wave of AI—provide essential foundations for trustworthy AI systems, while also revealing where additional techniques become necessary for handling complex, nuanced decision-making scenarios in real-world applications.
Conclusion
The first part of "Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI)" module in the eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program provided a solid foundation in understanding rule-based symbolic AI systems and their fundamental place in the broader AI domain.
While GOFAI's symbolic, rule-based AI has limitations in generalizing to complex real-world scenarios, its clear structure and interpretability make it valuable for applications requiring transparent decision-making processes. The practical exercises, theoretical frameworks, and expert insights combined to illustrate how traditional AI methods can drive business success when applied appropriately, while also highlighting when more advanced techniques become necessary.
Most importantly, I learned that the goal is not to use the 'fanciest' AI technique available, but to choose the right tool for the specific business problem at hand.
This reinforces the central theme from my previous articles - Successful AI deployment is not about technical sophistication or the latest technologies, but about solving real business problems with quantifiable outcomes. Even the most elegant GOFAI implementation must ultimately create measurable business value through the frameworks we've discussed.
However, my learning revealed critical limitations. While rule-based AI systems excel at transparency and deterministic control, they struggle with generalization to complex scenarios and risk encoding human biases into "objective" logical decisions. The inherent limitation of symbolic, rule-based AI is its inability to learn patterns from data, adapt to new situations, or handle nuanced decision-making—directly catalyzed the development of machine learning approaches.
As the first wave of AI, symbolic, rule-based AI established the foundational concepts of AI 'reasoning' and decision-making. Its constraints revealed the need for systems that could discover finely-nuanced patterns rather than follow pre-programmed rules.
This is why machine learning emerged as the second wave: to complement rule-based transparency with data-driven pattern recognition (with help from statistical and probabilistic approaches) and adaptive learning capabilities. I am excited to reflect on my fundamental learning from the program and explore how rule-based approaches can be combined with modern machine learning techniques to create more adaptive and powerful solutions for real business challenges.
The key insight I continue taking forward is that successful AI implementation starts with clear business objectives, good data, and simple approaches - then iterates toward complexity and higher investment costs only when justified by real business needs.
For those interested in developing a deeper understanding of how foundational AI principles can drive business success, I highly recommend Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program and join the rest of our cohorts today: https://ecornell.cornell.edu/certificates/technology/designing-and-building-ai-solutions/
What role do you see for transparent, rule-based AI systems in your organization's AI strategy? I would love to hear your thoughts in the comments below.
If you missed the other parts of this learning series about eCornell's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program, you can find all articles here in this newsletter.
