## Article #5

**Title:** Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (Part 2) - What I Learned from Cornell's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/creating-business-value-ai-insights-from-ecornells-solutions-lim-rfjgc/

**Content:**

As I continue my journey through Cornell University's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program on the eCornell platform (Instructed by Lutz Finger), I am excited to reflect on new additional insights from the first module focused on "Creating Business Value With AI."
This article builds on my previous article exploring the fundamental ratio for evaluating AI investments.
The Heart of AI Implementation: The "So What?" Question
What struck me most about this module was the emphasis on asking ourselves the fundamental question: "So what?" Every AI implementation must answer this simple yet profound challenge. If we cannot articulate why our AI solution matters and what specific action it enables, we likely should not proceed with the business decision for development. This perspective shifted my thinking from technology-driven solutions to value-driven implementations - a crucial distinction in today's AI-saturated and 'noisy' landscape.
Value Creation in the AI Era
The first module "Creating Business Value With AI" thoughtfully explored how AI initiatives must generate tangible business value that exceeds their implementation costs. With the emergence of foundation models, we are witnessing a democratization of AI capabilities, making sophisticated solutions accessible to organizations without the vast resources of tech giants.
AI Product Development Framework for AI Product Managers: Lutz Finger also taught a highly-comprehensive and flywheel-like Al product development framework for developing AI products with actionable value - starting with defining the business value and concluding with implementation and feedback loops.
This systematic AI product development approach ensures that AI solutions are built with clear purpose and measurable outcomes.
Industry Perspectives That Broadened My Horizon
Lutz Finger had also invited industry leaders to feature conversations that provided valuable real-world context to understanding the business value of AI:
Dr. Guido Appenzeller, who has held leadership positions at Intel and VMware and is currently at Andreessen Horowitz  (a16z), shared fascinating perspectives on how foundation models are creating a new building block for system design. His prediction that this shift could lead to trillion-dollar companies in the coming years was particularly thought-provoking.
John Buckman, CEO and founder of Decent Espresso Machines, demonstrated how data science and AI technologies are transforming even traditional industries like coffee-making. His innovative approach showed how AI can create unexpected value in specialized domains.
Measure to Improve: The Metrics Behind Successful AI Implementations & Investments
"If you can't measure it, you can't improve it."
While often attributed to Peter Drucker, this saying perfectly captures the approach to AI implementation taught by Lutz Finger in the program.
Beyond the AI ROI fundamental ratio I discussed in my previous article, organizations also need to establish quantified metrics for data quality, model performance, output quality, and bias & fairness evaluation.
The semantics of the English language (or any language) makes generative AI (GenAI) output quality hard to assess subjectively.
Taking large language models for example, the subjective nature of language comprehension makes establishing concrete metrics for GenAI evaluation both crucial and complex.
The English language is perceived and comprehended differently based on one's experiences, biases/priors, age, generations, cultures, socioeconomic norms, and geographies - even amongst native speakers.
This 'Tower of Babel' phenomenon that often afflicts business communications, intercultural communications and stakeholder alignment will be apparent when humans assess GenAI output quality in a subjective manner.
In the case of image and video generation models, the difference in semantic comprehension also apply to visual images or videos (excluding obvious quality features such as noise, grain, pixel-level variations, flickering, jerkiness, floating, color, distortion, and motion) as different viewers may interpret the same visual content differently based on their cultural context, personal experiences, and cognitive biases.
Even during my undergraduate days at the National University of Singapore, there were abundant, pre-existing and peer-reviewed research literature which showed that languages are used differently across social groups, can evolve over time and vary across regions (Note: I will consider adding citations to give examples of these research literature at a later time).
To address this challenge, organizations should establish grounded and continuous evaluation & measurement frameworks using GenAI metrics including (but not limited to):
Accuracy (factual correctness)
Recall (comprehensiveness)
Area Under the Curve or AUC (statistical measure of model discrimination ability across different thresholds; Ideally optimized for above 50% threshold)
Relevance (alignment with user intent)
Faithfulness (adherence to source material)
Confusion matrix analysis (error type categorization)
Toxicity (model's propensity to generate harmful, offensive, or malicious content)
For the more advanced adopters: I would even recommend a Composite Quality Score (CQS). This Composite Quality Score (CQS) uses an organization-specific weighted combination of the aforementioned metrics using logistic regression to create a binary "acceptable/unacceptable" classifier based on strategically-defined threshold values)
The Composite Quality Score (CQS) metric approach can be strategically relevant and valuable to organizations because:
It allows organizations to encode and formalize their consensus and acceptance criteria across different metrics
It is easier to measure, track and continuously refined as more evaluation data becomes available - reducing execution friction and development tax
It transforms multiple complex metrics into a clear decision point
It enables systematic tracking of quality over time and across model versions & AI projects
You create an information asymmetry about your organization's strategic metrics and efficiency gains about your AI implementation - changing the 'rules' of the game for your competitor players in game theory.
Most importantly, these measurement metrics must anchor in organizationally-agreed AI labels of quality output and standards that are aligned with strategic relevance and user/customer needs. Here, the AI label of a GenAI system can be one or multiple data samples that are agreed to be of good quality. These "gold standard" labels also double as benchmarks - representing outputs that the organization has collectively determined meet or exceed quality thresholds across multiple dimensions of evaluation.
AI's Competitive Advantage Through Specialization
“The question is, ‘What would Carl Sagan do?’ He would take it back to first principles.” — Randall (Leonardo DiCaprio), Don't Look Up (2021)
In my own perspective using First Principles thinking, one of the most compelling drivers of AI deployment is how it creates efficiency through specialization (or learning rate) in your organization.
In business & economic terms, efficiency is a function of specialization or learning - provided your organization or enterprise is measuring specialization or 'blended learning rate' of your whole organization.
In this AI supercycle, we can recognize that specialization can source from human users and/or the weights of your AI model/neural network. For now, we will ignore the sub-symbolic and probabilistic nature of activating a neural network (or neural net) with a large language model (LLM) architecture, and steering latent embeddings (We will discuss this in later articles).
Anecdotally, I have observed from my past careers how a higher 'learning rate' drives higher success for individual project or a team - compared to other teams in the same company. While they are associated concepts, this 'learning rate' in the context of an individual, team or entire organization is not the same as the learning rate in machine learning terms.
In an example of my career at Amazon Web Services (AWS), we undergo a routine of learning, testing and certifications for a specific technology every month - encompassing business use case(s), documented business impact, historical customer metrics, core technology concepts, architectural designs and implementation. I would not be surprised if AWS is measuring and optimizing a company-wide 'net blended learning rate' from these enablement initiatives across its global organizations.
Efficiency is a Competitive Advantage Driven by Specialization from AI: Specialization has long been recognized as one of the formal competitive advantages in business. Specialization is "a method of production whereby an entity focuses on the production of a limited scope of goods to gain a greater degree of efficiency". This concept of specialization has strong foundations in economic principles related to comparative advantage and division of labor.
AI accelerates specialization or learning by enabling human workers to focus on higher-value tasks while automating routine work. This creates and captures higher overall value for the organization. Value can be quantified as % net new revenue growth, % expansion revenue growth, net revenue retention rate, Return on Marketing Investment (ROMI) ratio, Customer Acquisition Cost (CAC) per logo, SaaS Magic Number or even a customer experience score ratio (e.g., Net Promoter Score) from AI-attributed activities.
Your efficiency in a select dimension is only good or bad relative to competition: As taught by Dr. Gautam Ahuja (who is one of the world's most cited scholars in advanced business strategy) at Cornell Johnson Graduate School of Management, specialization can be represented on an efficiency curve of at least two (or more) dimensions. This efficiency curve framework provides clarity on your firm's positioning relative to competitors, revealing specific areas where you can establish competitive advantage.
The goal is not to be "middle of the pack" on this efficiency curve. You want to clearly differentiate through an advantage by optimizing tradeoffs and maximizing for a specific dimension (e.g., Walmart leads in cost efficiency) - while residing on the efficiency curve. Any point/position on the efficiency curve is known as the 'efficiency frontier'. When your AI investments operate on the 'efficiency frontier,' you are fully maximizing value creation at the lowest possible cost. This is your 'objective function' or 'loss minimization function' from your AI investments.
The efficiency curve example from Investopedia (Shown below) illustrates production possibility tradeoffs between Wine and Cotton based on business decisions. In the GenAI context, your efficiency frontier might involve balancing tradeoffs between quality metrics like relevance and faithfulness.
Considering how hallucination and creativity/innovation are 'two sides of the same coin' in GenAI, your strategic position on this curve reflects your deliberate choice between factual accuracy and innovative thinking based on your specific business needs.
Credit: Image by Sabrina Jiang © Investopedia 2020
With proper measurement of business value and strategic tradeoff decisions, you gain clear visibility into your competitive positioning through quantifiable efficiency gains from AI investments. Voilà!
Beyond operational efficiency gains, businesses operating as AI software providers can unlock entirely new revenue streams, creating a dual pathway to value creation that compounds over time.
A Real-World Example: AI-Enhanced Content Personalization Engine for B2B Demand Generation
Let me illustrate what measurement looks like for a practical example by assuming an internal use case of an advanced content personalization engine for B2B demand generation.
For simplicity, I refrained from using an example of an agentic AI content creation system which will include more complex designs and metrics.
Agentic AI and its AI agent components can have complex designs: An agentic AI system would incorporate multiple coordinated AI agents working within a a more complex architecture that includes workflow orchestration, goal-setting capabilities, autonomous decision-making processes, closed-loop learning mechanisms, and 'robust' adaptation protocols. These agentic AI systems can execute multiple tasks by navigating and adapting to various environments - from handling interface changes in connected applications to responding appropriately when third-party APIs are updated or modified (unless there are radical changes in font type, size and pixels on the web browser or SaaS tooling user interface that challenge the generalization of the agentic AI system).
For readers interested in the distinction between AI agents and agentic AI systems, I recommend the recent research by Sapkota, Roumeliotis, and Karkee (2023), "AI Agents vs. Agentic AI: A Conceptual Taxonomy, Applications and Challenges." (Link to paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10468). Their work provides a comprehensive taxonomy that clarifies the divergent design philosophies and capabilities between these related but very distinct approaches to AI systems.
1/ Traditional B2B Demand Generation Approach: In conventional strategies, marketing teams create limited sets of content assets targeting broad customer segments - a process that is typically time-consuming, expensive, and often results in generic messaging. These materials undergo lengthy approval processes and frequently require expensive third-party agencies. This came from my own experiences having led B2B product marketing and GTM during my previous career.
2/ The Modern B2B Demand Generation Approach would mean implementing an NLP-driven content personalization engine with a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system. This modern marketing team could:
Connect to corporate branding guidelines, tone of voice documentation, approved messaging frameworks and even specific sales & marketing campaign briefs stored in data repositories
Generate hyper-personalized emails, landing pages, and other assets that speak directly to each prospect's unique situation by analyzing prospect data, website behavior, and industry-specific challenges
Scale content production dramatically without increasing headcount or agency spend - Effectively reducing the marginal cost of content creation
The implementation would involve:
Setting up a knowledge base with brand guidelines, product information, and success stories
Integrating with CRM, marketing automation platforms or a customer data platform (CDP)
Implementing feedback loops to track engagement metrics and continually refine the model
This approach would transform the software business's B2B demand generation from a one-size-fits-all model to a dynamic, personalized experience that speaks directly to each prospect's needs while maintaining brand consistency for any campaign run - accelerating the sales & marketing cycle and improving ROI efficiency on marketing investments.
Beyond quantifying the business value of this AI-powered B2B demand generation engine, we will also need a comprehensive measurement framework that addresses operational performance, technical robustness, and governance requirements. This includes metrics that facilitate troubleshooting, provide audit-ready documentation, and deliver transparent design explainability to both leadership and end users."
To build a truly comprehensive AI measurement framework, organizations must identify both leading indicators that predict future success and lagging indicators that validate past performance. This dual approach allows teams to both anticipate outcomes and verify results.
For leading indicators, consider metrics that provide early signals of AI system effectiveness. These predictive measurements help teams course-correct or troubleshoot before business outcomes are affected or escalations for SEV-1 incidents.
For lagging indicators, focus on metrics that demonstrate concrete business value, even if they emerge more slowly.
Beyond business metrics, technical performance indicators such as read-write rates, inference runtime, and output generation speed provide crucial visibility into system reliability and scalability. These operational measurements ensure your AI solutions can meet increasing demands while maintaining consistent performance.
Assuming our real world example of an AI Content Creation Engine for B2B Demand Generation, examples of leading indicators are system and engineering performance metrics such as:
System Performance Metrics: Retrieval precision and recall rates, Query processing latency (milliseconds), Context window utilization percentage, Vector database query throughput, Chunk relevance scores, Token processing rate, Cache hit ratio, Embedding quality metrics
Content Quality Metrics: Content coherence scores, Content relevance to user query, Hallucination detection rates, Source citation accuracy, Guidelines adherence score, Brand voice consistency rating
Examples of lagging indicators are business and product metrics such as:
User Engagement Metrics: Daily Active Users (DAU), Weekly Active Users (WAU), Monthly Active Users (MAU), Queries per user, Click-through rates on personalized content, Time spent with generated content, Content completion rates, Response action rates, Follow-up query frequency, User feedback scores
Business Impact Metrics: Conversion rate improvements, Content production velocity, Marketing team efficiency gains, Cost per personalized asset, Revenue attributed to personalized content, Customer satisfaction scores
Examples of operational metrics are engineering performance metrics such as:
Read-write rates for vector database performance
Inference runtime impacts on user experience
Speed of output generation for scalability
Retrieval latency (time to fetch relevant context)
End-to-end response time
Memory utilization during peak loads
Resource consumption per request
System stability under varying traffic
Warm/cold start performance differences
Token processing efficiency
Not all use cases are made equal or comparable for measurement: These measurement considerations can differ significantly between internal use cases like content creation and external, mission-critical use cases like fraud detection across high-volume financial transactions.
In the latter, error rates and processing speed become much more critical to business value than in marketing content generation, where creative quality and engagement metrics may take precedence.
Recalling my previous article where I introduce my own take on the AI ROI Fundamental Ratio:
ROI Fundamental Ratio = (Net New Revenue Streams + Quantified Value of Efficiency Gains) / (Development Costs + Operational Costs + Risk Costs)
The Risk Costs between the marketing content creation use case and financial fraud detection use case also vary significantly.
Conclusion
As I continue through Cornell University's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, I am struck by how this first module "Creating Business Value with AI" has transformed my approach to AI implementation, product development and investments.
The emphasis on defining actionable business value before rushing to technological solutions provides a crucial framework that will serve me well in future AI projects.
The insights gained from industry leaders like Dr. Guido Appenzeller and John Buckman have broadened my perspective on how foundation models are creating new building blocks for system design across industries. Their real-world applications demonstrate that AI's true power comes not from technology alone, but from its strategic application to specific business challenges.
In my own perspective using First Principles, one of the most compelling drivers of AI deployment is how it drives efficiency through specialization (or learning rate) in organizations. AI helps record learning in its weights and accelerates the learning rate of individuals, teams & organizations.
Perhaps most valuable has been learning to rigorously measure the business value of AI implementations. As the saying goes, "If you can't measure it, you can't improve it."
Through comprehensive frameworks like my AI ROI Fundamental Ratio and detailed metrics for system performance, content quality, user engagement, and business impact, we can move beyond technological fascination to ensure our AI investments realize investment returns.
The B2B content personalization example we explored demonstrates how AI can transform traditional approaches by enabling hyper-personalization at scale, but only when implemented with clearly-defined business objectives and concrete while robust measurement systems.
And as we examined, evolving this system into a truly agentic AI would require additional capabilities like autonomous decision-making frameworks, industry intelligence, execution autonomy, and sophisticated control mechanisms.
As I look forward to the next modules in this program, I am excited to build upon this foundation of business-first AI implementations and product development. The lessons learned about measuring business value, establishing meaningful metrics, and aligning AI capabilities with strategic objectives will prove invaluable to everyone who joins the eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program with the rest of the previous cohorts: https://ecornell.cornell.edu/certificates/technology/designing-and-building-ai-solutions/
What metrics do you use to continuously evaluate AI projects and investments in your organization? I would love to hear about your experiences in the comments below.

## Article #6

**Title:** Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (Part 3): Building Supervised Classifiers with Regression

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/exploring-good-old-fashioned-ai-part-3-building-supervised-lim-ymyfc/

**Content:**

Asking the Right Questions: The Foundation of AI Success
The most profound lesson from Lutz Finger's instruction in Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program was that successful AI implementation is not about technical proficiency but rather about identifying real business value. Through engaging examples and hands-on exercises, Dr. Finger demonstrated that the specific technology used matters far less than the measurable outcomes produced.
The program emphasized beginning with clear business objectives before considering technological solutions. This value-first approach ensures that AI implementations create meaningful impact rather than just following technological trends.
The Value Proposition First Approach and the "So What" Litmus Test
The program's first module "Creating Business Value with AI" emphasized beginning with a well-defined value proposition before obsessing over technical implementation details right at the beginning.
Finger repeatedly stressed the importance of the "So what?" question as a litmus test before execution - ensuring that any AI implementation actually creates measurable business value of strategic relevance.
The most valuable framework I gained was the disciplined application of the "so what?" question to any AI initiative.
If an AI solution cannot answer this fundamental question about its business value, you either need to dig deeper or you should not have started in the first place.
This simple yet powerful lens helps distinguish between technologies that merely fascinate and those that truly create measurable value.
“The answer to the ultimate question of life, the universe, and everything is 42.”– Douglas Adams, The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy
In the book "The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy", they overengineered and built a supercomputer called Deep Thought to calculate the Answer to Everything — but forget to define the Question (which remains unknown by everyone in the plot). Sound familiar?
Hands-On Learning Through Real Applications
The most engaging aspect of the module was the guest session with John Buckman, founder of Decent Espresso Machines, who demonstrated how measurement and data analysis are transforming the coffee making industry.
This real-world coffee-making example brilliantly illustrated how scientific measurement, data collection, and pattern recognition can create value through measurement, actionable prediction and optimization - fundamental principles that apply across industries.
This first module "Creating Business Value with AI" in the eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program also included practical exercises where we evaluated AI product concepts across diverse domains like marketing strategies, credit risk prediction, deforestation prevention, suicide risk assessment, hospital flow optimization and even coffee preparation.
Each exercise reinforced the importance of defining actionable value before implementing any technology solution. The practical, application-focused nature of these exercises ensured the concepts were not only theoretical but also immediately applicable to real business challenges of today.
The New Economics of AI Implementation
New AI tools have fundamentally transformed the execution and marginal costs of a wide array of undifferentiated and low-value tasks - reinforcing the importance of intentionally and strategically defining business value and business objectives from AI implementations.
As I discussed in my previous article about AI's competitive advantage, low-code and no-code AI tools deliver efficiency gains to users by harnessing the specialized 'intelligence' embedded in large language model weights, unique retrieval-augmented techniques that incorporate existing data/context into input prompts and test-time compute in 'reasoning' models.
With low-code and no-code AI tools (e.g., code-generation, envisioning UI) of today, successful AI deployments have higher weights on assessing business value, making good business decisions, running efficient experiments before investing a significant amount of resources & capital in full-scale implementations.
A Real-World Example: Building an Applicant Tracking System with AI in 1+ Days
I experienced this paradigm shift firsthand as a non-professional developer through a recent AI & Machine Learning hobby project called TalentSol.
Yes, you read right. Using Augment Code Agent and VS Code as IDE environment, I completed the bulk of frontend development in just 1.5 days - a process that would traditionally take weeks.
If you would like to try it out with the rest of us, do check out @augment here >>> https://www.augmentcode.com/
TalentSol is a prototype applicant tracking system designed to "revolutionize how companies manage their recruitment pipeline through intelligent candidate matching and workflow optimization." Built with React 18, TypeScript, Vite, and Tailwind CSS, this project demonstrates how AI code-generation tools can dramatically accelerate development cycles.
The project served as a practical application of concepts from my eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, specifically around machine learning model development for recruitment use cases, as opposed to user interface development. v0.1 frontend features include candidate pipeline management, interview scheduling, messaging, and an analytics dashboard, with AI-powered candidate prioritization models planned for future releases.
What impressed me most about Augment Code Agent was its contextual awareness of overall software architecture - automatically updating dependencies and routing across the entire codebase when implementing new features. Augment Code Agent is also proven to be phenomenally reliable and accurate when troubleshooting errors - automatically resolving issue root causes during the extent of this project. My role is to review the Augment Code Agent's proposed changes and approve them - almost like a pseudo-engineering lead.
The chat interface functioned more than a code generator It also serves as a fractional CTO, offering creative insights and troubleshooting complex errors with remarkable reliability, especially when discussing architectural design decisions in my prompts.
This rapid development experience demonstrates how AI tools are reshaping software development timelines and capabilities. For a visual overview of what 1.5 days of AI-assisted development can produce, including UI screenshots and technical implementation details, visit the TalentSol GitHub repository [Full link: https://github.com/youshen-lim/TalentSol---Applicant-Tracking-System-Application].
Alternatively, I had also provided screenshots of the prototype UI from this hobby project:
Landing Page (Inspired by existing ATS players in market)
Login Page
Dashboard Page
TalentSol - Modern Applicant Tracking System - 1 June 2025 - Watch Video

Job Management Page

Analytics Core Reports Page
Analytics Core Reports Pipeline Metrics Page
Analytics Core Reports Time-to-Hire Visualization_Page
Analytics Core Reports Time-to-Hire Table Page
Analytics Custom Reports Page
Analytics ML Reports Page
Candidate Messaging Page
Candidate Document Management Page
Creating Business Value With AI: Further Insights from the Cornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program
Throughout Cornell's eCornell program and Lutz Finger's instruction, I have learnt that effective AI implementation requires balancing technical capabilities with clear business/organizational/societal objectives. The program's curriculum content and hands-on exercises - from evaluating deforestation prevention systems to optimizing hospital flow - provided practical frameworks for distinguishing genuine innovation from fleeting hype. Most importantly, I learned that the "So what?" question must drive every AI initiative.
As foundation models continue to democratize access to powerful AI tools, the competitive advantage shifts from technical implementation to strategic vision and business acumen. The future belongs not to those who merely deploy AI technology, but to those who leverage it to address meaningful problems with measurable outcomes. Whether you are a business leader or technology professional, success in the AI era depends on your ability to identify where automation creates genuine value and to build solutions that deliver it consistently.
Do join us all in your AI product development journey today on eCornell: https://ecornell.cornell.edu/certificates/technology/designing-and-building-ai-solutions/

## Article #7

**Title:** Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI (Part 4): Building Supervised Classifiers with Decision Trees

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/exploring-good-old-fashioned-ai-part-4-building-supervised-lim-3rmic/

**Content:**

What I Learned from Cornell's "Designing and Building AI Solutions" Program
Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" certificate program (Instructed by Lutz Finger) showed me that decision trees offer a visually intuitive approach to classification that complements regression techniques in supervised machine learning. After implementing logistic regression in my previous article, I discovered through Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program and Lutz Finger's instruction that decision trees provide an alternative mathematical framework and AI approach for solving classification problems. This fourth article in my series continues to explore the eCornell program's second module, "Exploring Good Old-Fashioned AI" by focusing on decision trees. Specifically, I shall reflect on my learning about decision trees as an alternative AI approach for solving classification problems and my practical implementation experiences with 16 decision tree models. Stay tuned!
The eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program built our foundations by teaching the mathematical principles that operate decision trees, including entropy calculations and information gain. This ensures fundamental knowledge to apply decision trees as optimal solutions for business problems and objectives.
The visual nature of decision trees makes complex decisions transparent and interpretable through simple branching structures. Unlike probability curves, decision trees split data samples into increasingly pure subsets using yes/no questions. Each split creates branches leading to specific classifications, similar to Lutz Finger's analogy of the game of "20 Questions." During the eCornell program's practical exercises, decision trees create hierarchical structures that can mirror human decision-making processes. This visual representation appeals to non-technical or business stakeholders who need to understand AI decisions, a key insight emphasized throughout the program.
See content credentials
Credit: Image generated by ChatGPT in the style of New Yorker Cartoons, inspired by the sci-fi film Avatar.
The Mathematical Foundation of Decision Trees
Decision trees optimize splits by minimizing entropy, which measures disorder in data groups. Through the program, Lutz Finger introduced entropy as a way to quantify chaos within datasets. Pure groups have zero entropy, while perfectly mixed groups have maximum entropy. The goal is finding splits that create the greatest entropy reduction. The program demonstrated entropy calculations through visual examples, showing how splitting shapes by different attributes produces varying information gains. The model (or algorithm) evaluates every possible split to maximize information gain. For each potential split, it calculates weighted average entropy of resulting groups. The split producing lowest combined entropy becomes a tree node. This process continues recursively until reaching pure groups or stopping criteria, as I learned through hands-on exercises in the eCornell platform. Lutz Finger illustrated this concept using Shannon's entropy formula, showing how decision trees systematically reduce uncertainty from maximum entropy toward zero for pure classifications. This reminded me of the concept of entropy in physics laws of thermodynamics.
Comparing Decision Trees to Logistic Regression
Each approach has distinct advantages depending on business context and data characteristics. Lutz Finger taught that neither approach is inherently superior; each serves different needs. Decision trees excel with non-linear relationships and require less preprocessing, while logistic regression performs better with linear relationships and smaller datasets. Key differences emerge in data assumptions and interpretability. Through Cornell's program exercises, I discovered that decision trees make no assumptions about variable relationships, making them robust to multicollinearity and mixed data types. They naturally handle categorical variables and capture complex feature interactions, though they're more prone to overfitting.
Implementing the AI Product Development Checklist
Systematic application of the development checklist ensures consistent results regardless of model choice. Lutz Finger's structured approach in the eCornell program emphasized that the fundamental development process remains constant across different datasets and business problems. This checklist guides practitioners through data preparation, model development, and evaluation stages. The three-stage process covers data cleaning, model development, and performance evaluation. Stage one addresses legal concerns and data quality. Stage two focuses on splitting data and training models. Stage three evaluates performance using confusion matrices and business-aligned metrics. Through Cornell's program, I applied this checklist to multiple datasets, reinforcing its universal applicability.
Practical Exercises and Applications with Decision Trees
Traditional machine learning algorithms can effectively handle complex image recognition tasks. Working with handwritten digit datasets in Cornell's program exercises, I discovered both logistic regression and decision trees achieved remarkable accuracy in optical character recognition. This challenged preconceptions about deep neural networks being essential for image analysis, as Lutz Finger demonstrated through practical implementations. The program included practical hands-on exercises using MNIST datasets for postal service automation, showing how decision trees could automate mail sorting with significant cost savings. As with most AI approaches, memory constraints and computational limitations impact implementation on large datasets. The program's exercises revealed how large datasets can overwhelm certain development environments, requiring transitions to more robust platforms. This experience, guided by Lutz Finger's practical tips, highlighted how development environment choice significantly impacts model building efficiency. Large datasets required transitioning between different computational platforms for effective processing. Data preprocessing proves crucial, particularly with class imbalance. Through eCornell's hands-on exercises, I learned that decision trees show extreme sensitivity to unbalanced data, necessitating techniques to create synthetic examples of minority classes. Proper preprocessing can dramatically improve model performance metrics. Converting multi-class problems to binary classification revealed how synthetic resampling techniques (like SMOTE) could improve decision tree performance significantly.
Real-World Business Applications: The Storytel Case Study
As part of the eCornell program, Lutz Finger had invited Jonas Tellander as guest lecturer. As CEO and founder of Storytel, Tellander provided invaluable insights into how decision trees grew Storytel to millions of paying users generating hundreds of millions in annual revenue. This transformation demonstrates the practical success of decision tree-based recommender systems in the streaming and publishing industries.
Storytel's implementation of decision trees combines user reading history, preferred genres, and demographic data with book attributes to predict match probability. This approach scales efficiently by focusing on likely matches rather than entire catalogs.
Three key AI applications drove Storytel's success using traditional machine learning techniques. First, search and recommendation systems helped users navigate an expanding catalog, essentially creating perceived catalog size through better discovery. These systems replaced librarian-style recommendations with data-driven thresholds for audiobook suggestions. Second, optimization algorithms balanced user happiness, content costs, and publisher satisfaction to maximize profitability. Third, A/B testing determined optimal pricing bundles and subscription tiers based on usage patterns.
Temporal dimensions require continuous model updates to maintain relevance. User preferences (a form of data drift) evolve over time, requiring timestamp inclusion in feature vectors to capture preference shifts and ensure recommendations reflect current interests. Tellander explained how users might start with mainstream titles before drifting toward niche content, necessitating ongoing algorithmic adjustments.
The transition to generative AI demonstrates how foundational techniques enable advanced applications. Tellander revealed how Storytel recently launched Voice Switcher technology, reducing audiobook production costs dramatically. This required training models on thousands of hours of audio per language, showing how traditional machine learning principles of data quality and domain-specific training remain essential even with cutting-edge technology.
Storytel serves as an excellent business case study for implementing decision trees to serve customer needs while driving business growth.
My Implementation Journey: Building Decision Trees for TalentSol's AI-Powered Initial Candidate Screening
Decision trees may prove superior to logistic regression for my initial candidate screening system for my TalentSol applicant tracking system (ATS) project. In my previous article, I applied logistic regression to build supervised classifiers to screen candidates via text-matching between resume text and job descriptions (both public and private). The task is to identify applicants most likely to be hired, enabling organizations to prioritize promising candidates while reducing vacancy costs at scale. The non-linear relationships between candidate attributes and hiring outcomes made decision trees particularly suitable for this task. In both cases of implementatio logistic regression and decision trees, I continued to apply the AI product development checklist from business objectives through deployment strategy - as taught by Lutz Finger in the eCornell program.
To demonstrate these principles in action, I developed TalentSol, an AI-powered applicant tracking system that implements supervised classification for initial candidate screening. The complete implementation, including data preprocessing, model training, and evaluation code, is available in my GitHub repository: TalentSol_Supervised-Classifier-for-Initial-Candidate-Screening-Decision-Trees. This project serves as a practical case study for applying Cornell University and eCornell's systematic AI development methodology to real-world recruitment challenges.
Data Preparation Revealed Critical Challenges
Recalling my previous article, I utilized a Kaggle recruitment dataset comprising 10,000 synthetic records as the foundation for developing a practical candidate screening framework. This comprehensive dataset, released under CC0 Public Domain licensing, was engineered using Python Faker library combined with ChatGPT-generated content specifically for educational and research applications. The synthetic nature of this data addressed ethical considerations around candidate privacy while maintaining the complexity necessary to replicate authentic recruitment scenarios.
The dataset encompassed essential recruitment variables including candidate demographics, educational backgrounds, performance indicators, and comprehensive textual data from both job postings and candidate resumes.
Personal identifier removal protected privacy while preserving diversity metrics. Working with the Kaggle hiring dataset containing 10,000 records, I implemented a systematic approach to data privacy during preprocessing. I removed columns containing personally identifiable information (PII) including 'Job Applicant Name', 'Job Applicant ID', 'Age', and 'Gender' early in the preprocessing pipeline . This decision aligned with responsible AI considerations and mitigated risks of re-identification while ensuring data privacy.
Strategic retention of ethnicity data supported diversity hiring goals. While removing most demographic identifiers, I deliberately retained the 'Ethnicity' column to support organizational diversity metrics and goals . This decision required careful consideration—the model could potentially create associations between ethnicity and "Best Match" classification. However, many organizations have legitimate business justifications for tracking diversity metrics, such as ensuring equitable representation and meeting diversity targets. The key was documenting this decision transparently and implementing appropriate safeguards.
Class imbalance and multicollinearity demanded careful preprocessing. The dataset showed severe skew with only 48.5% of candidates receiving positive hiring decisions. This imbalance threatened model performance since decision trees exhibit extreme sensitivity to class distribution. I implemented oversampling using scikit-learn's resample function, creating a balanced training set of 10,300 records with equal representation of both classes.
Text preprocessing requirements differed dramatically between approaches. Traditional methods like TF-IDF and CountVectorizer required extensive manual cleaning—removing punctuation, converting to lowercase, and eliminating stopwords.
In comparison, more complex models like neural networks (BERT/ModernBERT or Large Language Models) can perform better with raw text, as they leverage punctuation and capitalization patterns for semantic understanding.
While this recruitment dataset primarily contains categorical and textual data, comprehensive preprocessing of numerical data columns typically requires additional statistical tools including mean, median, variance, correlation matrices, standard outlier detection (box plots, IQR, Z-score), feature/scaling normalization, hypothesis testing for statistical significance (our favorite t-test), correlation matrices, Z-score, Principal Component Analysis (PCA), and VIF for multicollinearity detection.
Responsible AI Considerations in Practice
Privacy-preserving preprocessing balanced multiple objectives. My preprocessing approach demonstrated how to handle sensitive data responsibly:
Removed PII / personal identifier columns: Name, Age, Gender, Race1, Race2, and Applicant ID eliminated re-identification risks.
Preserved diversity metrics: Retained ethnicity data with clear documentation of business justification (Retention/removal depending on the context of your business/organization).
Addressed spurious patterns: Removed ordinal Applicant ID to prevent the model from learning artificial/arbitrary ordering patterns for my "Best Match" class outcome.
Transparency and documentation proved essential. I created clear documentation explaining:
Which features were removed and why
The business justification for retaining ethnicity data
How the model would be monitored for bias
The intended assistive (not replacement) role for recruiters
Practical implications for production systems. Organizations implementing similar systems should:
Conduct bias audits to ensure ethnicity data doesn't lead to discriminatory outcomes
Implement human oversight for all hiring decisions
Use ethnicity data for post-hoc diversity analysis rather than decision-making
Maintain clear audit trails of preprocessing decisions
Key Implementation Insights
Start with privacy by design. Remove PII early in the preprocessing pipeline before any model training or analysis. This prevents accidental exposure and ensures compliance with data protection regulations.Document diversity data decisions transparently. If retaining demographic data for legitimate business purposes, clearly document:
The specific diversity goals being supported
How the data will be used (analytics vs. decision-making)
Safeguards against discriminatory use
Regular bias monitoring procedures
Balance competing objectives thoughtfully. The tension between privacy protection and diversity goals requires careful navigation. My approach showed it is possible to protect individual privacy while supporting organizational diversity initiatives through thoughtful preprocessing and clear documentation. The systematic evaluation demonstrated that responsible data handling does not compromise model performance, which I will illustrate shortly.
Text Matching Naturally Follows Rule-Based Patterns
Decision trees capture recruitment logic through interpretable rules that mirror actual recruiter thinking patterns. My implementation revealed how these models translate complex hiring decisions into clear decision paths.
For example, IF a resume contains "Python" AND the job requires "Python," the model predicts a Strong Match with 85% confidence. Conversely, IF a resume shows "Senior Engineer" experience AND the job specifies "Entry Level," the system identifies a Poor Match with 92% confidence. When skills match 70% or more of requirements AND experience aligns appropriately, the model classifies an Excellent Match with 78% confidence.
Sparse text features favor tree-based approaches over linear models in high-dimensional spaces. After vectorization, the 10,000 documents created feature spaces with over 16,000 dimensions, where most values remained zero for any given resume. Decision trees efficiently handled this sparsity by focusing only on informative non-zero features, while logistic regression struggled by attempting to assign weights to thousands of irrelevant zero-valued features. This tolerance to irrelevant features represents a significant advantage, as features that do not contribute to reducing impurity simply do not appear in tree splits.
Evaluating Text Embedding Approaches for Decision Trees
To build robust decision tree classifiers, I systematically evaluated multiple text embedding approaches: TF-IDF, HashingVectorizer, CountVectorizer, BERT, and ModernBERT.
Understanding their 1) conceptual and 2) technical differences proved crucial for optimizing model performance:
CountVectorizer:
Concept: Represents text as a matrix where each row is a document and each column is a unique word in the corpus. The value in each cell is the frequency of that word in that document.
Technical: Creates a vocabulary of all unique words and then counts occurrences. Can optionally use N-grams (sequences of words).
TF-IDF Vectorizer (Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency):
Concept: Similar to CountVectorizer, but it weights word counts by how often they appear in the entire corpus. Words that are common across many documents (like "the" or "a") get lower weights, while words unique to a document get higher weights. This emphasizes words that are more relevant to a specific document.
Technical: Calculates TF (frequency in document) and IDF (inverse frequency across corpus) for each word and multiplies them. Also supports N-grams.
HashingVectorizer:
Concept: Converts text features into a fixed-size numerical vector using a hashing function. It avoids building a vocabulary in memory, making it very memory-efficient, especially for large datasets. However, it can suffer from hash collisions (different words mapping to the same feature index).
Technical: Applies a hash function to words/N-grams to map them directly to feature indices in a vector of a predefined size.
Google's BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers):
Concept: A powerful, pre-trained transformer model that generates dense vector representations (embeddings) for words and sentences. Unlike older methods, BERT considers the context of a word (looking at words before and after it) to understand its meaning. These embeddings capture semantic relationships.
Technical: Uses a multi-layer bidirectional Transformer encoder trained on a massive text corpus using tasks like Masked Language Modeling and Next Sentence Prediction. Outputs context-aware embeddings.
ModernBERT (An improved version of BERT; Launched 2024):
Concept: A significantly improved version of BERT that is faster, more accurate, and can handle much longer text (16x longer than original BERT) while being a direct replacement for existing BERT models.
Technical: Similar to BERT but with architectural variations that uses updated building blocks from newer AI models to better understand word positions and relationships, with smarter attention mechanisms that process text more efficiently.Figure 1: Key differences between text vectorization approaches. Each technique/method offers unique trade-offs between complexity, performance and compute costs, serving as optimal solutions for the right problems.
Figure 1a: Key differences between text vectorization approaches. Each technique/method offers unique trade-offs between complexity, performance and compute costs, serving as optimal solutions for the right problems.
Figure 1b: Additional key differences between text vectorization approaches.
For my TalentSol implementation, this analysis revealed critical trade-offs. While BERT/ModernBERT offer state-of-the-art contextual embeddings, their computational overhead and complexity may not justify the performance gains when paired with decision trees. The simpler vectorization methods (TF-IDF, CountVectorizer) proved more practical, offering strong performance with significantly lower computational costs. This is a key consideration for production ATS systems processing thousands of applications daily.
Conceptual Strengths of Decision Tree Models for Text Matching
Decision trees possess several fundamental advantages that make them particularly suitable for text matching between resumes and job descriptions. These models naturally capture complex, non-linear relationships and interactions between text features without assuming simple linear boundaries between classes. In recruitment scenarios, the presence and combination of specific words or phrases can have non-linear impacts on match quality. A decision tree can 'discover' text feature interactions, such as how the importance of a specific skill mentioned in a resume might depend on the candidate's job role or experience level.
Engineering complexity and cost considerations strongly favor decision trees over neural network approaches. Training large neural networks like BERT or modern language models demands substantial computational resources, including high-end GPUs or TPUs, with training times spanning hours or days. Decision tree models using efficient vectorizers like HashingVectorizer require much lower training costs and faster execution on standard hardware. Inference speed presents another crucial advantage, as decision trees provide faster, more cost-effective predictions compared to the computationally intensive requirements of large neural networks.
The non-parametric nature of decision trees eliminates assumptions about data distribution while maintaining interpretability. Unlike linear models, trees make no strong assumptions about underlying data distributions, which proves advantageous when working with complex, often non-Gaussian distributions of text and categorical features. While ensemble methods reduce individual tree interpretability, the fundamental rule-based structure still provides insights into which combinations of text features and categorical attributes indicate strong candidate-job matches. This transparency proves invaluable for understanding and validating recruitment decisions in production systems.
How Decision Trees Process Text and Categorical Data Samples
As mentioned earlier, decision tree models process data samples by recursively partitioning/splitting the feature space into smaller subspaces containing more homogeneous samples based on the features that best separate our labels (or categorical classes): 'Best Match' versus 'Not Best Match'.
Requirement for Numerical Features: Decision trees require numerical input to determine split points. We must convert text data into a numerical format through vectorization or embedding techniques, such as CountVectorizer, TF-IDF, HashingVectorizer, Word2Vec, or FastText, and categorical data through encoding, such as One-Hot Encoding. These numerical features represent the presence, frequency, or semantic meaning of words and phrases, as well as the categories of applicants and jobs.
Splitting Based on Impurity Reduction: At each node in the tree, the decision tree considers splitting the data based on the value of a single feature. The goal is to find the split that results in the greatest reduction in "impurity" or the greatest "information gain." Impurity measures how mixed the classes are within a subset of data. Common impurity measures include Gini Impurity, which measures how often a randomly chosen element from the set would be incorrectly labeled if it was randomly labeled according to the distribution of labels in the subset, and Shannon's Entropy, rooted in information theory, which measures the randomness or unpredictability of the class labels in the subset. A lower entropy indicates a more homogeneous subset. The decision tree model will evaluate potential splits across all features and chooses the one that maximizes the reduction in impurity (whether Gini or entropy), effectively making the resulting child nodes as "pure" as possible with respect to the class labels. This process repeats recursively until a stopping criterion is met, such as reaching the maximum depth, meeting the minimum samples per leaf, or when no further impurity reduction is possible.
AI Model Approaches and Definitions
Like Shakira's character in the 2016 movie Zootopia, I embraced the "Try Everything" philosophy when implementing decision tree models for recruitment matching. Rather than settling for a single approach, I evaluated 16 decision tree model variations to discover which techniques would deliver optimal performance for resume-job matching tasks for TalentSol's candidate screening feature.
I implemented and evaluated 16-decision tree model approaches:
CountVectorizer Embedding Decision Tree Model: This model employs a simple Decision Tree classifier trained on features generated by the CountVectorizer. CountVectorizer converts text into a matrix of token counts, representing each document as a bag of words with their frequencies.
TF-IDF Embedding Decision Tree Model: We used a Decision Tree classifier on features created by TF-IDF Vectorization in this model. TF-IDF assigns weights to words based on their frequency in a document and its inverse frequency across the entire dataset, aiming to give more importance to words relevant to a specific document but not common everywhere.
HashingVectorizer Embedding Decision Tree Model: This model utilizes a Decision Tree classifier with features from a HashingVectorizer. HashingVectorizer serves as a memory-efficient alternative to CountVectorizer and TF-IDF, using hashing to map text features to a fixed-size vector and avoiding the need to store a vocabulary in memory.
Word2Vec Embedding Decision Tree Model: We trained a Decision Tree classifier on document embeddings generated using Word2Vec for this model. Word2Vec is a neural network-based technique that learns dense vector representations (embeddings) for words, capturing semantic relationships. We typically create document embeddings by averaging or summing the word vectors within a document.
FastText Embedding Decision Tree Model: Similar to the Word2Vec model, this model uses a Decision Tree classifier with document embeddings from FastText. FastText extends Word2Vec by considering sub-word information (n-grams), which can benefit handling out-of-vocabulary words and morphological variations. We also typically create document embeddings by averaging or summing word vectors in this approach.
Stacking Classifier Ensemble: This ensemble uses a two-layer approach. The first layer consists of diverse base models whose predictions are used as input features for a second-layer meta-classifier, typically a simpler model like Logistic Regression, which makes the final prediction.
Voting Classifier Ensemble: This ensemble combines predictions from a selection of diverse base models using a voting strategy, either hard voting based on predicted classes or soft voting based on predicted probabilities, to make the final prediction.
Random Forest Decision Tree Ensemble: This ensemble method builds multiple Decision Trees during training and outputs the mode of the classes (classification) or mean prediction (regression) of the individual trees. We train each tree on a random subset of the data and features (bagging).
Extra Trees Ensemble: This model is an Extra Trees classifier using HashingVectorizer for text features. Similar to Random Forest, it adds an extra layer of randomness by randomly selecting thresholds for each feature at each split point, rather than searching for the best possible threshold. This can sometimes lead to faster training and further reduction of variance.
Gradient Boosting Classifier Ensemble: This is a sequential ensemble method where we build new Decision Tree models to correct the errors of previous models. It focuses on improving predictions iteratively by boosting the performance of weak learners, typically shallow Decision Trees.
XGBoost Classifier Ensemble: This model is an optimized and highly efficient implementation of Gradient Boosting. We know XGBoost for its speed and performance, incorporating regularization and parallel processing.
LightGBM Classifier Ensemble: This framework is another gradient boosting approach that uses tree-based learning algorithms. We designed LightGBM for distributed and parallel computing; it is often faster and uses less memory than XGBoost, particularly on large datasets.
AdaBoost Decision Tree Ensemble: This adaptive boosting method increases the weights of misclassified samples at each iteration, forcing subsequent weak learners, typically shallow Decision Tree "stumps" with max_depth=1 or max_depth=2, to focus on the harder-to-classify instances.
Voting Classifier Ensemble (Hybrid): This ensemble combines predictions from a selection of diverse base models. The base models include individual Decision Tree models with different text vectorizers and other ensemble methods. We use a voting strategy, either hard voting based on predicted classes or soft voting based on predicted probabilities, to make the final prediction.
Stacking Classifier Ensemble (Hybrid): This ensemble employs a two-layer approach. The first layer consists of diverse base models, including individual Decision Tree models with different text vectorizers and other ensemble methods, whose predictions we use as input features for a second-layer meta-classifier. We typically use a simpler model like Logistic Regression as the meta-classifier, which makes the final prediction.
Multi-view Learning (Conceptual Exploration): Multi-view learning explores combining information from different "views" or representations of the data. Examples include text features from resumes and job descriptions as one view and categorical features like job roles as another view. We can use various fusion strategies, including early, intermediate, or late fusion. While conceptually promising for this task, the high complexity and significant computational requirements for implementing and tuning a robust multi-view learning framework made it impractical for this phase of this learning project.
Strategic Selection of Gradient Boosting Variants
When implementing gradient boosting ensembles, I faced a critical decision: Which variant would best serve the ATS text matching use case?
My analysis revealed distinct advantages for each approach. Based on this analysis, I prioritized LightGBM and XGBoost implementations for their superior handling of sparse TF-IDF features for our text matching requirement. With 10,000+ records in our dataset, we sat at the inflection point where performance differences become meaningful, justifying the selection of these optimized implementations over the standard GradientBoostingClassifier.
Exclusion of Word2Vec and FastText from Hybrid Ensembles
For my Hybrid Voting Classifier Ensemble and Hybrid Stacking Classifier Ensemble models, I intentionally designed and excluded the Word2Vec and FastText embedded Decision Tree models from these hybrid ensembles due to KeyError exceptions.
Reason: These exceptions likely occur during the cross_val_predict or internal cross-validation steps within the ensemble methods when the embedding models encounter tokens, or words or n-grams, in the validation or test folds that were not present in the vocabulary learned during training on the other folds. This is a common issue with embedding models, especially those trained on a fixed corpus like the training data, when dealing with new, unseen text containing out-of-vocabulary (OOV) words.
Impact: When a base estimator within an ensemble fails due to such an error, it can halt the entire ensemble training or prediction process, making the ensemble unreliable or unusable.
Decision: To ensure the stability and successful execution of the Hybrid Voting and Stacking ensembles, we excluded the Word2Vec and FastText models. While these embedding techniques offer theoretical advantages, their practical implementation required careful handling of OOV words, which we did not fully address within the pipeline structure for these sections, leading to their exclusion from the combined hybrid ensembles. I could have expanded the parameters for training vocabulary, but I had to consider efficiency. The main model architecture is a decision tree, and Word2Vec/FastText only serve the role of text embedding or vectorization. For every inference, text embedding must occur before running the decision tree model. Increasing the text embedding complexity will drive greater costs and inefficiencies of inference. The goal of this project is to gain a directional view of which techniques and decision tree models work best for our problem statement of initial candidate screening in recruitment.
Data-Driven Approach to Identifying Target Recall (0.7 / 70%)
I built a data-driven approach and system to identifying, setting and optimizing for a target recall of 0.7 (or 70%) in our analysis.
Problem Context: In a recruitment prioritization task, such as initial candidate screening on an applicant tracking system application, minimizing false negatives, or missing out on a good candidate/talent, is often more critical than minimizing false positives, or reviewing a candidate who is not a "Best Match." This translates to a business decision of prioritizing recall. From my perspective, the war for talent is always occurring in any given sector, regardless of the current labor market cycle. This is driven by the perpetual mean reversion of a company's DuPont-popularized Returns on Asset (ROA). If you are interested, you can compare this across accountancy-based ROA metrics of public companies, the timing of news announcements about strategic and high-ticket hires, and the timing of approaching mean ROA relative to competitors.
Recall Capability Analysis: Our analysis of Precision-Recall curves for each model using Out-of-Fold (OOF) probabilities from the cross-validated training data showed the trade-off between achieving higher recall and the corresponding drop in precision for each model. We specifically evaluated the achievable precision at different recall levels, for example, 0.70 or 0.75.
Recall Target Recommendation: Based on this capability analysis, we made a data-driven recommendation for the recall target. We identified a target of 70% as a "balanced" target, achievable by a significant number of models with reasonable precision. I generated a tabular artifact using Python to visualize the detailed results for each model. This approach prevents me from 'plucking' an arbitrary recall goal out of the air and setting an unrealistically high target that most models cannot meet without severely trading off precision.
Optimal Threshold Finding: For the chosen target recall of 70%, we then found the specific probability threshold for each model that maximized precision while achieving at least the target recall on the OOF probabilities. We then use this threshold to make predictions. That said, decision thresholds can also be strategic tools for a firm/organization if the costs of a false negative from an inference/prediction are significantly high or incalculable e.g., harm to humans (See my previous article).
Evaluation at Optimized Thresholds: Our final evaluation of the models on the held-out test set used these optimized thresholds. The performance tables and plots clearly show how each model performed at its tailored threshold, specifically highlighting the achieved recall, precision, and F1-score. This validated how well the optimization based on training data translated to unseen test data. The summary table shows that 10 out of 16 models successfully achieved or exceeded the 70% recall target on the test set at their optimized thresholds, demonstrating the effectiveness of this approach.
This data-driven process allowed us to select a pragmatic recall target and find the corresponding thresholds that balance the business need for high recall with the need to maintain acceptable precision, rather than arbitrarily selecting a threshold like 0.5 or 0.7.Figure: Model Performance Summary.
