## Article #12

**Title:** Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks (Part 1): Navigate the Structure and Complexity of Neural Networks

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/expanding-ai-power-value-through-neural-networks-part-lim-rd0oc/

**Content:**

Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks (Part 1): Navigate the Structure and Complexity of Neural Networks
The journey from symbolic rules to intuitive intelligence marks a fundamental shift in how we approach artificial intelligence. After completing three modules of Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" certificate program under Lutz Finger's instruction, I begin my reflections on the fourth program module, "Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks." Within this fourth module, I shall recap and reflect on first class, "Navigate the Structure and Complexity of Neural Networks," which introduces the mathematical, architectural and ‘learning’ fundamentals of neural networks (or neural nets). These are unchanging and fundamental knowledge. Through this fundamental understanding, I begin to see how neural networks extend beyond traditional AI (or classical AI or Good Old Fashioned AI) by capturing very subtle patterns we struggle to identify and articulate about the real world.
From Explicit Rules to Tacit Knowledge
Neural networks (or neural nets) address a fundamental challenge in AI known as Polanyi's paradox. In the eCornell program, we learned that humans possess remarkable abilities to recognize patterns and make decisions based on intuition, yet we often cannot explain precisely how we arrive at these conclusions. Consider how effortlessly a human can distinguish between a cat and a dog, yet struggle to provide explicit & definitive rules to explain images that are "cat-like" versus "dog-like." This limitation of human language to fully describe our knowledge creates a ceiling for traditional rule-based AI systems.
The shift from Good Old-Fashioned AI (GOFAI) to neural networks represents a paradigm change in how we encode knowledge (or ‘intelligence’). While GOFAI relies on explicit, human-defined rules and decision boundaries, neural networks develop their own internal and latent representations through exposure with data. These neural network-based systems essentially learn and create features (without explicit programming), capturing relationships we might never have conceived or have been able to articulate.
The Cornellian Beginnings and Architecture of Deep Learning
In the eCornell program and under Lutz Finger’s instruction, I learned the mathematical makeup and architectures of neural networks. Neural networks achieve remarkable complexity by connecting basic units called perceptrons (also called nodes or neurons by today’s definition). Each perceptron combines a linear function within an activation function, such as the sigmoid function used in logistic regression. These individual neurons act as switches, activating when certain thresholds are met, much like how electronic transistors enable computer logic through AND, OR, and NOT operations.
The power of neural networks emerges when we stack these simple perceptrons into ‘layers’. Lutz Finger demonstrated this concept through practical, real-life examples and datasets. While simple relationships between two variables could be modeled with a single layer, more complex interactions or relationships involve more variables (and their weights) required adding hidden layers. These additional layers created intermediate representations that captured nuanced relationships or very subtle patterns beyond what we could explicitly define. This performance improvement comes with significant computational costs. Each additional hidden layer multiplies the number of parameters that must be processed during forward propagation and updated during backpropagation (as discussed about Epoch AI’s research reports around compute requirements in our previous article).
Lutz Finger also shed light on Cornell University’s special place in AI history as the birthplace of the first perceptron. The story began in July 1958, the US Office of Naval Research funded a project invention which is an AI device that classifies visual patterns, albeit 60 years before ChatGPT became commonplace. An IBM 704 computer, the size of a room, was fed a series of punch cards. After 50 trials, the computer successfully ‘learnt’ to distinguish cards marked on the left from cards marked on the right. This system comprises of three layers: 1) a photocell-based image sensor/retina as its input layer (400 units in 20x20 grid), 2) an association layer with 512 randomly connected units with fixed weights and 3) an output layer of eight perceptron with trainable weights Each perceptron 'decides' whether to fire based on whether its weighted sum exceeds a threshold. While this system had three layers of units, only the weights connecting to the output layer were ‘trained’, unlike modern deep networks where weights between all layers are learned.
This was the first demonstration of the Mark 1 perceptron and was “the first machine which is capable of having an original idea," according to notable builder of both creations, Frank Rosenblatt '50, Ph.D. '56. At that time, Rosenblatt was research psychologist and project engineer at the Cornell Aeronautical Laboratory. He later became an associate professor of neurobiology and behavior in Cornell’s Division of Biological Sciences. Inspired by the way neurons are connected together by synapses in the human brain, Rosenblatt had designed the perceptron to classify visual input into two possible categories. The neural network makes a prediction – say, right or left; or dog or cat – and if it’s wrong, tweaks itself to make a more informed prediction next time. It becomes more accurate over thousands or millions of iterations. “Stories about the creation of machines having human qualities have long been a fascinating province in the realm of science fiction,” Rosenblatt wrote in 1958. “Yet we are about to witness the birth of such a machine – a machine capable of perceiving, recognizing and identifying its surroundings without any human training or control.”
Fig. 1: Frank Rosenblatt '50, Ph.D. '56, New York native and Cornell graduate (Image Credits: Cornell Chronicle)
Fig. 2: An image of the perceptron from Rosenblatt's "The Design of an Intelligent Automaton," Summer 1958 (Image Credits: Cornell University's Division of Rare and Manuscript Collections)
Just like how large language models (LLMs) captured global attention, the Mark I perceptron drew immense public imagination and media attention. A New York Times headline had even read “NEW NAVY DEVICE LEARNS BY DOING: Psychologist Shows Embryo of Computer Designed to Read and Grow Wiser.” This initial excitement was followed by the decades which marked the “AI winter” during which federal funding for AI wound down. Meanwhile, skepticism about the perceptron's capabilities was growing in academic circles. Minsky and Selfridge (1961) posed the first real critique and were skeptical that the perceptron can generalize towards binary parity. Subsequently, the famous book “Perceptrons” by Minsky and Papert (1969, 1988) proved mathematically that the perceptron was incapable of using locally learned ‘examples’ to make global generalizations. They also conjectured that this limitation held true for the perceptron and its variants of multi-layer neural networks (Surprise, surprise! – as we now know from today's deep learning revolution and AI supercycle). In the following year 1970, Minsky won the A.M. Turing Award 1970. Minsky’s academic conclusion about neural networks had doomed Rosenblatt’s work and casted doubt on the capabilities of neural networks in general till the mid-1980s.
Contrary to Minsky’s conjecture, modern neural networks dwarf the original perceptron in scale, capability and use. As Lutz Finger took a video of himself standing in front of Gates Hall where Cornell's software engineering programs are housed, he reminded us of the remarkable journey from those humble beginnings to our smartphones of today. In hindsight, Minsky’s conjecture seems unjustified as we see advanced forms of multi-layer neural networks (including LLMs) proliferating in research and use today. Unlike Rosenblatt’s Mark 1 perceptron, today's neural networks can have dozens to hundreds of hidden layers, with millions or even billions of parameters (weights and biases) distributed across these layers. For instance, Open AI’s GPT-3 has 96 “hidden” layers (excluding input and output layers) and 175 billion parameters. Vision models like ResNet-152 Convolutional Neural Network, used for image classification, has 152 “hidden” layers with 60 million parameters. Even 'smaller' models like BERT, widely used for natural language processing, contains 24 “hidden” layers and 340 million parameters.
Demystifying Hidden Layers Through Visualization
Hidden layers enable neural networks to create their own features and logic that humans might never conceive. One of the eCornell program exercises involving hands-on classification experiments with Google's TensorFlow Playground is an apt example. Using TensorFlow Playground web app, I get to visually observe how neural networks transform simple inputs into increasingly complex representations and how different network architectures handle varying levels of complexity.
Simple classification problems easily demonstrate the fundamental role of weights and decision boundaries. The visualization revealed how each neuron learns specific features, with their combined outputs forming varying decision boundaries (depending on model architectures e.g. number of layers or neurons in each layer). The thickness of connecting lines represents the weights between layers. These weights determine how strongly each neuron influences the next layer's decisions. This program exercise revealed the transformation process visually, showing how different network architectures handle varying levels of complexity.
Fig. 3: TensorFlow Playground visualization of single layer neural network with weights but no hidden layers  (Image Credit: Tensor Playground https://playground.tensorflow.org)
When attempting to classify (separate) two groups of data points that form a circular pattern, the neural network required specific features to capture more complex, non-linear relationships. A neural network without any hidden layers struggled with predictive classification of two-colored categories in the circular dataset (test loss: 1.391, training loss: 1.399; Worse than random guessing). The neural network could not map the circular boundary separating the two classes. This demonstrates that linear relationships alone are insufficient to handle more complex, non-linear patterns.
Adding hidden layers dramatically improved performance on the circular dataset. With 4 hidden layers, the neural network successfully classified the same circular pattern (test loss: 0.000, training loss: 0.001). Given the addition of hidden layers, the network could now create polynomial features (like X₁² and X₂²), that are essential for mapping circular boundaries.
The classification of more complex patterns like spirals demand deeper neural network architectures with multiple hidden layers. Through this particular exercise, I learned that spiral patterns required extensive networks with more layers and numerous neurons. The network progressively mapped out these intricate boundaries, creating features that would be nearly impossible for humans to define manually by feature engineering. Each layer built upon the previous one, combining simple structures into increasingly complex representations of our datasets.
These visualization exercises provide crucial insights into how neural networks process information through their hidden layers. By examining the activated neurons at different layers and reconstructing them as images or Javascript-based animations, we can take a superficial peek inside the "black box." We can observe how neural networks progress from identifying basic edges in the initial hidden layer to combining them into deeper layers to capture complex patterns. I also learned that the actual features created by these neural networks rarely match human intuition or expectations.
Automating Feature Engineering in Deep Learning
Traditional machine learning often requires extensive manual feature engineering to improve model performance. Data scientists spend considerable time transforming raw data, creating interaction terms, and developing domain-specific features to help algorithms identify patterns. Lutz Finger shared his own experiences where he had created hundreds of variables from just 15 original inputs through feature engineering. While this improved accuracy, it also introduced computational overhead that made the solution impractical for real-time inferences and decisions.
Neural networks automate this feature engineering process through their layered architecture. Rather than requiring humans to manually building features like "price per square foot" in real estate modeling or mathematical transformations like X₁² or sin(X₂), neural networks can capture subtle patterns and discover their representations automatically. The complexity of the relationships or parameters between these hidden layers essentially perform feature engineering at scale, creating abstract representations that capture complex, non-linear relationships in datasets.
Neural Networks Across Learning Approaches
During the course of the eCornell program, Lutz Finger demonstrated how neural networks excel across the three major learning paradigms and approaches - supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning, each underpinned by distinct mathematical principles and pattern recognition strategies.
Supervised learning leverages labeled data to learn mappings from inputs to outputs, with loss functions and finetuning/optimization techniques for training. Neural networks’ ability to model complex, nonlinear relationships makes them the method of choice for high- dimensional pattern recognition tasks, from image classification to speech recognition.
Unsupervised learning focuses on discovering hidden patterns in unlabeled data, with neural networks enabled advanced capabilities for clustering, dimensionality reduction, and generative modeling. Proven model architectures such as autoencoders, variational autoencoders (VAEs), and generative adversarial networks (GANs) rely on mathematical principles of information theory, probability, and optimization to learn compact, meaningful representations.
Reinforcement learning (RL) combines neural networks with Markovian decision processes and reward-based optimization, enabling agents to learn complex behaviors through trial and error. The integration of deep learning with RL (deep reinforcement learning) has led to breakthroughs in game playing, robotics, and autonomous systems, with neural networks serving as powerful function approximators or optimizers for value functions and policies. Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has emerged as a particularly impactful technique, using human preferences to train reward models that guide neural network optimization. This approach has been instrumental in aligning large language models with human values and expectations, demonstrating how neural networks can learn not just from data patterns but from nuanced human feedback.
The mathematical foundations of these learning approaches in modern AI are increasingly intertwined as modern techniques blend these approaches. Unsupervised pretraining on vast unlabeled datasets before being fine-tuned on specific supervised tasks helps improve efficiency of supervised learning (e.g., BERT and GPT achieve their capabilities with relatively little task-specific data). Meanwhile, reinforcement learning techniques are being applied beyond game plays to train models for complex reasoning, enabling AI systems to think through multi-step problems rather than simply recognize patterns.
The Human Element Remains Critical
Despite their sophistication, neural networks still require human oversight and critical thinking. The program highlighted this through several real-world case studies where the algorithm (or model) used to identify welfare fraud was ultimately found unlawful. The issue was not with AI itself but with how human system designers set up and scaled the algorithm without proper oversight, resulting in disastrous corner or edge cases. This serves as a crucial reminder that we remain transparent, responsible and accountable for the AI models we design, create and deploy, especially in high-stakes business and societal applications.
Lutz Finger taught that transparency and accountability must guide our deployment of neural networks. While hidden layers operate as "black boxes" whose logic may be beyond complete human comprehension, we can still test and analyze output and outcomes. The eCornell program emphasized that we should never blindly trust AI systems and omit controls. Lutz Finger emphasized that we need quality metrics, continuous evaluation & monitoring, and multidisciplinary oversight to ensure these systems create genuine value while avoiding harmful outcomes during value capture.
Lutz Finger also reinforced the key lesson that the iterative nature of data science and AI engineering means that we must constantly question our assumptions and actively seek diverse feedback to validate our priors about the real-world and AI models. In this context, assumptions also include consensus perspectives, blind spots or biases. This learning habit of constructive criticism or intellectual honesty is not the easiest habit or process but it is crucial. Neural networks might identify patterns we cannot see, but they are also equally capable of amplifying biases, capturing spurious associations or optimizing misaligned outcomes. Through the program exercises, I learned that even small changes in neural network model architectures, hyperparameters like activation functions, or training decisions like the number of epochs can dramatically impact model performance and behavior.
Practical Applications and Business Value
Understanding neural network architectures enables more informed decisions about when and how to deploy these powerful tools. Lutz Finger emphasized that neural networks are not “silver bullet” solutions but rather sophisticated tools for specific problems and objectives. They excel when dealing with high-dimensionality data where traditional feature engineering becomes impractical or when capturing tacit knowledge beyond the limitations of explicit rule formulation.
Lutz Finger had also invited discussion about real-world AI applications through the Andrew Huberman bot example. This demonstration showed how neural networks can synthesize vast amounts of research and knowledge to create conversational AI that provides domain-specific insights. The bot illustrated how deep layers enable systems to capture nuanced understanding that would be impossible to program through traditional rule-based or symbolic approaches.
Trends and Advances in Industry Deployments of Neural Networks
Neural networks have become a key part of modern AI deployments. They can be trained using several learning paradigms, each suited to different types of problems and data availability. Supervised learning remains the predominant method in commercial neural network deployments, powering applications from medical imaging to fraud detection and predictive maintenance. In this approach, networks learn from labeled examples where the correct answer is known.
Neural networks trained through unsupervised and self-supervised learning now extract powerful insights from unlabeled data, transforming how companies like Netflix and Spotify understand user behavior. Unsupervised learning identifies patterns and structure without predefined labels, using techniques like autoencoders for dimensionality reduction, clustering with self-organizing maps, and generative models such as GANs and VAEs. Self-supervised learning, a particularly powerful form of unsupervised learning, has become foundational to modern AI. These models train on vast amounts of unlabeled data by generating their own supervisory signals, such as predicting masked or next tokens in text. This approach now underpins natural language processing, computer vision, and genomics applications.
Reinforcement learning approaches for neural networks have moved from the research lab to production systems spanning the factory floors, trading desks, operating rooms, robotic assembly lines and even Tesla’s Autopilot. Reinforcement learning enables adaptive, goal-driven AI systems that learn from interaction and feedback. Unlike supervised learning, RL agents learn optimal behaviors through trial and error, receiving rewards or penalties based on their actions in an environment. Instead of using LiDAR, Tesla integrates reinforcement learning with its multiple cameras positioned around the car, feeding visual data into neural networks that interpret surroundings in real-time, learns from past mistakes and refines its driving strategy over time. RL-trained robots are deployed for precision assembly, warehouse picking, and autonomous driving, with real-world deployments benefiting from advances in simulation-to-reality transfer and lifelong learning. The technique has also mastered complex games like AlphaGo and Dota 2. Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has emerged as a particularly impactful technique for aligning large language models with human values and expectations. In RLHF, humans provide preferences between different model outputs (such as rating which response is more helpful or accurate), and these preferences train a reward model that guides the neural network's optimization during fine-tuning. This approach has been instrumental in making LLMs like OpenAI ChatGPT and Anthropic Claude more useful and aligned with user intentions. RLHF also enables GitHub Copilot, OpenAI Codex, and similar tools to improve code suggestions by learning from developer feedback. When developers accept, modify, or reject suggestions, these actions serve as training signals that refine the model's understanding of code quality, security, and developer intent.
The business and industry applications of neural networks span numerous domains:
Banking and Financial services: neural networks evaluate credit and loan applications, detect fraud and assess risk, predict loan delinquencies, and analyze customer attrition: 1) J.P. Morgan has been using AI-powered fraud detection for more than three years to handle rising fraud attempts and false positives, reporting fewer fraud cases and reduced false positives, 2) Capital One uses gradient boosting machine algorithms to enhance fraud detection capabilities and successfully identify potential fraudulent transactions, 3) Stripe built the world's first foundation model for payments, a transformer-based neural network trained on tens of billions of transactions using unsupervised learning to generate nuanced latent embeddings. These embeddings are then fed into a classifier to predict card-testing attacks in real time. This two-stage approach - 1) foundation model for embeddings and 2) predictive classifier - increased Stripe's detection rate for attacks on large businesses from 59% to 97% practically overnight.
Business Analytics: Neural networks to model customer behavior, predict purchases and renewals, segment customers, and analyze credit line usage for loan advising and real estate appraisal.
Transportation: Neural networks power routing systems, truck brake diagnosis, and vehicle scheduling.
Medicine and Healthcare: Neural networks are used to perform cancer cell analysis, provide emergency room test advisement, and assist in prosthesis design.
Social Media: LinkedIn applies neural networks along with linear text classifiers to detect spam or abusive content in feeds and to understand the kinds of content shared on the platform, enabling better recommendation and search products for members and customers.
DevOps/Observability/Infrastructure Monitoring: Datadog has developed Toto, a time series foundation model built using transformer architecture—the same neural network design underlying LLMs. Trained on nearly one trillion data points, Toto achieves superior performance on observability metrics by processing high-frequency data and accurately identifying trends and seasonality patterns. Datadog's Bits AI suite provides autonomous agents that investigate alerts, suggest code fixes with full stack telemetry context, and take action on external systems, enabling engineering teams to scale without proportional headcount increases.
Application Monitoring and Debugging: Sentry.io leverages embeddings, large language models (LLMs) and neural networks to transform debugging through its AI-powered agent Seer for root cause analysis. Since beta, Seer has helped fix 38,000+ issues, identified root causes with 94.5% accuracy, and saved development teams over 2 years in aggregate debugging time. Seer accesses comprehensive context including stack traces, logs, commit history, and source code to automatically identify root causes, suggest fixes, generate code changes with tests, and open pull requests, demonstrating debugging accuracy comparable to senior engineers.
Entertainment and Content Streaming: Netflix employs deep neural networks including variational autoencoders and recurrent neural networks to model sequential viewing patterns, with their personalization algorithms saving over $1 billion annually by reducing subscriber churn. The streaming giant processes several terabytes of interaction data daily, with 75-80% of all viewing hours coming from algorithmic recommendations rather than user searches. Spotify leverages graph neural networks, reinforcement learning, and heterogeneous graph neural networks to personalize music and audiobook recommendations, resulting in a 46% increase in new audiobooks start rate and a 23% boost in streaming rates.
Building and deploying these neural network applications requires robust infrastructure and tooling. Several platforms have emerged as leaders in enabling enterprise neural network (or deep learning) workflows:
IBM's enterprise AI and data platform and IBM watsonx Studio offers tools for training, validating, and deploying generative and self-supervised models with minimal labeled data.
Dataiku is a leading enterprise AI platform, widely adopted across industries for building, deploying, and managing machine learning and deep learning models, including those based on unsupervised and self-supervised learning.
In addition to Dataiku and IBM watsonx , several other platforms are recognized as leaders in enabling enterprise neural network workflows:
Databricks : Excels in scalable, distributed training of deep learning models, with strong support for unsupervised/self-supervised learning and MLOps
Amazon Web Services (AWS)'s Amazon SageMaker: Comprehensive ML lifecycle support, including built-in and custom unsupervised/self-supervised learning, and managed reinforcement learning environments
H2O.ai: Known for AutoML, explainable AI, and support for unsupervised and deep learning workflows
Google Cloud Vertex AI and Microsoft Azure ML: Both offer robust support for unsupervised, self-supervised, and reinforcement learning, with deep integration into their respective cloud ecosystems
These platforms are widely adopted by enterprises to build, train, and deploy neural network models for a variety of use cases, from anomaly detection and recommendation systems to computer vision and NLP.
World Models: The New Frontier in Robotics and Simulation
As foundational as these neural network concepts are, the field continues to evolve at a remarkable pace. During the eCornell program, Lutz Finger emphasized that mastering the fundamentals, including perceptrons (or neurons or nodes), hidden layers, backpropagation, is key for understanding next-generation model architectures, novel techniques and applications that are reshaping modern AI capabilities.
World models represent one such evolution, extending neural networks' pattern recognition into simulation and spatial reasoning. World models are large, generative neural networks capable of simulating and reasoning about complex, dynamic environments have the potential to power next-generation robotics and autonomous systems. Meta AI's V-JEPA 2, a 1.2 billion-parameter model trained on over one million hours of video and one million images, enables state-of-the-art understanding, prediction, and zero-shot planning for robots in new environments. After training on just 62 hours of robot data, V-JEPA 2 achieves success rates of 65-80% for pick-and-place tasks involving objects and settings not encountered during training, when deployed zero-shot on robots in new laboratory environments.
Healthcare robotics represents a promising area where world models could enhance capabilities. Improvements in accuracy and reduced complication rates have driven rapid adoption of AI-assisted robotic surgery across various surgical specialties. The keyword is “assisted”. Human expertise and oversight are still critical for high-stakes applications such as surgeries, while AI augments precision, consistency, and real-time decision support. Just as we observed neural networks learning abstract representations of patterns in the eCornell program, surgical robots can leverage world model architectures to gradually learn spatial representations of specific human anatomy and tissue structures, complementing (or augmenting) rather than replacing human judgment.
Industrial and logistics robotics could benefit from world model integration. Industrial and logistics robotics has the potential to benefit from world model integration. Today, DHL’s AI-powered sorting robot - DHLBot - increased capacity by at least 40% after adoption in Singapore and South Korea. Amazon’s Sequoia system uses AI, robotics, and computer vision systems for inventory identification and storage, speeding up fulfilment center processing by 75%. In September 2025, the International Federation of Robotics reported global operational stock of industrial robots reached 4.66 million units in 2024 (9% year-over-year increase from previous year). In terms of installments, Asia accounted for 74% of new deployments in 2024, compared with 16% in Europe and 9% in the Americas. Within Asia, China led with 54% of global deployments, followed by Japan, South Korea, and India. By enabling robots to simulate and reason about complex, dynamic warehouse environments, world model architectures could potentially accelerate this growth trajectory beyond research settings, allowing pretrained systems to adapt to novel situations without retraining and handle more diverse object types.
Research platforms are accelerating world model development and adoption. Google DeepMind's Genie 3 and NVIDIA's Cosmos World Foundation Models provide physics-aware simulation environments that generate photorealistic, physics-based synthetic data. These platforms enable robots to train in simulation before real-world deployment, reducing reliance on costly physical testing. At the same time, researchers can explore "what if" scenarios and corner/edge cases that would be impractical or dangerous to test with real physical assets and human lives. These models allow for the generation of photorealistic, physics-based synthetic data, accelerating training and reducing reliance on costly physical testing.
These emerging architectures build directly on the foundational concepts we studied in the eCornell program. Just as we observed hidden layers creating abstract representations of circular and spiral patterns, world models create temporal and spatial representations of physical environments. The same principles of automated feature engineering that distinguished neural networks from traditional AI now enable robots to develop physical intuition by understanding object permanence, gravity, and causality without explicit programming. Understanding these foundations positions us to engage critically with AI's rapid evolution while maintaining the rigor, transparency, and accountability that Lutz Finger emphasized throughout the program.
Anthropic’s Model Context Protocol (MCP): Standardizing Agentic AI
In modern AI applications, LLMs are sophisticated text-to-text predictive neural networks with hundreds of billions of parameters distributed across deep architectures, embodying the same foundational principles we studied: Pattern recognition through layered representations & backpropagation, learning from vast datasets, and generating predictions through forward propagation.
As LLM adoption grew, a critical challenge emerged when connecting these models to enterprise data and tools in a standardized, scalable way. In November 2024, Anthropic introduced the Model Context Protocol (MCP) as a potential open infrastructure standard for connecting AI agents (which are systems that use LLMs) to enterprise data, tools, and workflows. MCP enables large language models to access real-time company data and orchestrate actions across multiple systems, moving beyond isolated chatbots to integrated AI applications.
MCP distinguishes itself from traditional REST APIs through its design for autonomous AI agent interaction. Rather than requiring developers to hardcode specific and potentially brittle integrations, MCP enables AI systems to discover and use available capabilities dynamically through persistent connections. The protocol organizes these capabilities into three primitives: resources (data sources), tools (executable actions), and prompts (behavioral templates). Machine-readable descriptions allow agents to understand and combine these capabilities without explicit programming for each integration.
The architecture follows a client-server model with standardized interfaces for secure, bidirectional communication. Host applications connect to multiple MCP servers that expose specific data sources or tools. To accelerate adoption, Anthropic pre-built MCP servers for popular enterprise systems including Google Drive, Slack, GitHub, Postgres, and Puppeteer.
Fig 4.: Example of an MCP architecture using a host application such as Claude Desktop
To explore MCP's practical capabilities firsthand, I built the Tweet Processor MCP Agent, an automated content distribution system for my personal CoreAI newsletter (Check out GitHub repo: https://github.com/youshen-lim/tweet-processor-mcp-agent).
This personal exploration of MCP was fundamentally driven by a personal need and conversations with Andrew Hoh, co-founder of LastMile AI, about the powerful patterns available in their popular MCP Agent Cloud framework. My Tweet Processor system orchestrates Google Drive, Claude Sonnet 4.5, and Twitter through standardized MCP interfaces. It reads my published newsletter articles, analyzes them to extract strategic insights, generates tweet variations, and manages posting schedules, all without custom integration code for each API. While authentication still requires API credentials, MCP eliminates the need to write custom code for each service's specific API methods and data formats. The MCP servers handle the complexity of interacting with Google Drive's document API, Twitter's posting endpoints, and Claude's inference API. The implementation has successfully processed and posted real tweets on schedule, validating MCP's composability in production. Intelligent caching of article analyses across multiple tweet variations reduces redundant API calls, demonstrating how MCP enables both rapid development and operational efficiency.
Anthropic's 2025 "Building Effective Agents" guide provides a framework for developers creating production AI systems. The guide draws from Anthropic's work with dozens of teams building LLM agents across industries. The research reveals that the most successful implementations use simple, composable patterns rather than overly-complex frameworks or highly-specialized libraries. Therefore, this Anthropic "Building Effective Agents" guide emphasizes that agents should 1) maintain simplicity in design, 2) prioritize design transparency and accountability by explicitly showing planning steps, and 3) carefully craft component interfaces through thorough: 3a. tool documentation and 3b. testing.
The guide outlines key architectural principles, patterns and best practices to create a ideal foundation for reliable agent systems.:
Augmented LLM: An LLM enhanced with retrieval, tools, and memory serves as the foundational building block
Prompt chaining: Complex tasks decompose into sequences of steps, where each LLM call processes the output of the previous step
Routing: Systems direct inputs to specialized prompts, allowing separation of concerns and optimized performance for different task types
Parallelization: Tasks break into independent subtasks that run simultaneously for speed, with results aggregated afterward
Orchestrator-workers: A central LLM dynamically breaks down tasks, delegates them to worker LLMs, and synthesizes their results
Evaluator-optimizer: One LLM generates a response while another provides evaluation and feedback in a loop
MCP adoption is showing initial promise. At time of MCP’s introduction in November 2024, Anthropic mentioned that major companies like Block, Apollo, and Replit are integrating MCP into their systems. By February 2025, Ksenia Se from Turing Post blogged that there were over 1,000 open & community-built MCP servers (connectors). Enterprise case studies from 2025 help define the impact of MCP-powered agents. In August 2025, TELUS deployed Claude with MCP integrations to allow employees, including developers, to build custom AI solutions for their department needs. This bundle of Claude and MCP resulted in the creation of over 13,000 internal AI-powered tools and contributed to the time savings of more than 500,000 staff hours through workflow automation.
MCP's standardized approach enables rapid development of agent systems for structured, repetitive workflows. The protocol excels at orchestrating multi-step tasks across multiple services: Data sourcing/gathering, text-generation (or text prediction), and distribution to destination services.
Fig. 5.: My MCP Three-Layer Architecture Perspective: MCP provides standardized service access but system designs and developers are responsible for designing business logic, state management and orchestration patterns.
My Tweet Processor demonstrates this workflow, using Google Drive (file system) to read my previously published newsletter articles, Claude Sonnet 4.5 to extract insights and compose tweets through prompt chaining, and Twitter to post content on schedule. The system implements my ideal workflow state management to ensure systematic distribution: 1) Article posts in sequential order, each generates four unique variations, and 2) State persistence tracks published content to prevent repetition. This validates MCP's composability in production while executing practical patterns, including stateful orchestration, when building similar automation workflows.
The complete Tweet Processor implementation, including architecture decisions and lessons learned, is available at https://github.com/youshen-lim/tweet-processor-mcp-agent.
That said, MCP's standardized approach and the community's open frameworks reduces the technical complexity of building AI agents, but technical feasibility alone does not guarantee business value creation and capture. Organizations must still evaluate whether a specific workflow justifies the investment in AI agent development. AI agents excel at workflows with predictable sequences, system coordination bottlenecks, and clear success criteria. They struggle with tasks relying heavily on human expertise & intuition, or adaptive reasoning. This basic framework helps evaluate whether a specific workflow justifies agent development investment. It is not exhaustive and should be adapted to your organization's specific context, risk tolerance, compliance requirements, and strategic priorities.
For example, my Tweet Processor scores high across all factors:
Objective success criteria (280-character limit, professional style, automated distribution)
Repetitive execution (weekly), clear sequence (read → generate → post),
Multi-system coordination bottleneck (Pre-agent workflow involves manually copying between Google Drive, Claude, Twitter)
MCP's early adoption demonstrates the value of composable agent architectures. Enterprise deployments like TELUS (500,000 staff hours saved through 13,000 AI-powered tools) and rapid community growth (over 1,000 MCP servers created within months) validate the shift toward standardized protocols. For developers building automation systems, the complete Tweet Processor implementation provides a basic template and practical reference for orchestrating services, managing workflow state, and designing reliable agent patterns: https://github.com/youshen-lim/tweet-processor-mcp-agent. As the ecosystem grows with enterprise adoption and community contributions, MCP has potential to establish itself as an open infrastructure standard for the next generation of AI applications.
Looking Ahead
The journey from Cornell's 1958 Rosenblatt perceptron to today's billion-parameter LLMs reveals how mastering neural network foundations enables us to navigate AI's exponential evolution. This first class "Navigate the Structure and Complexity of Neural Networks” covers the foundational understanding of neural network architecture that sets the stage for exploring more advanced topics in deep learning.
Three critical themes emerge from this foundational understanding under Lutz Finger’s instruction and key lessons:
First, neural networks are evolving from simple classifiers to powerful, complex systems that predict physical dynamics and coordinate multi-system workflows. World models and frameworks like MCP demonstrate this expansion, demanding we think beyond accuracy metrics to consider integration with human judgment.
Second, standardization is democratizing AI development. MCP's growth from 1,000+ community servers within months to enterprise use cases signal initial shifts from low-level, community implementations to business focus on workflow design and business logic. My Tweet Processor application is a very simple example to demonstrate the latter in the course of driving a personal outcome.
Third, human oversight remains irreplaceable for quality control and preventing harmful outcomes. As we learned from eCornell program’s case studies, neural networks can amplify biases and create disastrous edge cases when deployed without proper oversight. In my previous article, I discussed research that demonstrate that bias can persist in LLMs. Whether in surgical robotics or agent systems, these pattern recognition tools augment rather than replace human expertise. Neural networks identify patterns, including biased & harmful ones. Humans must provide intuition, wisdom, context, ethical reasoning, and continuous monitoring for performance & fairness.
As I continue through this module and explore backpropagation, activation functions, specialized model architectures (like GANs) and finetuning techniques, the key lies in developing judgment and making (mostly) right decisions about their applications. These must be decisions that optimize for a business or societal loss objective better than other alternative solutions. When does model complexity justify reduced interpretability? Which workflows merit automation investment? These context-specific trade-offs require both technical understanding and business acumen.
The transformation from explicit rules to learned representations that began with Rosenblatt's perceptron continues to accelerate. Yet the core challenge remains: how do we harness sophisticated pattern matching to create value while maintaining human agency? These systems find statistical regularities we might never discover manually, but operate without consciousness or understanding. Neural networks are instruments for augmenting human capability through pattern recognition, not substitutes for human judgment.
Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program transforms abstract neural network concepts into practical frameworks for AI implementation. Through Lutz Finger's expert instruction and hands-on exercises, participants gain essential skills for understanding how neural networks capture tacit knowledge and automate feature engineering. The eCornell program equips professionals with fundamental knowledge and tools to navigate the complexity of hidden layers while maintaining transparency and accountability in AI deployment. Whether you are a product manager, data scientist, business strategist, or technology leader, this eCornell Certificate Program provides the foundational understanding needed to leverage neural networks effectively in the AI era. I encourage professionals seeking to advance their understanding of deep learning architectures to explore this transformative learning experience at: https://ecornell.cornell.edu/certificates/technology/designing-and-building-ai-solutions/
Join the Conversation
Your insights and experiences with neural network implementation enrich our collective understanding. I invite you to weigh in and share your thoughts.
Question #1: How does your organization balance the power of neural networks' automated feature discovery with the need for interpretability and transparency? What strategies have you found most effective for explaining "black box" decisions to stakeholders?
Question #2: What challenges have you encountered when determining the optimal neural network architecture for your specific use cases? How did you navigate the trade-offs between model complexity and computational efficiency?
About This Series

Stay tuned for upcoming articles covering deeper dives into my learning reflection specific neural network architectures, backpropagation, activation functions, and finetuning techniques that power modern AI applications.

## Article #13

**Title:** My Learning Reflections about Optimizing Neural Networks (Part 2): The Mathematics of Learning

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/my-learning-reflections-optimizing-neural-networks-part-lim-g3ngc/

**Content:**

My Learning Reflections about Optimizing Neural Networks (Part 2): The Mathematics of Learning
Editor's Note: This is the second of multiple focused articles covering my learning reflections and industry validations about neural network optimization from Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program (Instructed by Lutz Finger). Each article examines a specific optimization aspect alongside market observations, building toward comprehensive understanding.
What I have been up to: I built the semantic layer and LLM component for my personal News Analyst application. This locally deployed system transforms high volume, fast moving AI news into structured metadata for deeper pattern analysis. Truly exciting times!
My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer project directly validates the neural network optimization principles covered in this article. The project involved data engineering work to extract and parse data from various APIs and MCP servers. I built ETL functions to format data for training. I hand annotated 460 AI news examples, inspecting each one individually. I designed Python configurations for three model hyperparameter comparisons across the Llama 3.2 1B baseline model, Tinker API finetuned model, and Unsloth finetuned model. I conducted controlled experiments, debugged training pipelines, and ran comprehensive evaluations.
Project repositories:
v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production): GitHub | Hugging Face
v1 AI Related News Enhancer (Proof of Concept): GitHub | Hugging Face
With v2 now deployed and my News Analyst application system is delivering production-ready results, we are back to regular programming! 📚
In Part 1, "My Learning Reflections about Optimizing Neural Networks," we examined how neural networks are structured. We explored basic perceptrons through to layered architectures. We learned how design choices affect model complexity and fit. Understanding architecture tells us what a neural network can potentially compute. But how does it discover what to compute?
Part 2 turns to the mathematical engine that makes learning possible: backpropagation and gradient optimization. As I continue reflecting on my 5 month journey through Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" certificate program instructed by Lutz Finger, this segment of the fourth module revealed how elegant mathematical principles enable neural networks to learn from data through systematic error minimization.
The Mathematics Behind Neural Network Learning
Backpropagation emerged as the breakthrough that made modern AI possible. It transformed neural networks from theoretical concepts into practical tools. First introduced in the 1960s, this mathematical approach remains one of the most influential concepts in AI. Lutz Finger and the eCornell program used a compelling analogy. Just as a child learns to recognize cats through repeated exposure to building and adjusting neural synapse connections in the brain, neural networks learn by adjusting their weights and priors or biases through training data.
The technical process begins with random initialization, followed by systematic error minimization through gradient descent. The neural network starts with a random guess in the form of a seed value or parameter. It makes predictions through inference. It calculates errors using a loss function. It then propagates this error backward through the network. The backward pass adjusts weights and biases in the direction that minimizes the training loss for the next forward pass. Think of it like navigating a maze by backtracking from dead ends to find the correct path.
The network repeats this process many times over the entire dataset. This allows the neural network to automatically learn feature representations from data. However, the network architecture, training procedure, and hyperparameters still require significant manual design and tuning. These repetitive cycles of forward prediction and backward adjustment of weights and biases are called backpropagation. Backpropagation makes it computationally feasible to train deep neural networks. It enables automatic feature learning without explicit programming. This distinguishes neural networks from traditional machine learning approaches.
The computational complexity scales dramatically with network architecture. In our MNIST classifier exercise in the eCornell program, we used 16 input neurons, two hidden layers of 16 neurons each, and 10 output neurons. The network must optimize 672 weights and 42 biases. That makes 714 parameters in total. Despite this complexity, the fundamental chain rule of calculus enables efficient computation of gradients for all these parameters. The chain rule applies through matrix operations.
Speaking of chain rule: Remember learning differentiation in school? This knowledge is paying off again. 📐 We use it for economic curves and business growth. We use it for financial concepts like Sharpe ratios. And now we use it for training AI.
Managing the sheer computational complexity at scale requires specialized hardware. Custom design or specialized hardware like Nvidia GPUs and Google TPUs make this possible. Unlike general purpose compute hardware, GPUs or TPUs optimize the intensive multiply accumulate (MAC) operations simultaneously at the core of these matrix operations. This makes deep learning practical at scale.
Recent Industry Developments: Training Optimization Breakthroughs
Part 1 examined hardware innovations driving neural network scale. This section focuses on algorithmic and software optimizations that complement those hardware advances.
Training optimizers are dramatically lowering memory usage and improving scalability for reinforcement learning finetuning. Liger, an optimized trainer for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), cuts memory usage by 40%. It adds FSDP and PEFT support. This makes RL fine tuning more efficient. RL techniques, such as those DeepSeek used, encourage reasoning in models. They apply large scale RL combined with simple reward models based on answer accuracy and formatting. That said, pretraining using supervised learning continues to unlock massive value for neural network development and optimization before further optimization by RL.
New methods enable high quality reasoning training at a fraction of the traditional RL cost. Thinking Machines Lab demonstrated that training smaller AI models by having a larger teacher model grade them achieves the same reasoning performance as RL at 9 to 30 times lower cost. This "on policy distillation" uses dense feedback from the teacher model while training on the student model's own outputs.
Training innovations prioritize stability and data sovereignty. , Chinese, Alibaba-back startup Moonshot AI released the Kimi K2 model. They trained it using a novel MuonClip optimizer that specifically prevents training crashes. These crashes commonly plague development of large models (e.g., trillion-parameter). Furthermore, federated computing, exemplified by Apheris, enables secure AI model training without moving highly-sensitive and regulated health data. This approach finds success with major enterprise clients like Roche.
Software and architectural breakthroughs rapidly accelerate real time inference speeds. OpenEvolve, an evolutionary coding agent, independently discovers breakthrough algorithms that achieve a 5.0 times speedup in LLM inference. End to end GPU fusion transforms LLM inference into a single megakernel. It reduces latency by 1.2 to 6.7 times by eliminating launch overhead. Cursor demonstrated that rebuilding the Mixture of Experts (MoE) layer using CUDA and PTX achieved a 3.5 times speedup in MoE operations. This resulted in a 1.5 times (1.5x) end-to-end training speedup on Blackwell GPUs compared to Hopper.
Practical Application: My v2 AI News Enhancer Project
I applied these optimization principles to my v2 llama tinker lora news enhancer project through rigorous framework comparison. My goal was to understand how different training frameworks implement backpropagation and gradient optimization in practice. I used controlled experiments to isolate the impact of implementation choices.
I compared two fine tuning frameworks: Thinking Machines Lab's Tinker API and Unsloth AI's Unsloth optimization library. Both services implement LoRA (Low Rank Adaptation) fine tuning but with different optimization strategies. Tinker is a training API that lets you control every aspect of model training and fine tuning while handling the infrastructure. You write a simple Python training loop that runs on your CPU only machine. Tinker figures out how to make the training work on a bunch of GPUs efficiently. Tinker exposes low level primitives like forward_backward() for computing gradients and optim_step() for applying weight updates. This maps directly to the backpropagation cycle. Unsloth offers a local framework with highly optimized CUDA kernels for maximum speed.
LoRA makes backpropagation practical for large language models by dramatically reducing trainable parameters. My Llama 3.2 1B model has 1.26 billion total parameters. With LoRA rank 32, only 22.5 million parameters require gradient computation. This represents a 98.2% reduction in trainable parameters. Instead of computing and storing gradients for over a billion weights, the optimizer only updates small adapter matrices. This is why I could fine tune a billion parameter model on consumer hardware in under 10 minutes.
My experimental design controlled for all hyperparameters to ensure fair comparison. I used identical configurations across both frameworks:
Base model: Llama 3.2 1B (1 billion parameters)
LoRA rank: 32 (balancing capacity with efficiency)
Learning rate: 2e-4 (standard for LoRA fine tuning; also dependent on my dataset volume)
Batch size: 4 (fits within 16GB RAM constraints)
Training steps: 455 (5 epochs times 91 steps per epoch)
Dataset: 460 hand annotated examples (364 train, 96 test)
Loss masking: Response only training on assistant outputs
Both frameworks use response only training to focus gradient computation. This technique masks the loss for user input tokens by setting their labels to negative 100. Gradients are only computed for the tokens we want the model to learn: the assistant responses. Tinker implements this through train_on_what=ALL_ASSISTANT_MESSAGES. Unsloth uses its train_on_responses_only() wrapper. This selective gradient computation makes training more efficient and focused.
The training results revealed fascinating differences in implementation efficiency. Tinker API completed training in 8.51 minutes with a final loss of 0.0447 using Negative Log Likelihood loss. Unsloth completed the same training in 3.65 minutes with a final loss of 0.1300 using Cross Entropy loss. This represents a 2.33 times speedup for Unsloth. It demonstrates the impact of optimized CUDA kernels on training efficiency.
The different loss values do not indicate different performance. They reflect different loss function implementations. Tinker uses NLL (Negative Log Likelihood) loss. Unsloth uses Cross Entropy loss. Cross Entropy and NLL are mathematically related but calculated differently. Tinker reports mean NLL across all tokens while Unsloth reports per step Cross Entropy loss. The absolute values cannot be directly compared. The true comparison comes from evaluation metrics on held out test data, not training loss values.
Both models demonstrated successful convergence through gradient descent. Unsloth showed strong convergence with 44.3% loss reduction from maximum (0.2335) to final (0.1300) over 455 training steps. The cosine learning rate schedule provided smooth decay for training stability. Five epochs proved sufficient for convergence on 364 training examples.
Both frameworks achieved production ready results despite the training speed difference. On the 96 example test set:
Tinker: F1 score 0.612, 100% JSON validity, MAE 1.80
Unsloth: F1 score 0.603, 100% JSON validity, MAE 1.43
Statistical test: p=0.698 (no significant difference)
This validates a crucial principle from the eCornell program: Optimization is about finding the right balance for your specific constraints. Thinking Machines Lab's Tinker API offers ease of use and managed infrastructure. This makes it ideal for rapid prototyping. Unsloth provides 2.33 times faster training and local control. This makes it ideal for iterative development and cost conscious deployment. Both achieve production quality. This proves that implementation efficiency matters for development velocity but not necessarily for final model performance.
The baseline comparison proved that fine tuning was essential, not optional. The untrained Llama 3.2 1B baseline achieved only 5.2% JSON validity, F1 score of 0.396, and MAE of 6.98. The fine tuned models achieved 100% JSON validity and F1 scores of 0.603 to 0.612. They also achieved MAE of 1.43 to 1.80. This represents a 55% improvement in F1 score. It also represents a 19 times improvement in JSON validity. The relevance score error dropped by nearly 5 times. This demonstrates that architectural optimization through LoRA fine tuning unlocks capabilities that the base model cannot achieve through prompting alone.
Looking Ahead
The mathematics of backpropagation and gradient optimization form the foundation of modern neural network training. Understanding these principles enables practitioners to make informed decisions about training strategies, optimizer selection, and framework choices. The industry developments of 2025 validate that optimization remains an active area of innovation. New techniques continuously emerge to improve training efficiency, reduce costs, enable new capabilities and push beyond established scaling laws.
My hands on experience with my v2 AI News Enhancer Project demonstrated that theoretical understanding translates directly into practical benefits. By understanding how different frameworks implement gradient optimization, I could make informed choices about which tools to use for different stages of development. The controlled comparison revealed that both Thinking Machines Lab's Tinker API and Unsloth achieve production ready results. The choice between them depends on specific project requirements rather than fundamental capability differences.
In the next article (Part 3), I will explore activation functions and gradient flow. I will examine how these choices affect the learning process. I will discuss strategies to address common challenges like vanishing gradients and dying ReLU neurons.
This article is Part 2 of a multi-part series on my learning reflections about neural network optimization from "Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks" module of Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program.
