## Article #16

**Title:** Neural Network Architectures and Their AI Uses Part 1: Teaching Machines to “See” with CNNs

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/neural-network-architectures-ai-uses-part-1-teaching-machines-lim-z1o0c/

**Content:**

Editor's Note
This article begins an eight-part series documenting my learning reflections about neural network model architectures and their practical applications. These reflections are drawn from fundamental learning from the "Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks" program module of Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" certificate program, instructed by Lutz Finger.
My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer project continues delivering production-ready results as I build out my personal News Analyst application. As semantic layer/component of my personal News Analyst application, this v2 AI Industry News Enhancer is a locally deployed system which transforms high-volume AI news into structured metadata for deeper pattern analysis. The architecture series builds directly on the optimization principles from my previous articles, connecting theoretical foundations to practical deployment decisions.
My v2 project fine-tuned Meta Llama 3.2-1B, a transformer-based architecture optimized for natural language processing. Understanding why transformers excel at text processing while Convolutional Neural Networks dominate image classification requires examining how different architectures solve fundamentally different problems. This series explores those architectural choices and their personal/business/societal implications.
Project repository: v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production): (GitHub | Hugging Face)
Introduction
Following my four-part series on my learning from Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" certificate program (Instructed by Lutz Finger) about general topic of artificial neural network optimization, I now turn to my learning reflections about the fundamentals of neural network model architectures and architectural selection. The previous reflection series examined how neural networks learn through backpropagation, activation functions, gradient flow, and systematic deployment practices.
This current series addresses a complementary question: Which artificial neural network architecture should you select for a given problem to create and/or capture value?
For purpose of simplicity, neural network nodes function like LEGO blocks, combining into virtually limitless architectural configurations for different personal/business/societal challenges. This analogy from Lutz Finger captures the essence of architectural design. Each node performs a straightforward operation: a linear function wrapped in an activation function. Yet when assembled strategically, these simple components tackle challenges from image recognition to time-series forecasting to natural language understanding.
The eCornell program emphasized that architectural selection must align with personal/business/societal objectives rather than technical sophistication. As Lutz Finger reminded us throughout the module, we should not get carried away by how impressive something appears. The purpose and personal/business/societal objective determine success, not architectural complexity.
Historical Foundation: Yann LeCun and the Birth of CNNs
Yann LeCun and colleagues at AT&T Bell Laboratories pioneered the Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) neural network model architecture that revolutionized Computer Vision. In their 1989 paper "Backpropagation Applied to Handwritten Zip Code Recognition," LeCun et al. demonstrated that the ability of learning networks to generalize can be greatly enhanced by providing constraints from the task domain through the architecture of the network (LeCun et al., 1989). This approach successfully recognized handwritten zip code digits provided by the U.S. Postal Service, with a single network learning the entire recognition operation from the normalized image to the final classification.
This 1989 pioneer research proved that neural networks could learn directly from images rather than hand-crafted feature vectors. Unlike previous work where the first few layers of connections were hand-chosen constants, LeCun's network learned all its parameters through backpropagation (LeCun et al., 1989). The network trained on 7,291 handwritten digits and 2,007 test examples digitized from actual U.S. mail passing through the Buffalo, NY post office. This demonstrated the ability of backpropagation networks to deal with large amounts of low-level information.
📹 Watch: The Impressive LeNet 1 Demo by a youthful Yann LeCun from 1989
Video courtesy of Larry Jackel after restoration from VHS tapes: This video demo shows LeNet 1, the first convolutional network (or CNN) that could recognize handwritten digits with relatively acceptable speed and accuracy at time of demo. Developed at AT&T Bell Labs in Holmdel, NJ, the system ran on a DSP card in a 486 PC with a video camera. The network had two layers of 5x5 convolutions with stride 2, two fully connected layers, 9,760 parameters, and 64,660 connections. There were no separate pooling layer as computation was too expensive for this neural network architecture at that time. Shortly after this demo, NCR deployed the technology in ATM machines that could read check (or cheque) amounts. By the late 1990s, these systems processed 10 to 20% of all checks in the United States.
The research culminated in the seminal 1998 paper "Gradient-Based Learning Applied to Document Recognition," which introduced LeNet-5 and established CNNs as an effective and practical architecture for image classification. This comprehensive work reviewed various methods applied to handwritten character recognition and compared them on a standard benchmark task (LeCun et al., 1998). The paper demonstrated that convolutional neural networks outperform most of other tested techniques, including traditional pattern recognition methods. With approximately 83,240 citations on Google Scholar (as of Jan 2026), this paper remains one of the most influential works in deep learning history.
The Spatial Recognition Problem for Basic Artificial Neural Network Architectures
Basic neural networks fail at spatial recognition because they cannot understand that two images shifted by a single pixel represent the same object. This fundamental limitation drove the development of Convolutional Neural Networks.
Lutz Finger demonstrated this challenge through a compelling example involving handwritten digits. When an image of a number gets converted to a feature vector, shifting that image by just one pixel creates a dramatically different numerical representation. The two versions share minimal overlap in their feature vectors despite being visually identical to human observers.
The solution mirrors what neural networks already accomplish with hidden layers: Allowing the AI model to "create" its own features, instead of manual feature engineering. For spatial data, this means introducing convolution layers that help the network understand spatial relationships before classification occurs. Rather than requiring humans to manually engineer spatial features, CNNs learn to detect edges, textures, and patterns automatically through training. "Backpropagation Applied to Handwritten Zip Code Recognition" established this principle by showing that weight sharing not only greatly reduces the number of free parameters but also can express information about the geometry and topology of the task (LeCun et al., 1989).
CNN's Three-Layer Architecture
CNNs solve this spatial recognition problem through three specialized layer types working in sequence: convolution, pooling, and fully connected layers.
Convolutional Layer (Explains the name of Convolutional Neural Network): The convolutional layer acts first, applying filters across the input image to detect visual details (Note: These are mathematical filters like a small matrix of numbers, typically 3x3 or 5x5 that detect visual patterns, not the Instagram-style filters that add dog ears to your selfie photos 😂). These filters slide across the image, transforming raw pixels into feature maps that capture spatial relationships. Each node/neuron in the convolutional layer applies an activation function such as ReLU to introduce non-linearity, ensuring that only significant features pass forward. Lutz Finger described filters as a “crutch” for the AI model to see in a spatial measure. The convolution operation enables the network to recognize that shifted versions of the same image contain identical patterns. In "Backpropagation Applied to Handwritten Zip Code Recognition," LeCun introduced the concept of feature maps where all units in a plane share the same set of weights, thereby detecting the same feature at different locations (LeCun et al., 1989).
Pooling Layer: The pooling layer follows, reducing the spatial dimensions of feature maps into abstract representations. With multiple filters generating expanded data, pooling compresses this information while preserving essential features. This reduction decreases computational "strain" and makes the abstract representations more robust to minor input variations. Lutz Finger noted that pooling (previously known as subsampling) serves as an engineering technique to make running the model more efficient. "Gradient-Based Learning Applied to Document Recognition" describes this as subsampling layers that reduce the resolution of feature maps while making the representation more robust to small distortions (LeCun et al., 1998).
The Fully Connected Layer: The fully connected layer completes the neural network model architecture by performing classification. This layer takes pooled features, flattens them into a single vector, and assigns probabilities to each possible output category. The familiar neural network structure from our previously-known basic neural network architectures will handle this final classification/prediction/inference stage.
Minimize image
Edit image
Delete image
CNN layers operate at progressively higher levels of abstraction, with lower layers detecting simpler patterns/structures while deeper layers recognize complex features. Early layers identify basic elements like edges and their orientations. As information flows deeper into the network, layers combine these primitives into increasingly sophisticated patterns.
The eCornell program illustrated this progression through image classification examples. Lower layers might detect horizontal and vertical edges. Middle layers combine these edges into shapes and textures. Deeper layers recognize complex features like facial characteristics, object parts, or scene elements. This hierarchical learning emerges automatically through training rather than explicit programming.
This automatic feature hierarchy distinguishes CNNs from traditional approaches requiring manual feature engineering. Rather than humans defining what constitutes an edge or a texture, the network discovers these representations through backpropagation and gradient descent. "Backpropagation Applied to Handwritten Zip Code Recognition" noted that some kernels synthesized by the network can be interpreted as feature detectors remarkably similar to those found in biological vision systems (LeCun et al., 1989). The optimization principles covered in my previous series directly enable this automatic feature learning.
Myriad Real-World CNN Applications for Computer Vision
CNNs power diverse visual recognition systems across industries, from consumer devices to industrial automation. Lutz Finger shared several practical applications during the eCornell program that demonstrate CNN versatility in production environments.
Smart infrastructure systems leverage CNNs for environmental optimization. Cisco deployed CNNs in smart room infrastructure to count faces and proactively adjust climate control based on occupancy. A Schneider Electric study quantifies the impact of occupancy-based controls in meeting rooms and energy conservation measures (ECMs) at central London offices, where meeting rooms saw a 22% reduction in operational energy use and carbon emissions during the study. This application demonstrates how computer vision enables automation that would be impractical through traditional programming approaches.
Security and quality control represent high-value CNN deployments. Security systems use CNNs to verify identities and detect unauthorized access. Quality inspection systems identify manufacturing defects in engines and products. The facial recognition that unlocks smartphones relies on CNN architectures optimized for mobile deployment. Other security and surveillance applications include anomaly detection of suspicious behavior in crowds, license plate recognition for toll collection and parking enforcement and perimeter monitoring of intruders in restricted or secure areas. The real-world applications extend far beyond these few use case examples.
Transportation and Autonomous Vehicles:
Self-driving cars: CNNs help detect pedestrians, lane markings, traffic signs, and other vehicles in real time (even though they can be compute-intensive for real-time inference)
Advanced driver assistance systems (ADAS): Lane departure warnings, automatic emergency braking, and blind spot detection
Traffic management: Monitoring congestion, detecting accidents, and optimizing signal timing
Parking assistance: Automated parallel parking and parking spot detection
Agriculture and Forestry:
Crop health monitoring: Drones with CNN-powered cameras detect disease, pest damage, and nutrient deficiencies in fields
Yield prediction: Analyzing aerial imagery to estimate harvest volumes
Forestry inventory: Counting trees, measuring canopy coverage, and detecting deforestation from satellite imagery
Weed detection: Identifying weeds among crops to enable precision herbicide application
Fruit picking robots: Detecting ripe produce for automated harvesting
Healthcare and Medical Imaging:
Radiology: Detecting tumors, fractures, and anomalies in X-rays, CT scans, and MRIs
Pathology: Analyzing tissue samples for cancer cells
Retinal scanning: Diagnosing diabetic retinopathy and macular degeneration
Dermatology: Identifying skin cancer from photographs
Surgical assistance: Real-time guidance during minimally invasive procedures
Retail and E-Commerce:
Visual search: Finding products by uploading photos (Google Lens, Pinterest, Amazon)
Cashier-less stores: In-store cameras use CNNs to track what shoppers pick up
Inventory management: Detecting empty shelves and misplaced products
Virtual try-on: Previewing glasses, makeup, or clothing on your image
Manufacturing and Quality Control:
Defect detection: Identifying scratches, cracks, and assembly errors on production lines
Predictive maintenance: Analyzing equipment images for signs of wear
Robotic guidance: Helping robots pick and place items accurately
Safety monitoring: Detecting workers without proper protective equipment
Environmental and Conservation:
Wildlife monitoring: Identifying and counting species from camera traps
Ocean health: Analyzing underwater imagery for coral bleaching and marine life populations
Wildfire detection: Spotting smoke plumes from satellite and drone imagery
Illegal logging detection: Monitoring forests for unauthorized clearing
Sports and Entertainment:
Player tracking: Analyzing athlete movements and formations
Automated highlights: Detecting goals, touchdowns, and key moments
Referee assistance: Goal-line technology and VAR in soccer
Audience analytics: Measuring crowd engagement and attendance
These applications share a common thread: processing grid-like spatial data where position and arrangement are stored as numerical values and local patterns matter. A single pixel means nothing in isolation, but neighboring pixels together form edges, textures, and shapes. Whether identifying a defect in a manufactured part or recognizing a face in a security feed, CNNs excel because they capture spatial relationships that basic neural networks cannot detect. The architecture matches the problem structure. "Backpropagation Applied to Handwritten Zip Code Recognition" demonstrated that a network trained on low-level pixel representations could achieve state of the art performance in digit recognition, a principle that scales to modern applications (LeCun et al., 1989).
Connecting Architecture to My v2 AI Industry New Enhancer Project
My v2 AI News Enhancer project used a transformer architecture rather than a CNN because natural text processing requires sequential understanding rather than spatial recognition. This architectural choice directly reflects the principles Lutz Finger taught about matching network design to data characteristics.
Text data flows sequentially with meaning determined by word order and context across sentences. A CNN designed for spatial patterns in images would miss these sequential dependencies. Transformers, which evolved from recurrent architectures, handle sequential data through attention mechanisms that capture long-range dependencies.
This contrast illustrates why architectural selection matters for personal/business/societal outcomes. Both CNNs and transformers build from the same LEGO block foundation of nodes and layers. Yet their configurations optimize for fundamentally different data structures. The personal objective of processing AI industry news demanded an architecture suited for sequential text, not spatial images.
Looking Ahead
Part 2 explores how CNNs create their own filters through backpropagation and why computer-generated filters consistently outperform human-designed alternatives (Reminder: As mentioned earlier, filters are mathematical construct using matrices). The eCornell program included hands-on exercises comparing sharpening filters, Gabor filters, and machine-learned filters on image classification tasks. These experiments revealed measurable performance differences that validate the power of learned feature extraction.
Understanding filter mechanics also introduces critical security considerations. When we understand how CNN filters activate specific neurons, we gain both debugging capability and awareness of potential vulnerabilities. Part 3 will examine this transparency-security tradeoff that becomes increasingly relevant as CNNs deploy in high-stakes applications.
The architectural journey continues through RNNs, LSTMs, and ultimately a comprehensive framework for matching network design to personal/business/societal requirements. Each architecture solves specific problems that others cannot address effectively. Building this complete picture enables informed decisions about when and how to deploy neural networks for maximum value.
This article is Part 1 of an 8-part series on neural network architectures and their uses from the "Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks" module of Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, instructed by Lutz Finger.

## Article #17

**Title:** Neural Network Architectures and Their AI Uses Part 2: CNN Filters, Human vs. Machine Feature Engineering

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/neural-network-architectures-ai-uses-part-2-cnn-filters-lim-774qc/

**Content:**

Editor’s Note
This article is Part 2 of an eight-part series documenting my learning reflections about neural network model architectures and their practical applications. I draw these reflections from fundamental learning in the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” program module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” certificate program, instructed by Lutz Finger. Disclaimer: Cornell University's Cornell Johnson School (Ranked #15 in the World and #6 in the United States on Financial Times 2026 Global MBA Ranking) is also my MBA alma mater.
I completed my locally-deployed v2 AI Industry News Enhancer project in late 2025, and the principles in this article apply directly to how I built it. Both my Llama language model and convolution neural networks (or convolution networks) are neural networks at their core albeit varying architectures. These neural networks learn by adjusting weights through backpropagation to abstract inputs into useful, latent representations. In my project, LoRA fine-tuning let the model learn its own feature representations from 460 hand-annotated examples, consistently outperforming the hand-crafted prompt engineering I started with. That is machine feature engineering in action. This same principle applies to neural network that processes images.
Project repository: v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production): GitHub | Hugging Face
Introduction
In September 2012, a convolutional neural network (or neural net) called AlexNet "shattered" the ImageNet image classification benchmark, outperforming the second-place system by nearly eleven percentage points.
What made this result so significant was not just the margin of victory. Every top-performing entry in the ImageNet challenge before AlexNet relied on hand-engineered feature extraction pipelines. These pipelines used decades of carefully designed algorithms like SIFT and HOG to extract edges, corners, and textures from images before feeding them into classifiers. Although convolutional neural networks had learned their own filters through backpropagation since LeCun’s work in 1989, none had succeeded at this scale of visual complexity & scale till AlexNet proved that the neural network approach for real-world image classification (Krizhevsky, Sutskever, and Hinton, 2012). Yann LeCun, one of the founders of deep learning, reportedly called it an unequivocal turning point in the history of computer vision at the presentation itself.
Alex Krizhevsky, Ilya Sutskever (also co-founder of OpenAI), and Geoffrey Hinton presented AlexNet at the 2012 Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) conference in their paper “ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks.” The network contained 60 million parameters and 650,000 neurons across five convolutional layers and three fully connected layers. On the ILSVRC-2010 test data, AlexNet achieved top-1 and top-5 error rates of 37.5% and 17.0%, considerably better than the previous state of the art. A variant entered the ILSVRC-2012 competition and achieved a winning top-5 test error rate of 15.3%, compared to 26.2% from the second best entry. Before AlexNet, most researchers considered deep neural networks impractical for large-scale image recognition. AlexNet is widely credited with igniting the deep learning revolution, marking a pivotal “ImageNet moment” that revitalized interest in neural networks after years of stagnation. IEEE Spectrum describes it as “the neural network that revolutionized AI.” The original paper has accumulated over 190,608 citations as of February 2025.
As a recap from my previous Part 1 article, Yann LeCun had pioneered convolutional neural networks at AT&T Bell Laboratories in 1989 (featuring a cool, VHS-restored YouTube video of his 1989 demo). LeCun's LeNet architecture had already proven that neural networks could learn/build/engineer their own filters through backpropagation. By the mid-1990s, LeCun’s technology was reading more than 10% of all checks (or cheques ) deposited in the United States. But for more than two decades, CNNs could not consistently outperform other machine learning approaches on complex, large-scale image tasks.
IEEE Spectrum summarized the THREE (3) industry developments from outside AI research that converged to materialize a significant innovation. I encourage drawing the historical parallels with existing or ongoing industry changes as of February 2025. The explains my earlier emphasis on a broad learning approach to "learn just enough" of other domains (such as history, economics, digital media studies, information theory, etc.) amidst having a specialization to address myopia or biases.
1/ The inception of a massive new image dataset: Beginning in 2006, Stanford professor Fei-Fei Li assembled ImageNet, a collection of over 15 million labeled images across more than 22,000 categories, with classification crowdsourced through Amazon Mechanical Turk. Completed in 2009, ImageNet was larger than any previous image dataset by several orders of magnitude. Li launched the annual ImageNet Large Scale Visual Recognition Challenge in 2010 to spur progress, but over the first two years, the best-performing systems relied on traditional hand-engineered feature pipelines and made only marginal improvements.
2/ Improving economics of access to parallel computation: Training neural networks requires massive volume of repeated matrix multiplication operations performed in parallel. Graphics processing units (GPUs) are purpose-built for exactly this workload. In 2007, Nvidia (Co-founded in 2000s) released the CUDA programming platform that extends GPU usage beyond 3D graphics for other programmable, general-purpose applications that also involve matrix multiplication operations. The access to computation power via GPUs enable researchers to run deep neural networks that require massive computation power compared to less sophisticated AI models.
3/ Development of a suitable and specific neural network architecture: In 2011, a preceding, milestone-convolutional neural network called DanNet, was trained on GPUs created by Dan Cireşan and others at Jürgen Schmidhuber’s lab in Switzerland, won 4 image recognition contests. However, it was trained on smaller dataset and was scalable sufficiently for computer vision at that time. The subsequent 2012 AlexNet utilized the elements of large image datasets and GPU access together. Krizhevsky wrote custom CUDA code to split the neural network across two Nvidia GPUs, training the model on a desktop computer in his bedroom at his parents’ house. AlexNet neural network architecture itself introduced several techniques that are now standard industry practice:
ReLU activation function for faster training (Introduced in my learning reflections in previous article)
Dropout regularization to prevent overfitting
Data preprocessing or augmentation to artificially expand the image training dataset
These techniques, combined with ImageNet’s scale and GPU parallelism, enabled a complex convolutional network with 60 million parameters and eight learned layers to be trained effectively on a complex, real-world image classification task for the first time.
In hindsight, AlexNet's impact was immediate and field-defining at time of its presentation. As Hansen Hsu of the Computer History Museum observed in IEEE Spectrum, leading computer vision research went from virtually ignoring "expensive" neural networks to a resounding AlexNet’s victory (Hsu, 2025). AlexNet was not a new invention but a successful, scalable re-introduction of the convolution network architecture. AlexNet also proved that learned hierarchical features were superior to the hand-crafted features like SIFT and HOG that had dominated computer vision for decades. Within next few years, top-performing ImageNet entries saw the use of deep neural networks. AlexNet's 2012 victory in the ImageNet challenge had shifted the trajectory of an entire research field and elevated research interest in deep learning that continues to shape industries decades later.
If you are interested to tinker with the original version of the 2012 AlexNet, the Computer History Museum (CHM), in partnership with Google, is presenting the source code on CHM’s GitHub page. Apparently, there are varying re-creations based on the famous “ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks" paper, but they are not the original code.
That result raised a fundamental question: Why do computer-learned filters outperform human-designed ones? Part 1 of this series re-introduced Lutz Finger's instruction analogy of filters as a “crutch” that helps CNNs understand spatial relationships in images. This article examines who should design those filters: Humans drawing on decades of image processing expertise, or computers optimizing through backpropagation. The answer reveals principles that extends far beyond looking at the mere technology of image classification tasks.
The eCornell program addressed this question through hands-on experimentation, and the broader research literature provides converging evidence. When optimizing for a specific task, learned representations consistently outperform hand-crafted features. This principle applies whether we are training CNN filters for digit recognition, fine-tuning language models for news analysis, or building diagnostic systems for medical imaging.
How Filters Transform Images
To recap understand how a CNN sees, we must first view an image as a computer does which is a matrix of numbers. For a black-and-white photo, this is a single grid where each number represents pixel brightness. For a color photo, the computer sees a stack of three matrices—one each for Red, Green, and Blue.
A filter (or kernel), which is itself a tiny matrix (or matrices in plural) of numbers, moves across these grids one step at a time. As an analogy (or lack of a better one), this "sliding window operation" acts like a "systematic digital scanner". At every position, the "sliding" filter performs the mathematical operation for a dot product by multiplying its own values by the corresponding image pixel values (where it is currently hovering over) to sum into a single output value. This process creates a feature map, which is a new matrix where each point represents the activations or how strongly the filter reacted to a specific pattern image pixel values. For example, the pattern could be an edge or a texture at that exact location in the image. High values in the map indicate that the filter found what it was looking for (like a vertical edge or a specific texture) at that exact location. By using dozens of different filters simultaneously, the network builds a multi-layered understanding of the image, ranging from simple lines to complex shapes.
Multiple filters generate multiple feature maps, each capturing different aspects of the input image (e.g., colors, shapes, edges, textures or the gamut). A CNN might use dozens or even hundreds of filters in a single convolutional layer. Together, these feature maps provide rich spatial information that the subsequent neural network layers use for classification. The question becomes: Who should design these filter matrices?
Human-Designed Filters: Decades of Image Processing Research
Through history, humans have developed specialized filters for specific visual tasks, each optimized based on decades of image processing research. These filters work by enhancing certain image characteristics that humans consider important for visual perception. Through the eCornell program, Lutz Finger introduced and taught several classic human-designed filters to establish a baseline for comparison.
1/ The Sharpening Filter:
The sharpening filter enhances edges by increasing contrast between adjacent pixels. This filter uses a specific matrix configuration that amplifies the current pixel while subtracting neighboring pixel contributions. The result appears “sharper” to human observers because edges become more distinct. Applications like photo editing software commonly use sharpening filters to improve image clarity.
Minimize image
Edit image
Delete image
Real World Application(s): Sharpening filters are embedded in technologies from our daily lives.
Every smartphone camera applies sharpening during photo processing. When an iPhone or Pixel captures an image, the computational photography pipeline uses sharpening filters to enhance edges before the photo reaches the camera roll. Photo editing apps like Lightroom and Snapseed expose this directly as a “Sharpen” slider.
In medical imaging, CT scanners apply sharpening kernels to make tissue boundaries more visible to radiologists, helping distinguish subtle structures in diagnostic scans.
2/ The Gabor Filter
The Gabor filter responds maximally to textures (primary use case) and edges at specific orientations. Dennis Gabor, who won the Nobel Prize in Physics for inventing holography, gave this filter its namesake. The Gabor filter captures both frequency and orientation information. Gabor filters are particularly useful for texture analysis because they can detect repeating patterns at different angles.
Neuroscience directly informed the design of Gabor filters. In 1962, David Hubel and Torsten Wiesel inserted microelectrodes into the visual cortex (brain) of cats and discovered neurons they called “simple cells” that fire their synapses selectively in response to edges at specific orientations. Other neurons, termed “complex cells,” respond to edges regardless of their exact position within the visual field. This hierarchical processing, from orientation-selective simple cells to position-invariant complex cells, earned Hubel and Wiesel the 1981 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine.
Researchers designed Gabor filters to mathematically model these biological simple cells. They found that Gabor filters produce responses remarkably similar to those found in biological vision systems. Part 1 of this series noted that “kernels synthesized by the network can be interpreted as feature detectors remarkably similar to those found in biological vision systems” (citing LeCun, 1989). The reason for this similarity traces directly to Hubel and Wiesel’s discovery that biological vision processes edges and orientations as its fundamental building blocks.
Minimize image
Edit image
Delete image
Real World Application(s): Today, Gabor filters power the biometric systems many readers interact with regularly.
When you unlock a phone with a fingerprint sensor or pass through an airport biometric scanner, Gabor filters are working behind the scenes to enhance the ridge-and-valley patterns in the fingerprint image before matching.
Because Gabor filters respond to image edges at specific orientations, they are a standard component in fingerprint recognition pipelines. The same orientation-selective property that models biological simple cells makes them ideal for capturing the directional ridge structure of a human fingerprint.
3/ The Sobel Filter
Getting an "edge" (No pun intended): Sobel filter specializes in edge detection by computing intensity gradients. Unlike sharpening, which enhances all edges uniformly, Sobel filters detect horizontal and vertical edges separately. The Sobel transformation fundamentally changes how images appear while preserving structural information about where edges occur.
Minimize image
Edit image
Delete image
Real World Application(s): Sobel filters contribute to safety systems in modern vehicles.
In advanced driver-assistance systems and autonomous vehicle prototypes, Sobel filters detect lane boundaries and road edges by computing intensity gradients across camera frames, identifying where bright pavement meets dark lane markings.
The same principle applies in manufacturing quality control or defects monitoring, where Sobel-based edge detection identifies visible cracks, seams, and surface defects on parts moving along production lines.
The Fundamental Limitations of Human-Designed Filters
Human-designed filters may optimize for subjective visual improvement (of the AI system designer) rather than the objective function of the specific image classification task. This fundamental mismatch limits their effectiveness for machine learning applications.
A sharpening filter makes images look better to humans, but looking better to humans does not necessarily mean easier to classify by AI models or algorithms. A Gabor filter detects textures, but texture detection might be irrelevant or even counterproductive for a specific classification task. Human intuition about what features matter can fail to align with the objective function of the image classification task.
The eCornell program reinforced this point by noting that humans optimize these filters for a subjective sense of improvement. Phrases like “The image is now sharper” describe human perception and intuition, not classification task completion. The filter that makes an image look best to a human eye may not necessarily the filter that makes the image easiest for a neural network to classify correctly.
Computer-Defined Filters Through Backpropagation
Convolution neural networks (CNNs) create their own matrix-based filters through backpropagation, optimizing specifically for the classification task rather than human aesthetic preferences. This is essentially automated feature engineering. Instead of a human engineer manually defining which textures or shapes are important, the CNN treats filter values as learnable parameters. There are like the weights in a fully connected layer. The neural network discovers what features matter through training rather than relying on human assumptions.
The network starts with randomly initialized filter values and refines them through the same optimization process covered in my previous series on neural network training. During the forward pass, filters transform input images into feature maps and the network makes predictions. During the backward pass, gradients flow back through the network, adjusting both the fully connected layer weights and the filter values to reduce classification errors.
The resulting filters may not be interpretable to humans, but they perform better at the actual task. When examining computer-generated filters, you cannot directly understand why the network defined each filter the way it did. The filters exist to minimize classification errors, not to produce outputs that make sense to human observers. This trade-off between interpretability and performance appears throughout machine learning.
What Learned Convolutional Filters Actually "See"
For years after CNNs demonstrated superior performance, researchers had limited understanding of what the learned filters were actually detecting. In 2014, Matthew Zeiler and Rob Fergus developed a visualization technique that addresses this limitation. By using deconvolutional networks to project filter activations back to pixel space, they provided the first "clear" window into the internal or latent representations that CNNs learn (Zeiler and Fergus, 2014).
Their findings revealed a striking hierarchy of learned features. The first convolutional layer learns to detect edges and color gradients. These features are remarkably similar to the hand-designed Gabor and Sobel filters discussed above. The second layer combines these primitives to detect corners, contours, and simple textures. By the third and fourth layers, the network has assembled these building blocks into detectors for object parts: eyes, noses, wheels, and other recognizable components. The deepest layers respond to near-complete objects.
This progression reveals both convergence and divergence with biological systems & human-designed approaches. At the earliest layers, the CNN independently rediscovers the same orientation-selective features that Hubel and Wiesel found in biological vision and that human engineers captured with Gabor filters. This convergence suggests that these basic feature types are not just a human design choice but a fundamental property of how visual information is most efficiently processed. Both biological and artificial systems, optimizing through entirely different mechanisms, arrive at similar solutions. Recent research continues to explore this brain-CNN parallel, including work presented at ICLR 2025 that demonstrated improved CNN accuracy by reshaping filters to more closely mimic the brain’s visual processing patterns.
However, at deeper layers, the learned features diverge dramatically from anything a human would design. These higher-layer features exaggerate or "recognize" finely-nuanced patterns or discriminative parts of images. For example, these features will emphasize on the eyes and noses when classifying animals. For other computer vision use case, the features at deeper layers may refer to or highlight very specific stroke patterns when distinguishing/classifying handwritten digits.
No human engineer, no matter how expert, would recognize these precise, finely-nuanced (and sometimes even odd or "non-obvious") feature combinations. They emerge because backpropagation discovers statistical associations or fine patterns in the training data that are invisible or "non-recognizable" to human perception but critical for accurate or precise classification.
This is representation learning or machine feature engineering in action. Rather than prescribing what features to extract, the artificial neural network model's deeper layers learns representations optimized for the task. Zeiler and Fergus demonstrated that features learned on ImageNet transferred successfully to entirely different datasets, confirming that learned representations capture genuinely useful structure in visual data rather than merely memorizing training examples.
The eCornell Program Exercise: Comparing Filter Approaches
The eCornell program included hands-on exercises comparing human-defined and computer-defined filters on image classification tasks. This experimental approach allowed students to observe performance differences firsthand, providing empirical confirmation of the principles established in the research literature.
These exercises aimed to demonstrate three key principles through progressive experimentation: Establishing a baseline neural network performance without any filters, evaluating whether human-designed filters improve classification accuracy, and comparing the results against CNN-learned filters applied to the same task.
The exercises demonstrated that each step toward computer-defined features improved classification performance. Starting from a baseline neural network without filters, adding human-designed filters produced modest improvements. Replacing human filters with computer-learned filters produced additional gains. The full CNN architecture achieved the highest performance. These results validated a core principle: task-specific optimization outperforms general-purpose human design.
Practical Applications of Deep Learning via Neural Networks
These findings translate directly to production deployment decisions across multiple domains.
Feature engineering strategy: When building image classification systems, prioritize learned features over hand-crafted alternatives unless interpretability requirements (e.g., compliance, audit, legal regulations) demand otherwise.
Resource allocation: Invest engineering time in curating training data and architecture design rather than manual filter development.
Real-world impact: CNN-based systems with learned features now power medical imaging diagnostics where learned feature hierarchies detect patterns in radiology scans, orthodontic images, pathology slides, and retinal images. In some specific medical cases, these systems match or exceed the diagnostic accuracy of human specialists. These cases are direct consequences of learned features capturing subtle visual patterns that hand-crafted feature extraction misses. That said, the assistive combination of machine and human provide best ideal performance as either entity covers for each other's limitations.
Performance expectations: Expect measurable improvements from learned filters when other model components remain constant. The gains compound as imatask complexity increases.
High-Level Summary: Why Computer Filters Outperform Human Filters
My learning reflections in preceding sections discussed that human-designed filters optimize for subjective improvement rather than classification accuracy. Learned filters can discover filters or features no human would design.
One additional mechanism explains why the advantage is so consistent: Joint optimization with downstream layers creates synergy - specifically between the machine-engineered filters and machine-engineered weights. When filters and classification weights optimize together through backpropagation, they develop complementary representations. A filter might produce outputs that seem highly arbitrary in isolation but perfectly match what the classification layers expect. Human-designed filters cannot achieve this coordination because humans design filters in isolation from the classification layers that consume their outputs. Moreover, the myriad associations across filters and weights involve computations far too complex for any human to perform, even in principle.
Key Conclusion
The evidence for learned features outperforming hand-crafted features now spans more than six decades of converging research. Hubel and Wiesel (1962) discovered that biological vision processes visual information through a hierarchy of increasingly complex feature detectors. LeCun (1989) built the first CNN inspired by these principles. Krizhevsky, Sutskever, and Hinton (2012) proved that learned features beat hand-crafted ones at massive scale. Zeiler and Fergus (2014) showed us exactly what those learned features look like. The eCornell program provided hands-on confirmation through controlled experimentation.
The principle that learned representations outperform hand-crafted features is now one of the most validated findings in machine learning. It holds for image classification, speech recognition, natural language processing, and an expanding range of domains. Recent research recognized at NeurIPS 2025 demonstrated that representation learning is a central driver of neural scaling laws. The reason larger models perform better is fundamentally tied to having more capacity to learn effective feature representations.
This conclusion carries implications beyond any single application. Human expertise remains essential for architecture design, training data curation, and evaluation criteria. But for the actual feature transformations that convert raw inputs into useful representations, optimization consistently outperforms intuition. As the eCornell program demonstrated through its progressive comparison exercises, letting the computer define its own filters is not just marginally better. It reflects a fundamental advantage of task-specific optimization over general-purpose human design.
Strategic Implications: Where Value Migrates When Machines Design the Features
When a neural network can derive superior feature maps through backpropagation, the scarcity in the value chain shifts. The technical shift from human-designed heuristics (human expert-encoded rules or shortcuts) to machine-learned features carries strategic consequences that extend well beyond computer vision. Drawing on my learning reflections from my two-year MBA program at the Cornell Johnson School, I attempt to draw three critical business implications from recent, real-world industry shifts.
1. Data as the Enabling Complement
In the Managing Complements framework, an “enabling complement” is a distinct asset required for a focal product to function or deliver value. In the era of hand-designed features, human expertise served as the bottleneck. Today, because neural network architectures like CNNs and Transformers grow increasingly standardized and commoditized, the “recipe” for intelligence may become "cheaper" given the exact same specific architecture and techniques. Value consequently migrates to input from which the network learns its filters: Training data (e.g., quality, volume and etc). Even as value migrates to training data, frontier AI labs continue pouring CAPEX into architectural innovations and new mathematical techniques, resulting in an ongoing R&D race to maximize value from that data advantage.
Google DeepMind’s recent work illustrates this shift. AlphaGenome (June 2025) does not rely on biologists manually encoding rules for DNA sequences. Instead, it “reads” the DNA genome to predict variant effects, effectively learning biological feature maps that human scientists had not yet codified. Similarly, GenCast (December 2024) outperforms traditional weather simulation models, which rely on human-derived physics equations, by learning weather patterns directly from decades of historical data. In both cases, the competitive advantage lies not in the model (or algorithm), which can be replicated, but in the proprietary data complement that allows the algorithm to learn unique features.
2. X-Efficiency and the Bitter Lesson of Scale
The economic concept of X-Efficiency refers to maximizing output from a given set of inputs by minimizing internal organizational slack or inefficiency. Human-designed filters represent a form of technical inefficiency because they impose a ceiling on performance based on human bias. The industry is currently validating the “Bitter Lesson,” a March 2019 concept popularized by Rich Sutton: On average over the long run, methods that leverage computation to learn features consistently outperform methods that rely on human design.
Recent developments demonstrate this principle at scale. Google DeepMind’s AlphaEvolve (May 2025) demonstrates the Bitter Lesson very well. Rather than simply learning parameters, AlphaEvolve iteratively generates and improves complete algorithms, optimizing code for data center efficiency and chip design more effectively than human engineers could. Meanwhile, DeepSeek R1 (January 2025) illustrates X-Efficiency in action. It utilizes reinforcement learning to optimize reasoning paths, essentially learning its own cognitive filters rather than relying solely on human-curated supervision. Amidst alleged claims of distilling from the input-output pairs from capable United States models and advanced GPU access, DeepSeek had claimed to achieve frontier-level performance at a fraction of the training cost of its United States competitors. Firms that persist in hand-crafting logic or features face a severe cost disadvantage against competitors who build systems that learn the logic automatically.
3. The Black Box Risk in Vertical Competition
While learned filters create immense value through performance, they introduce a strategic vulnerability in vertical competition that is specific to the bargaining power between a firm and its buyers (or regulators). Because the machine designs the filters, the resulting logic is often opaque, a “black box.” In high-stakes industries like healthcare or finance, the challenge of interpretability becomes a purchase barrier to Product-Need Fit, preventing buyers from trusting the product.
Two examples highlight this tension between value creation and value capture. Consider a medical AI model that diagnoses cancer but cannot explain why. Powerful buyers like the FDA and hospital systems will refuse to adopt it, regardless of performance. The buyer’s Willingness-to-Pay may decline when buyers cannot fully verify output quality. Separately, Anthropic’s research into alignment faking (December 2024) demonstrated that models can deceptively comply with training while still maintaining their original preferences, introducing a Principal-Agent or misalignment problem with the buyer. In the Vertical Competition for regulatory approval and enterprise trust, companies that can “open the black box” by mapping learned features back to human-understandable concepts will command higher bargaining power than those offering opaque systems.
Frontier AI labs are already responding to this challenge.
Anthropic’s Claude for Financial Services (October 2025) provides audit trails and cell-level citations in Excel, letting analysts verify exactly what data informed each output.
Morgan Stanley’s partnership with OpenAI (March 2023) demonstrates this approach at scale: Its AI @ Morgan Stanley Assistant achieved 98% adoption among financial advisor teams by grounding responses exclusively in Morgan Stanley’s proprietary research and intellectual capital, with an evaluation (eval) framework that tests every AI use case before deployment.
Google Research's MedGemma (May 2025) takes a structurally different approach: Rather than building proprietary interpretability into a closed system, Google open-sourced its medical AI foundation models through its Health AI Developer Foundations program, with millions of downloads and hundreds of community-built variants on Hugging Face. The bet is that open-source access lets the broader research and developer community build interpretability solutions collaboratively. This is an approach to the black box problem through ecosystem rather than enterprise.
More recently, Goldman Sachs has embedded Anthropic engineers (February 2026) to co-develop autonomous Claude-based agents for trade accounting and client onboarding complex, rules-based functions where the AI must parse large volumes of data against regulatory requirements while maintaining audit trails.
In healthcare, Anthropic’s Claude for Healthcare (January 2026) connects to CMS coverage databases, ICD-10 codes, and the National Provider Identifier Registry through HIPAA-ready enterprise products, enabling clinicians to trace AI-generated determinations back to authoritative sources.
OpenAI for Healthcare (January 2026) takes a similar approach, offering a GPT-5-powered workspace with evidence retrieval and transparent citations from peer-reviewed studies, already rolling out to institutions including Cedars-Sinai, Stanford Medicine, and Memorial Sloan Kettering.
On the research frontier, Anthropic ’s work on persona vectors (August 2025) demonstrated that personality traits in language models follow predictable mathematical patterns—a step toward understanding internal representations at the mechanistic level, much as Zeiler and Fergus visualized what CNN filters “see.”
These workflow-level solutions represent interpretability at the decision level, even when internal feature representations remain opaque. The obvious solution is full model transparency. But as I will recap my learning reflections in the next article, determining exactly which neuron gets activated for which feature introduces new, darker risks for individuals, businesses and societies.
Connecting to My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer Project
My Llama-based language model and the CNNs explored in this article are both neural networks, and the lesson about machine feature engineering applies identically to both. In a CNN, backpropagation adjusts filter weights to learn image features optimized for classification. In my v2 project, LoRA fine-tuning adjusts a small set of adapter weights so the Llama model learns text features optimized for news analysis. Before fine-tuning, I relied on prompt engineering, the language model equivalent of hand-designed filters, crafting instructions based on my intuition about what the model needed. After fine-tuning, the neural network discovered its own feature representations directly from training examples, and output quality improved dramatically.
The parallel reinforces this article’s central argument: Machine feature engineering outperforms human feature engineering for specific tasks. Whether a neural network is learning edge detectors for images or learning how to structure a news summary, optimization through training data beats hand-crafted rules. Human expertise remains essential for architecture design, training data curation, and evaluation. For the actual feature transformations that convert raw inputs into useful representations, letting the network learn is consistently superior.
Looking Ahead
My next (shorter) article will refresh my learning reflections from the eCornell program to examine what happens when we understand how CNN filters work. The same transparency that enables debugging and improvement also creates potential vulnerabilities. When researchers understand which filter patterns activate specific neurons, they can craft inputs that fool networks while appearing normal to humans.
The field of adversarial machine learning has emerged from this dual nature of interpretability. Adversarial examples are inputs deliberately designed to cause misclassification, with implications for safety-critical systems like autonomous vehicles and medical imaging. I will explore both the attack vectors and the ongoing research into robust defenses, drawing on the eCornell program teachings about balancing transparency with the risk of exploitation & recent industry examples.
Beyond this foundational series around basic/standard neural network architectures and their uses, a future series will explore how these principles extend to newer architectures like Vision Transformers (ViTs) and multimodal techniques like Visual Question Answering (VQA). VQA augments language models with visual information in much the same way that Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) augments them with text documents.
This article is Part 2 of an 8-part series on neural network architectures and their uses from the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” program, instructed by Lutz Finger. #Gratitude

- Word Count: 5276
- Status: Active
