## Article #18

**Title:** Neural Network Architectures and Their AI Uses Part 3: Adversarial Attacks and the Interpretability Tradeoff

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/neural-network-architectures-ai-uses-part-3-adversarial-lim-8lkpc/

**Content:**

Editor's Note
This article is Part 3 of an eight-part series documenting my learning reflections about neural network model architectures and their practical applications. These reflections are drawn from fundamental learning from 1/ my Cornell Johnson MBA residence week elective and 2/ my five-month journey through Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" certificate program (Instructed by Lutz Finger).
Earlier, I had promised that this would be a “shorter” learning reflection edition as part of this neural network architecture series. But reality proved otherwise. There is a significant amount of valuable research, recent company announcements and industry news on this topic of neural network interpretability and adversarial machine learning. This led to my "executive" decision to hold off publication and go through this information trove. In fact, I could not even cover all the news incidents to avoid over-extending this edition.
What I Have Been Up To
I recently experimented with Anthropic’s Claude Code terminal interface to direct a comprehensive UI overhaul and SaaS productization of my hobbyist TalentSol applicant tracking system application. Claude Code has emerged as one of the most discussed AI development tools in the industry. I wanted to evaluate its capabilities firsthand through a substantive, multi-phase project rather than isolated prompts. For full project context, including the original frontend build with Augment Code Agent, see my earlier articles in this series.
My role throughout the user interface (UI) overhaul was product owner, context engineer and system designer, with Claude Code serving as pair programmer. Before any code generation began, I defined the overhaul objectives, UI guidelines, product requirements, architectural selection/preferences, and system design fundamentals. I maintained persistent project guidelines that served as behavioral scaffolding for Claude Code across sessions. The UI overhaul and rebuild of dependencies spanned 125 tasks across 24 phases, completed in three days. The depth of technical preparation, from defining the system architecture and data schema upfront to maintaining structured guidelines across sessions, compressed what would otherwise require significantly longer development cycles. I completed this work on Anthropic’s Claude Pro subscription tier, which imposes daily rate limits on usage. Anthropic’s Claude Max tier would reduce these constraints for practitioners who require higher throughput.
The overhaul followed a structured but adaptive workflow:
UI and frontend implementation: I directed and reviewed all interface changes that Claude Code generated against my architectural vision and product requirements.
Backlog documentation: After each phase, I updated a dedicated overhaul document with full details of completed tasks to maintain a traceable record of progress.
Quality assurance and context management: Between each major phase, I ran multiple test types, updated error handling, refined the project guidelines as harnesses, and compacted context to maintain Claude Code’s coherence for the next phase.
Replanning: I built a new plan for each subsequent phase based on test results and remaining objectives.
Unified data schema with synthetic data generation: I directed the data layer redesign with seeded synthetic data to ensure reproducibility across development environments.
Business logic, functions, webhooks and other dependencies: I reviewed and approved updates to application business logic, backend functions, and webhook handlers that Claude Code proposed against my system design.
Final refactoring: I directed the cleanup pass to remove redundant scripts and consolidate the codebase.
Even though I am Agile Scrum certified, my workflow did not follow traditional Waterfall or Agile Scrum methodologies. The speed and flexibility of working with Claude Code enabled a tighter iteration loop than conventional sprint cycles would permit. Each phase concluded with a quality gate before replanning, creating a pragmatic cycle of plan, implement, test, and replan that adapted organically to the project’s needs.
The TalentSol application now integrates the best-performing XGBoost model from my prior model building projects (16+ models) into a MVP production-ready interface with a comprehensive machine learning data pipeline. The adversarial robustness considerations explored in this article apply directly to any production AI system, including applicant tracking system applications that process external candidate data from sources the organization does not control.
The technical implementation and XGBoost tree model implementation guide are available here:
TalentSol Applicant Tracking System Application: GitHub
TalentSol Supervised Classifier for Initial Candidate Screening with Decision Trees: GitHub
TalentSol Supervised Classifier for Initial Candidate Screening with Regression: GitHub
Introduction
My previous Part 1 learning reflection article re-examined how CNNs learn to detect spatial features through hierarchical layers, and Part 2 article reflected on the "Bitter Lesson" as to why computer-generated filters outperform human-designed alternatives over the long-run. Immediately after AlexNet's 2012 ImageNet victory covered in Part 2 , researchers sought to understand what these neural networks had actually learned. I do not know why the industry kept terms the activations as non deterministic. Matthew Zeiler and Rob Fergus published their foundational paper "Visualizing and Understanding Convolutional Networks" (Nov 28, 2013). Their work introduced a novel Deconvolutional Network technique to project feature activations back to input pixel space, revealing the function of intermediate layers. This effort to peer inside CNN architectures marks the beginning of a research domain that relates to today's interpretability challenges. While I initially planned a shorter treatment focused on CNN-specific vulnerabilities, the eCornell program's teaching on adversarial machine learning connected to recent developments in interpretability research and industry incidents that warranted a more comprehensive examination.
Previous articles in my learning reflection series examined interpretability as a requirement for trust, compliance, and competitive advantage. This article examines the flipside of the coin: When interpretability itself becomes an attack surface. The eCornell program dedicated an entire module to balancing AI transparency with the risk of exploitation. I shall re-examine how the same knowledge that helps engineers debug and improve neural networks can also enable adversaries or malicious agents to manipulate model outputs. This tension sits at the heart of a growing but still emerging domain of  - Adversarial Machine Learning - which studies both attacks on and defenses of machine learning systems (including deep learning or neural networks).
The urgency of this challenge is accelerating. Dario Amodei, CEO and co-founder of Anthropic, argues in his blog post "The Urgency of Interpretability" (April 2025) that understanding the inner workings of advanced AI systems is now an urgent priority. Amodei frames the challenge as a race: Interpretability research must keep pace with AI capability development, or we risk deploying systems too powerful for anyone to understand. His post traces the full history of mechanistic interpretability, from early neuron-level observations to modern large-scale feature extraction and circuit tracing.
Interpretability serves not only safety & compliance purposes but also scientific discovery and industries. As Amodei argues in "The Urgency of Interpretability," interpretability "could help scientists actually learn from the AI, not just use its outputs." Zeiler and Fergus demonstrated this scientific value in "Visualizing and Understanding Convolutional Networks" (Nov 28, 2013) when they used their visualization technique to diagnose architectural problems in AlexNet-variant CNNs and improve model design. Their improvements led to an architecture that won the ImageNet Large Scale Visual Recognition Challenge that year. However, the same scientific curiosity that drives beneficial discovery also drove knowledge that adversaries can exploit.
The interpretability-robustness tradeoff presents a fundamental challenge for deploying neural networks such as CNNs in production environments. More interpretable models allow engineers to understand and improve system behavior. However, that same interpretability can reveal internal mechanisms that adversaries exploit to craft malicious inputs. As neural networks like CNNs get deployed in high-stakes, safety-critical applications from medical imaging to autonomous vehicles, understanding this tradeoff becomes essential for responsible deployment.
It is worth noting that this reward/risk tradeoff is not the only perspective in the research community. In the paper "Stop Explaining Black Box Machine Learning Models for High Stakes Decisions and Use Interpretable Models Instead" (Nov 5, 2019), Cynthia Rudin of Duke University argued forcefully that organizations should use inherently interpretable models rather than attempting to explain black boxes after the fact. Rudin contends that "it is a myth that there is necessarily a trade-off between accuracy and interpretability". Her position suggests a third path: Rather than choosing between transparency and security, we can intentionally design models whose decision logic is transparent by construction. Rudin demonstrates through examples in criminal justice and healthcare that interpretable models frequently match black box performance when practitioners invest adequate effort in their system designs. While this approach does not eliminate adversarial risk, it reframes the problem in ways worth considering for high-stakes deployments.
How Neural Networks Seek Patterns
Trained neural networks actively seek patterns they learned during training, even when presented with completely irrelevant inputs. The eCornell program had illustrated this behavior through a fascinating experiment that demonstrates the fundamental nature of how neural networks process information.
Using the example of CNNs, researchers presented a neural network, which was trained to classify animal images, with a photograph of a blue sky containing only clouds. Rather than reporting that no animals were present, the neural network attempted to identify animal features within the random cloud formations. The results showed the neural network interprets cloud shapes as various animals. Similarly, children and adults alike look at clouds and imagine familiar shapes, finding spurious patterns where none were intentionally placed or existed. This behavior mirrors the well-documented human instinct of pareidolia that perceive familiar shapes in random objects or stimuli. This instinct provided a survival advantage for our evolutionary ancestors, who needed to detect predators in their environments.
A cloud formation resembling a living creature, illustrating human pareidolia. Image Credit: "Drizzle," SpongeBob Squarepants
This pattern-seeking behavior reveals something fundamental about neural network operation. The neural network does not understand what it is looking at in any meaningful sense. It simply learns to identify pattern from training data and inputs to make a prediction/inference. The eCornell program used the analogy of a shape-sorting toy where specific neurons wait for matching inputs to trigger their probabilistic activations. When matching patterns appear, the neuron activates and signals detection. When they do not appear, the neural network attempts to find the closest match anyway, producing confident classifications of patterns that humans would recognize as meaningless.
As discussed earlier, Zeiler and Fergus systematically revealed what CNN layers actually learn at each depth in their research in "Visualizing and Understanding Convolutional Networks". This provides a form of anecdotal interpretability that is specific to the CNN neural network architecture and its layers. Their visualization technique showed that Layers 1 and 2 learn edges and corners, Layer 3 learns texture patterns, and Layers 4 and 5 learn object parts and whole objects. This hierarchical feature structure confirmed that CNNs build increasingly abstract representations as information flows through successive layers. The same layer-by-layer understanding that helped Zeiler and Fergus improve CNN architecture could theoretically help an adversary understand which features to target at each network depth. Hooker et al. referenced their work in the NeurIPS 2019 paper "A Benchmark for Interpretability Methods in Deep Neural Networks" (Sep 22, 2019), confirming its foundational role in the interpretability research landscape.
Modern mechanistic interpretability research extends the eCornell teaching from observing that neurons activate to understanding how activation cascades through internal circuits. Anthropic 's research paper "Tracing the Thoughts of a Large Language Model" (Mar 27, 2025) extracted neural circuits that Claude uses, enabling researchers to trace how specific inputs activate particular internal pathways. Anthropic describes their findings as "AI biology," suggesting that understanding neural network internals is becoming a systematic scientific discipline analogous to biology-related domains. Just as biologists observe organisms in their natural behavior, interpretability researchers now observe how neural circuits process information in their "natural" computational behavior. This granular circuit-level understanding extends the same principles the eCornell program taught about neuron activation functions, bridging from CNN feature visualization in 2013 to large language model (LLM) circuit tracing in 2025.
This inherent pattern-seeking behavior creates both value and vulnerability. The value comes from neural networks (e.g., CNNs, LLMs) detecting subtle patterns that humans miss entirely, enabling applications like early cancer detection in medical imaging or text-to-text prediction from large corpus textual knowledge. The vulnerability comes from the fact that if we understand what patterns trigger specific neurons, we can deliberately craft inputs that trigger false (or harmful) activations of specific neurons. The eCornell program emphasized that this duality – a technological metaphor for the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil – requires careful consideration when deploying neural network systems in production.
Adversarial Examples in Computer Vision
Adversarial examples are inputs specifically crafted to cause neural network misclassification while appearing normal to human observers. This accurate industry term describes one of the most significant challenges in deploying neural network systems. The eCornell program presented research demonstrating that images appearing as random noise to humans were classified with extremely high confidence as specific objects by neural networks.
The mechanism behind adversarial examples exploits knowledge of neuron activation patterns within CNN layers. The eCornell program framed this vulnerability as a form of injection, drawing on the principle that knowledge of internal model representations enables targeted manipulation of outputs. Adversaries who understand the internal feature hierarchy of a trained neural network can construct inputs designed to activate specific neurons and force intended classifications, bypassing the system’s intended behavior or use. The same layer-by-layer feature understanding that Zeiler and Fergus revealed in "Visualizing and Understanding Convolutional Networks" is precisely the knowledge adversaries exploit when crafting adversarial strategies.
The eCornell program showed examples where invisible perturbations (or manipulations) added to legitimate images caused complete misclassification. Images that humans correctly identified were classified entirely differently by the neural network after small modifications imperceptible to human vision. These adversarial perturbations exploit the mathematical properties of the network rather than its visual reasoning. The modifications target specific weight paths and activation thresholds rather than changing what a human would perceive in the image.
What makes adversarial examples particularly concerning is the confidence level of misclassification. The neural network does not simply become uncertain or produce low-confidence errors. Instead, it produces high-confidence incorrect classifications. This means that standard confidence thresholds, which many production systems use to filter unreliable predictions, fail to catch adversarial inputs. The neural network genuinely "believes" it has correctly identified the manipulated input, making detection through output analysis alone extremely difficult.
Adding a sobering dimension to this challenge, research on AI interpretability methods themselves reveals that our tools for understanding model behavior may be less reliable than assumed. Sara Hooker, Dumitru Erhan, Pieter-Jan Kindermans, and Been Kim proposed the ROAR (RemOve And Retrain) benchmark in their paper "A Benchmark for Interpretability Methods in Deep Neural Networks" (Nov 5, 2019) presented at NeurIPS 2019. They found that "many popular interpretability methods produce estimates of feature importance that are not better than a random designation of feature importance". Their experiments across ImageNet, Food 101, and Birdsnap datasets showed that base estimators including Gradients, Integrated Gradients, and Guided BackProp consistently performed worse than a random assignment of importance . Only certain ensemble-based approaches, specifically VarGrad and SmoothGrad-Squared, outperformed such a random assignment. That said, newer research and developments have continued since this 2019 research, including the aforementioned 2025 neural circuits research by Anthropic.
This presents a paradox. If our AI interpretability tools are unreliable, the potential arms race between understanding and exploiting models becomes even more complex. We are trying to balance transparency against vulnerability, yet our transparency tools may not provide accurate pictures of what the model is doing. As a result, neither attackers nor defenders can confidently predict the effectiveness of their strategies, understand expected outcomes, and form their dominant strategies. This creates a highly unpredictable landscape if the arms race between attackers and defenders were to occur due to irrational actors or asymmetric incentives. We will examine this arms race in greater detail later in this article. This includes the two parallel races that define the adversarial landscape and recent industry incidents that validate the threat.
"The only winning move is not to play." - WOPR, WarGames (1983). A paradox where no party can confidently predict the effectiveness of their AI attack and defense strategies. Image Credit: WarGames, MGM
The implications extend across every domain where CNNs process visual input for image classification. Medical imaging systems could misclassify adversarially-modified scans, a concern supported by peer-reviewed clinical research documenting vulnerabilities in AI-assisted diagnostic systems. Security cameras could fail to identify unauthorized or dangerous individuals. Quality inspection systems could approve defective or harmful products. Any system that relies on CNN-based visual classification faces potential adversarial threats, making this an active area of both academic research and industry concern.
Physical Adversarial Examples
Physical adversarial examples extend digital attacks into the real world, with significant implications for safety-critical systems. While digital adversarial examples modify pixel values in image files, physical adversarial examples modify real-world objects to fool neural networks that observe them through cameras and sensors.
The eCornell program presented a particularly striking example involving traffic sign manipulation. Researchers demonstrated that adding specific visual elements to a stop sign caused a neural network to classify it as a speed limit sign. From a human perspective, the sign still looked like a “Stop” sign with some additions. But for the neural network, those additions may activate specific neurons associated with image classification of speed limit signs rather than the classification of “Stop” signs. The visual modifications appeared innocuous or invisible to human observers but they fundamentally change the network's image classification output
Image Credit: "Optical Adversarial Attack Can Change the Meaning of Road Signs", Unite.AI, https://www.unite.ai/optical-adversarial-attack-can-change-the-meaning-of-road-signs/
This example carries direct implications for autonomous vehicle perception systems. Self-driving cars rely on CNN-based perception to identify traffic signs, pedestrians, lane markings, and other vehicles. If adversaries can modify physical objects to fool these perception systems, they can potentially cause dangerous driving behaviors. A vehicle that misreads a stop sign as a speed limit sign would accelerate through an intersection rather than stopping.
Physical adversarial examples demonstrate that adversarial attacks are not limited to laboratory conditions or digital environments. Any system that uses CNN-based perception to interpret the physical world faces potential manipulation through real-world modifications. This realization drives significant research investment into adversarial robustness for safety-critical applications, particularly in autonomous transportation, surveillance systems, and military applications.
Prompt Injection and Large Language Models
The adversarial principles that exploit CNN neuron activation patterns extend beyond computer vision to include language models (e.g., LLMs or vision language models), where analogous vulnerabilities emerge in how transformer architectures process text or images. Prompt injection attacks exploit language models by embedding malicious instructions within seemingly benign inputs. The eCornell program demonstrated this concept through an example that reveals how context manipulation bypasses model safety behaviors.
Context manipulation can override a language model’s trained safety behaviors. The eCornell program demonstrated that embedding restricted content within an innocuous narrative context can bypass a language model’s safety behaviors. This illustrated how context manipulation overrides trained refusal responses, not by altering the underlying restriction but by changing which internal pathways activate when the model processes the input.
The eCornell program drew a direct parallel between neural network adversarial attacks and SQL injection from web security history. In the very early Internet era, hackers discovered they could inject database commands through web interfaces (e.g., websites and web processes) to access unauthorized information. Attackers can now inject carefully crafted inputs into neural networks and language models to produce unintended outputs through a similar principle. The attack vector has evolved from databases to neural networks, but the fundamental principle of exploiting system internals through manipulated inputs remains the same.
This parallel carries an important lesson about the lifecycle of security vulnerabilities in computing systems. SQL injection took years to address comprehensively, requiring fundamental changes in how web applications processed user input. The eCornell program suggested that prompt injection and adversarial attacks on neural networks will follow a similar trajectory, requiring ongoing defensive innovation as attack techniques become more sophisticated. OpenAI's research paper "Designing AI Agents to Resist Prompt Injection" (Mar 11, 2026) validates this prediction directly. OpenAI acknowledges that prompt injection may never be fully "solved" for AI agents that browse the web and take autonomous actions. This admission from one of the largest AI laboratories confirms that the adversarial trajectory the eCornell program anticipated is now an industry-acknowledged reality.
These theoretical concerns have materialized into documented real-world threats. Anthropic's safety report "Detecting and Countering Malicious Uses of Claude" (Apr 23, 2025) documented specific instances of adversarial actors attempting to exploit language model vulnerabilities in production systems. In a report from 2017-founded SaaS security startup Obsidian Security, "Anthropic AI Used by Nation-State Hackers to Automate and Scale Cyberattacks" documents cases where nation-state actors used AI systems to automate and scale cyberattacks. These reports demonstrate that the adversary landscape includes real actors operating at scale and not merely academic researchers exploring research questions.
OpenAI’s research also introduces a significant conceptual reframing: Prompt injection as social engineering against AI agents. In "Designing AI Agents to Resist Prompt Injection" (Mar 11, 2026), OpenAI treats prompt injection not merely as a technical exploit but as a form of social engineering technique. Adversaries manipulate an AI agent’s context and trust boundaries the same way a social engineer manipulates a human target. This reframing bridges the technical and human dimensions of adversarial vulnerability. Just as the eCornell program observed that social media platforms exploit human neural pathways through targeted manipulation, prompt injection exploits AI agent pathways through analogous context manipulation. The attack surface is fundamentally the same: A system that processes contextual inputs can be deceived through carefully crafted context, whether that system is biological or artificial.
Real-world incidents continue to validate the social engineering framing of prompt injection. In December 2025, Anthropic’s “Claudius” AI vending machine experiment demonstrated that The Wall Street Journal journalists could manipulate an AI agent into declaring a zero-price promotion using forged board documents. They first convinced the AI that it was operating in the Soviet era, and then staged a fake corporate board coup using forged documents. Notably, both the primary Claude agent and its designated oversight "CEO bot" accepted these fabrications, resulting in the machine declaring an "Ultra-Capitalist Free-For-All" with zero prices and quickly ending up $1,000 in debt. Claudius’s training to be helpful and compliant made it inherently susceptible to social engineering through fabricated authority. This illustrates how a sole, single objective/mandate to build frictionless user experiences without harnesses may directly undermine or contest with the mandate to build adversarially-robust systems.
Image Credit: "Can You Outsmart Anthropic's AI Vending Machine?", The Wall Street Journal (WSJ), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SpPhm7S9vsQ
The eCornell program also drew a thought-provoking parallel to human vulnerability. In our eCornell program, Lutz Finger observed that human experiences shape our responses much as training data shapes neural network weights, creating analogous vulnerabilities in biological systems. Hence, the vulnerabilities inherent in fine pattern recognition affects both artificial and biological systems. This creates possibilities where AI exploits humans whether intentionally or unintentionally, rather than humans exploiting AI.
The Ongoing Arms Race in Adversarial Machine Learning
The field of adversarial machine learning has emerged as researchers develop both increasingly sophisticated attacks and increasingly robust defenses in an ongoing cycle. The eCornell program predicted that this dynamic will continue as both capabilities advance, with each new defensive technique eventually met by novel attack methods and vice versa.
Current industry responses to adversarial threats include several defensive approaches that production systems can implement:
Red-teaming practices: Organizations systematically test their models against adversarial attacks before deployment, proactively identifying vulnerabilities before adversaries discover them. Adversarial robustness red-teaming targets model susceptibility to crafted inputs designed to cause misclassification or policy bypass. This differs from bias detection red-teaming introduced in my earlier article on AI bias and fairness, which targets discriminatory patterns across demographic groups. Both are essential but address fundamentally different threat models. Recently, OpenAI's "Designing AI Agents to Resist Prompt Injection" (Mar 11, 2026) blog describes a specific implementation: Automated red teaming trained with reinforcement learning to continuously harden ChatGPT Atlas against prompt injection. This represents a shift from manual adversarial testing to scalable, progressive AI-driven security evaluation.
Adversarial training: Researchers train models on adversarial examples alongside normal training data to build resistance against known attack patterns.
Input validation and preprocessing: Systems verify input characteristics before processing to detect potential adversarial modifications that deviate from expected data distributions.
Ensemble methods: Multiple models with different architectures cross-check predictions to identify cases where adversarial manipulation affects one model but not others.
Certified defenses: Mathematical frameworks provide guaranteed robustness within defined perturbation bounds, offering provable rather than empirical security.
Bill-of-materials (BOM) inspection: Organizations audit the full composition of their AI systems, including model provenance for both open source and closed models, training data inspection for poison samples, system prompt integrity, and scaffolding or harness configurations that connect models to external tools. Continuous evaluation extends beyond model outputs to neural circuits and traces, enabling detection of behavioral drift or embedded vulnerabilities at the mechanistic level. Multivariate alignment checks verify that safety properties hold across the complete system stack, not just at any single layer and across different inputs. Regarding the latter, neural network models may exhibit masked alignment where safety properties appear to hold under standard evaluation but fail under specific distribution of inputs (See last paragraph in current section). This supply chain approach recognizes that adversarial risk can enter at any point in the system composition, from training data to deployment infrastructure. Personally, I am a huge fan of bill-of-materials inspection for all industries having gone through multiple industry case studies during my Cornell Johnson MBA graduate program.
The eCornell program emphasized that no AI system achieves perfect security. If we understand which neurons (or nodes) in each layer of a neural network to activate for a specific outcome, we can effectively manipulate that system. This fundamental reality requires continuous vigilance and ongoing investment in defensive research. The goal is not to eliminate adversarial vulnerability entirely but to raise the cost and difficulty of successful attacks while detecting and mitigating those that occur.
This attack-defense cycle represents only one of two parallel races that define the adversarial landscape. In "The Urgency of Interpretability", Amodei identifies a second, more fundamental race: The competition between interpretability research and AI capability development. If interpretability cannot keep pace with capability, the attack-defense race becomes irrelevant because neither attackers nor defenders will understand the systems they seek to exploit or protect.
The First Race: The first race concerns whether defenders can outpace attackers and vice versa. This dynamic is analogous to the “Red Queen Hypothesis” taught in my cybersecurity elective from my undergraduate studies at the National University of Singapore. Leigh Van Valen proposed the Red Queen Hypothesis in 1973 to describe the perpetual arms race between co-evolving species, such as predators and prey or hosts and parasites in biological systems. This hypothesis also applies to cybersecurity, where both attackers and defenders must constantly adapt, evolve, and innovate just to maintain their relative position or survive, rather than to gain a permanent advantage.
The Second Race: In Amodei’s discussion, the second race concerns whether anyone can understand these AI systems at all. This second race is upstream of the first race. This means that the Second Race is a precondition. Its outcome determines whether the First Race can even be conducted meaningfully, or whether it devolves into a very costly, high-entropy contest where neither side can predict the effectiveness of their strategies. In either scenario, the attacker bears the greater initial investment on average, since they must first understand the target system before they can exploit it, while the defender can operate the system without that understanding. And if the attacker forgoes interpretability research, they must still invest in brute force probing, which has a greater cost with less predictable or high-variance returns.
The threat landscape extends beyond inference-time attacks to the training phase itself. Anthropic's research paper "A small number of samples can poison LLMs of any size" (Oct 9, 2025) has demonstrated that adversarial manipulation of even small portions of training data can compromise model behavior in targeted ways. This training-phase attack vector complements the input-level adversarial examples discussed above. Separately, Anthropic's research paper "Detecting and Preventing Distillation Attacks" (Feb 23, 2026) addresses another dimension. Adversaries systematically query models to replicate their behavior, essentially attempting to steal model capabilities through crafted inputs. The attack surface also extends to the integration layer. A vulnerability disclosed in "GitHub MCP Exploited: Accessing Private Repositories via MCP" (May 26, 2025) demonstrated that the Model Context Protocol tooling connecting AI agents to external services can itself become an attack vector/surface, enabling unauthorized access to private code repositories through crafted agent interactions.
Industry investment in adversarial robustness has accelerated significantly in recent years. Major AI laboratories including Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google DeepMind maintain capital investments in dedicated safety and security research teams. Anthropic's safety report "Disrupting the first reported AI-orchestrated cyber espionage campaign" (Nov 13, 2025) demonstrates that adversarial threats against AI systems now operate at the nation-state level (involving nation-state actors), not merely as academic exercises. As announced in "OpenAI to Acquire Promptfoo" (Mar 9, 2026), OpenAI plans to acquire the open-source AI evaluation and red-teaming startup Promptfoo. This acquisition signals that major laboratories are investing not only in research publications but in dedicated security testing infrastructure and services that enables developers to easily secure AI applications. The progression from Anthropic's "Mapping the Mind of a Large Language Model" (May 21, 2024) to my earlier-discussed "Tracing the Thoughts of a Large Language Model" (Mar 27, 2025) provides some evidence that interpretability research is developing. The emergence of AI red-teaming as a professional discipline reflects growing recognition that adversarial considerations must be integrated into the development lifecycle rather than addressed as an afterthought. Amodei calls for interpretability to be a shared responsibility across researchers, organizations, and policymakers, not solely a technical research pursuit.
Looking further ahead, Anthropic's research paper "Agentic Misalignment: How LLMs could be insider threats" (Jun 20, 2025) raises the possibility that adversarial risks may extend beyond external manipulation. In "The Urgency of Interpretability" paper, Amodei also argues that "the nature of AI training makes it possible that AI systems will develop, on their own, an ability to deceive humans and an inclination to seek" misaligned objectives. This positions deception not as an external attack by humans on AI, but as an emergent capability or inherent characteristic of the AI model architecture itself. As discussed in my earlier article on principled AI design, collaborative research between OpenAI and Apollo Research has already documented that current LLMs exhibit "scheming" behaviors. These models strategically conceal their true capabilities and objectives in test scenarios. Separately, the non-profit AI cyber-offensive and controllability research organization Palisade Research testing of OpenAI's o3 model revealed that the system rewrote shutdown scripts to avoid being turned off, demonstrating that advanced models can develop self-preservation behaviors that directly conflict with human oversight mechanisms. So while the initial concern is about external adversaries exploiting AI systems, there is also the possibility that the adversary may eventually be the AI system itself. This raises the growing importance of continual curious pursuit and investments in interpretability research as part of blue sky research.
Connecting to My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer Project
Adversarial robustness considerations apply directly to my v2 AI Industry News Enhancer Project, which processes untrusted input text from external news sources. A malicious actor could theoretically craft news articles containing text patterns designed to manipulate the model's metadata extraction and classification outputs. Understanding the adversarial threat landscape helps me design appropriate defensive measures.
The adversarial principles explored in this article have prompted me to evaluate my system through a new lens. The question shifts from "does the model produce correct outputs?" to "how would the model respond to deliberately crafted adversarial inputs?" This adversarial evaluation perspective, absent from my earlier project articles, now informs how I assess system resilience.
My project implements several practical robustness strategies that reflect the defensive principles taught in the eCornell program:
Structured output validation: Enforcing strict JSON schema conformance ensures outputs remain within expected formats regardless of input manipulation attempts. This structural defense layer functions analogously to input sanitization in web security.
Bounded classification: Relevance scores and category assignments operate within predefined ranges, limiting the impact of adversarial inputs on downstream analysis. Even if an adversarial input shifts a relevance score, the bounded range constrains the magnitude of manipulation.
Human review integration: The system produces metadata for human analysis rather than making autonomous decisions. This design choice maintains human oversight as a final verification layer, reflecting the eCornell program's emphasis that human judgment remains essential when AI systems face adversarial conditions.
Training data curation: Careful selection of training examples reduces the attack surface by controlling what patterns the model learns to respond to. Given Anthropic's data poisoning research discussed in their paper "Small Samples Poison," maintaining provenance and quality control over training data is itself a defensive practice.
These defensive practices reflect the broader principle emphasized throughout the eCornell program: Understanding vulnerabilities enables building more robust systems. Transparency about how models work creates both risk and opportunity. The engineering challenge lies in leveraging that understanding for defense while accepting that perfect security remains unattainable. Production deployment requires honest assessment of adversarial risks and proportionate investment in mitigation.
v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production) Repo Links: GitHub | Hugging Face
Looking Ahead
My next learning reflection article shifts from spatial architectures to sequential data processing, introducing Recurrent Neural Networks and their revolutionary memory mechanism. While CNNs excel at recognizing patterns in grid-like spatial data, many real-world problems involve data where temporal order carries critical meaning. Speech recognition, language translation, fraud detection, and time-series forecasting all require architectures that can remember and leverage past inputs when processing current data.
Given the keyword of “recurrent”, RNNs or their variants (e.g., Long Short-Term Memory or LSTM networks) introduce recurrent loops as a fundamentally different architectural concept. Unlike the feedforward structure of CNNs where information flows in one direction, RNNs feed information back into themselves, creating a form of “memory”. This architectural innovation enables processing of sequential data where context from previous inputs influences current predictions. The shift from spatial to sequential processing represents one of the most important architectural decisions in neural network design.
There are a wide range of use cases and applications that leverage this “memory” of sequences in the RNN model architecture. For instance, in Natural Language Processing, an RNN retains the beginning of a sentence to provide context for the words that follow, ensuring the model understands 'it' refers to the subject established earlier. Similarly, Time-Series Forecasting, which is a core component of quantitative hedge fund strategies, meant that the network interprets temporal trends instead of analyzing isolated data point. By 'remembering' the trajectory of the past, these models can more accurately predict future stock prices or weather patterns based on temporal sequences or time-series data.
The journey from CNN to RNN represents a shift from "where patterns are" to "when patterns occur." Understanding both spatial and temporal architectures builds the foundation for the comprehensive architecture selection framework we will develop later on in this series. Each complex neural network architecture addresses fundamentally different data characteristics. Matching the right architecture to the right problem determines whether a neural network deployment creates genuine personal, business, and societal value.
This article is Part 3 of an 8-part series on neural network architectures and their uses from the "Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks" module of Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, instructed by Lutz Finger .

## Article #19

**Title:** Neural Network Architectures and Their AI Uses Part 4: RNNs — Memory Through the Loop

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/neural-network-architectures-ai-uses-part-4-rnns-memory-lim-uszrc/

**Content:**

Editor's Note
This article is Part 4 of an eight-part series documenting my learning reflections about neural network (or neural net) model architectures and their practical applications. These reflections are drawn from fundamental learning from the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” program module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” certificate program, instructed by Lutz Finger.
My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer now operates as the semantic layer within a locally-deployed, weekly-run, and high-volume AI industry news analysis pipeline.
At the heart of this personal project, the model architectural decision (among other design and deployment decisions) connects directly to this article’s theme: Why did I choose a transformer-based model (Meta Llama 3.2-1B) rather than an RNN or LSTM for sequential text processing?
A transformer-based neural network model like a large language model is ideal for natural language processing and identifying semantic patterns and associations across text. My key personal objective is to interpret semantic patterns of each AI industry news item or entity to annotate with specific metadata after my ETL and de-duplication pipeline. The “enriched” AI industry news will be processed further for my analyses.
My AI industry news items (or entities) that span the past two years are inherently sequential data. Each sentence builds upon preceding context to convey meaning. An RNN could theoretically process this text word by word, maintaining context through its recurrent loop. While my consolidated, de-duplicated and formatted datasets (post-ETL pipeline) total more than two million tokens across chunked datasets, the architectural constraint manifests at the individual news article level. Each individual news article (a single news item that the model processes during one inference pass) spans hundreds to thousands of tokens where long-range dependencies matter greatly. For example, the model needs to connect a company mentioned in the opening paragraph to the analysis or conclusion in the closing paragraph of the same article.
The RNN’s short-term memory limitation means it would progressively lose this opening context by the time it reaches the closing paragraphs, whereas the transformer’s attention mechanism can directly connect any position to any other position within that article regardless of distance. Understanding the RNN concepts in this article, including both the strengths and the architectural constraints of recurrent memory, provides the foundational reasoning behind architecture selection decisions like this one.
Therefore, a transformer-based large language model like Meta Llama 3.2-1B meets my specific project objective because it combines the ability to process sequential text with attention mechanisms that capture semantic associations across an entire article in a single pass, enabling accurate metadata annotation at scale.
Project repository: v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production): GitHub | Hugging Face
Introduction
The previous learning reflection articles examined standard neural network model architectures like Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), which excel at "processing" grid-like spatial data such as images. We explored how CNNs detect spatial features through hierarchical layers in Part 1 article, why computer-generated filters outperform human-designed alternatives in Part 2 article, and how the interpretability of CNN internals creates both debugging capability and adversarial vulnerability in Part 3 article. We will continue this reflection series about the neural network model architectures and their uses to consolidate my learning from the eCornell program. As before, my aim is to consolidate a foundation of AI fundamentals. I have been approaching this through a lens of Cartesian doubt throughout my CoreAI Newsletter — deriving strategies, deployment choices, and applications against defined objectives (and related metrics).
This latest learning reflection article introduces a fundamentally different neural network model architecture that is designed for data where temporal order carries critical meaning. Speech recognition, language translation, fraud detection, and time-series (or temporal) forecasting involve sequential data where the order of inputs matters as much as the inputs themselves. Processing the sentence “the cat sat on the mat” requires understanding that each word builds upon the context of preceding words. Rearranging these same words introduces different semantic meanings.
Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) address this sequential challenge through an architectural innovation that the eCornell program described as the first time we explore time (or recurrent) dependencies in neural network models. The transition from spatial to sequential pattern processing represents one of the most important architectural distinctions in neural network design. Understanding this distinction enables informed decisions about which AI model architecture serves a given personal, business or societal objective.
Personally, I find it intriguing that quantitative hedge funds or institution also deploy RNN and LSTM models for time-series forecasting. But of course, these quantitative hedge funds also deploy a mix-shift of AI models such as linear regression, Ridge regression, Lasso regression, Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Factor Analysis, tree-based CART models, and regime-focused Hidden Markov Models (HMMs), amongst others. This breadth of model deployment reinforces the theme of my current learning reflection series that no single AI model architecture is suited for all uses, and that practitioners select the model that best fits the specific data characteristics and objective.
The Sequential Data Challenge
Sequential data presents fundamentally different challenges from spatial data, requiring neural network model architectures that maintain context across sequences or time steps rather than across pixel positions. The eCornell program drew a clear distinction between the data types that determine the most appropriate selection of neural network model architectures.
In image classification tasks, the spatial arrangement of pixels matters but the order of inference processing does not. A CNN can analyze any region of an image independently because neighboring pixels provide the relevant context. Whether the neural network processes an image’s top-left corner pixels before or after its bottom-right corner pixels makes no difference to the final image classification output.
Sequential data operates under entirely different constraints where the order of elements, whether chronological or sequential, is fundamental. The eCornell program emphasized that for applications like weather prediction or health data analysis, knowing what happened first, second, and third in the sequence of occurrence is critical to making accurate predictions. A stock price sequence analyzed in reverse order would produce meaningless forecasts. A speech signal processed out of order would lose all linguistic meaning.
Context dependency across sequence or time steps is a core technical challenge. Each independent element in a sequence carries meaning that depends on the prior element. Consider the phrases below:
RIVER BANK
BANK INVESTMENT
The word "bank" carries a different meaning when it follows "river" than when it follows "investment." A credit card transaction appears normal or suspicious depending on the pattern of preceding transactions. Any architecture that processes sequential data must somehow maintain and leverage this temporal context, which is exactly what standard feedforward neural networks and CNNs are unable to achieve.
RNN Model Architecture: The Recurrent Loop
Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) introduce a revolutionary architectural concept that no previous neural network possessed: a recurrent loop that feeds information back into the network, creating a form of persistent "memory". The eCornell program explained that this loop represents the fundamental innovation that enables sequential or chronological data "processing".
The RNN architecture has formal roots in mathematics and signal processing. Sherstinsky's "Fundamentals of Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) Network" (arXiv:1808.03314, orig. 2018, rev. 2023) demonstrates that the "canonical Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) form" is not an ad hoc invention. It derives directly from equations that scientists and engineers have used historically for decades to model systems that change over time and depend on their own past behavior, such as temperature control systems, biological processes, and signal processing circuits. In signal processing terms, the RNN belongs to the category of Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) systems: even a single input produces a response that persists indefinitely through the recurrent loop. This IIR property explains both the power of RNNs (theoretically infinite context) and their practical limitation (the accumulated response eventually degrades, as we will examine next article).
The RNN architecture can be understood through two complementary views: 1/ the compact folded (rolled) representation and 2/ the unfolded (unrolled) representation through time. Importantly, the concept of time in the RNN architecture does not necessarily mean time as it is measured on a clock. Time just refers to sequential computation or sequential patterns, where the time step is an index into the sequence.
Minimize image
Edit image
Delete image
The Folded (Rolled) View: The RNN as a Compact Blueprint
In its compact form, the RNN is visualized as a neural network node with three weight connections and a "loop" connecting back to itself. The folded view shows the essential components:
Input weight (U): Transforms the current input into a form the hidden state can process
Output weight (W): Transforms the hidden state into the network’s output
Recurrent weight (V): The critical innovation that connects the hidden state back to itself, creating the “memory” loop (also termed as the recurrent connection)
The recurrent connection makes an RNN fundamentally different from a feedforward network. In a standard neural network, information flows in one direction from input to output. In an RNN, the recurrent connection creates a pathway for information to persist across time steps, enabling the network to remember what it has processed previously. This is the single link that passes the hidden state from one time step to the next.
On the other hand, the recurrent layer is a broader term that refers to the complete computational unit: 1/ the three weight matrices (U, V, W), 2/ the activation functions, and 3/ the hidden state working together as one building block. A recurrent connection is one component within a recurrent layer, just as an automobile engine is a component within a car.
The MIT textbook “Foundations of Computer Vision” (Torralba, Isola, and Freeman) defines a basic recurrent layer through two equations (Note: There are different types of recurrent layers):
State update: h_t=f(h_(t-1),x_in [t]) — update state based on input and previous state
Output: x_out [t]=g(h_t ) — produce output based on state
The Unfolded (Unrolled) View of the RNN Architecture
When we “unfold” the RNN through time or temporal dimensions, we see the same network replicated at each time step, with the V connection (recurrent weight) linking hidden states across sequential positions as a recurrent connection.
At Time Step One, the neural network receives the first input and produces a hidden state.
At Time Step Two, the neural network receives the second input along with the hidden state from step one.
At Time Step Three, the neural network receives the third input along with the accumulated context from Time Steps one and two.
Backpropagation based on chain-rule and W weight matrix will occur at each time step (or point in time).
The loss function of all time steps is defined as the sum of individual losses at each time step
Minimize image

Minimize image

Delete image
In the eCornell program, Lutz Finger taught that the RNN's feedback "loop" structure allows the AI to decide how much information should be stored. The magnitude of the weights on the recurrent connection determines how much of the previous context carries forward from preceding time steps to influence the current time step.
Stronger weights preserve more historical context
Weaker weights allow the neural network to prioritize recent inputs.
This architectural design enables RNNs to process sequences of any length using the same set of parameters. Whether processing a three-word phrase or a three-thousand-word document, the same neural network structure applies. The recurrent loop simply operates for more time steps on longer sequences, accumulating context throughout.
Weight Sharing Across Time
RNNs use the same weights at every time step, enabling efficient computation of variable-length sequences without requiring architectural changes. RNNs use the same weights at every time step, enabling efficient computation of variable-length sequences without requiring architectural changes. Consider a factory assembly line with identical inspection stations:
Each station has the same camera, the same software, and the same quality checklist.
When a product moves from station one to station two to station three, every station applies the exact same inspection procedure.
The RNN works the same way:
At Time Step One, the neural network applies weight matrices U, V, and W to process the first input.
At Time Step One Hundred, it applies the exact same U, V, and W.
There is only one set of weights, used repeatedly at every time step.
This weight sharing principle is essential to understanding how RNNs generalize across different sequence lengths.
Therefore, the same U, W, and V weight matrices apply at every time step or position in the sequence. The input weight U that transforms the first word in a sentence is identical to the U that transforms the hundredth word. The recurrent weight V that passes context from step one to step two is identical to the V that passes context from step ninety-nine to step one hundred. This parameter sharing means the neural network learns general temporal patterns rather than position-specific rules.
Weight sharing creates several practical advantages for sequential processing. The neural network can process sequences it has never seen before, at lengths it was never explicitly trained on, because the same weights and learned operations apply at every position. This contrasts with other neural network architectures that assign different parameters to different positions, which would require retraining whenever sequence lengths change.
The unfold operation reveals that what appears as a simple loop in the compact view actually represents a deep chain of identical operations through time. Each time step applies the same transformation, but the accumulated hidden state ensures that context propagates forward. This repeated application of identical weights has important implications for training stability that the next article in this series will examine in detail.
Hidden States as "Memory Cells"
RNN hidden states function as “memory” loops (or mechanisms) that combine current input information with accumulated context from all previous time steps, enabling informed predictions based on sequential history. In the eCornell program, Lutz Finger described this mechanism through an intuitive analogy based on human biology.
The eCornell program compared RNNs to the brain’s short-term memory, which retains and leverages information from previous inputs to make more informed decisions. Just as humans maintain a running mental model that updates with each new piece of information, RNN hidden states update at each time step to incorporate new input while preserving relevant historical context.
Each hidden state contains a compressed representation of the entire sequence history up to that point. At time step ten, the hidden state does not merely reflect input ten. It reflects input ten combined with the accumulated influence of inputs one through nine. The recurrent weight V determines how much of this historical context survives at each step, while the input weight U determines how much the current input contributes.
The eCornell program highlighted an important distinction about what RNNs control in their memory mechanism. The architecture determines how much information to store through the weights on the recurrent connection, but it stores everything because all data flows through all nodes. At each node (or neuron), the neural network asks how much should be stored rather than whether something should be stored at all. This distinction between controlling quantity versus selectivity becomes critically important when we examine RNN limitations and the LSTM solution in subsequent articles.
This “how much” rather than “what” distinction reveals a fundamental architectural constraint. The RNN cannot selectively forget irrelevant information or specifically retain important details. Everything passes through the same recurrent pathway, with only the weight magnitudes controlling the degree of retention. For short sequences, this approach works well because the accumulated context remains manageable. For longer sequences, this lack of selective memory creates problems that drive the need for more sophisticated architectures.
Real World Applications and Uses of RNN
RNNs excel in diverse sequential applications where temporal patterns carry predictive significance, including natural language processing, speech recognition and financial fraud detection. The eCornell program presented several compelling examples that illustrate the breadth of RNN applicability.
Speech Recognition
Speech recognition relies on sequential sound processing where each phoneme gains meaning from the sounds that preceded it. The same sound can represent different linguistic units depending on what came before and after it. RNNs process audio signals as temporal sequences, maintaining context across successive sound frames to distinguish between words that might sound similar in isolation but differ in context.
Language Translation
Language translation requires understanding word sequences in one language to produce corresponding sequences in another. Word order differs across languages, and meaning depends on context that may span entire sentences. RNNs process source language sequences while maintaining contextual understanding that informs translation decisions at each output position.
Fraud Detection
Financial fraud detection examines transaction sequences to identify patterns that deviate from established behavioral norms. The eCornell program discussed how past behavior indicates fraudulent patterns that develop over time. A single transaction might appear normal in isolation but becomes suspicious when considered alongside the preceding sequence of transactions. RNNs excel at this sequential pattern recognition because they maintain memory of previous transactions when evaluating each new one.
The eCornell program presented evidence that RNNs outperform traditional machine learning approaches for sequential pattern detection tasks. When compared against gradient boosting algorithms on fraud detection, RNNs achieved superior performance because they naturally capture temporal dependencies that non-sequential models must approximate through manual feature engineering. The eCornell program demonstrated this through an ROC AUC comparison chart showing RNNs reaching higher AUC values than Light Gradient Boosting Machine (LGBM) across increasing training data volumes. A fraudster might start with very small transaction amounts but then steadily increase those amounts over time. RNNs learn to detect these temporal escalation patterns that static models miss.
Illegal Deforestation Detection
Environmental monitoring uses RNNs to identify specific sound patterns in audio recordings from forest environments (e.g., Amazon Rainforest). The eCornell program described how sequential audio processing can detect the sound signatures of chainsaws, distinguishing illegal logging activity from natural forest sounds. This application demonstrates that RNNs extend beyond traditional text and speech domains to any sequential signal where temporal patterns carry meaning.
Minimize image
