Delete image
Industry Developments: The RNN Legacy and Modern Sequential Architectures
RNN concepts continue to shape real-world AI deployments and have inspired a new generation of sequential neural network architectures that address RNN limitations while preserving their core strengths. While transformers now dominate natural language processing, the recurrent paradigm introduced by RNNs remains strategically important across multiple industry domains. Mienye, Swart, and Obaido’s “Recurrent Neural Networks: A Comprehensive Review of Architectures, Variants, and Applications” (MDPI Information, August 2024) provides a thorough survey confirming that RNNs and their variants, including LSTM, GRU, and bidirectional architectures, remain actively deployed across NLP, speech recognition, time-series forecasting, autonomous vehicles, and anomaly detection.
The RNN Renaissance: State Space Models and Hybrid Architectures
The recurrent paradigm is experiencing a resurgence through State Space Models (SSMs), which represent a modern evolution of the sequential processing concepts that RNNs introduced. In December 2023, Albert Gu and Tri Dao published “Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces,” introducing a selective state space model that achieves five times higher throughput than Transformers with linear scaling in sequence length. Mamba addresses the core limitations of classical RNNs, including vanishing gradients and slow training, while preserving the recurrent paradigm’s fundamental advantage: efficient sequential inference without the quadratic memory cost of transformer-based attention.
Hybrid neural network model architectures that combine recurrent and attention mechanisms are gaining industry adoption. NVIDIA’s Nemotron-H family and IBM’s Granite 4.0 both incorporate SSM (Mamba) layers alongside transformer attention layers, combining the efficiency of recurrence with the ability of attention to capture complex relationships across an entire sequence (or the “expressivity of attention”). These developments validate the eCornell program’s teaching that RNNs and their variants are available tools in the neural network model architecture toolkit.
The Extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) architecture demonstrates that the RNN family continues to evolve. Introduced by Sepp Hochreiter’s group in “xLSTM: Extended Long Short-Term Memory” (May 2024), xLSTM modernizes the LSTM concept covered later in this series with improved gating and expanded memory structures through two variants (sLSTM for scalar memory and mLSTM for matrix memory) that enhance how the neural network stores and retrieves sequential information. Building on xLSTM, Kong et al.’s “Unlocking the Power of LSTM for Long Term Time Series Forecasting” (AAAI, 2025) proposed P-sLSTM, which adapts sLSTM specifically for time-series forecasting through techniques that improve how the model segments and processes input data. P-sLSTM outperforms the original LSTM by 20% in accuracy and reaches comparable performance against state-of-the-art transformer-based models. These developments validate that the recurrent memory concepts (which are explored in this learning reflection article) continue to drive competitive architectural innovations.
Recurrent depth models demonstrate that the recurrent loop concept from this article can scale to frontier-level reasoning performance. Geiping et al.’s “Scaling up Test-Time Compute with Latent Reasoning: A Recurrent Depth Approach” (February 2025, accepted at NeurIPS 2025) trained a 3.5 billion parameter model called Huginn that applies the same recurrent computation repeatedly to deepen its reasoning internally rather than producing visible step-by-step reasoning text. The model achieves performance equivalent to a 50 billion parameter model on reasoning benchmarks by “unrolling” its recurrent block to arbitrary depth at test time (Recall my earlier reflection about “unrolled” view of RNN). This approach validates the core RNN principle explored in this article: the same recurrent operation applied repeatedly across steps can accumulate increasingly sophisticated understanding.
The Mixture-of-Recursions framework extends the recurrent paradigm by enabling each token to receive a different number of recursive iterations. Bae et al.’s “Mixture-of-Recursions: Learning Dynamic Recursive Depths for Adaptive Token-Level Computation” (July 2025, Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology, Google Cloud, Google DeepMind, Google Research) combines parameter sharing through recursive transformers with lightweight routers that dynamically assign different recursion depths to individual tokens. Not every word needs the same amount of "thinking." Simple, predictable words like "the" or "and" need less processing than complex or ambiguous ones. Mixture-of-Recursions solves this by letting the model decide how much "processing each token actually needs — similar to an editor who quickly skims simple sentences but carefully rewrites complex ones, reusing the same editing process rather than hiring a new editor for each pass. A lightweight "traffic director" (the router) makes these decisions on the fly for each token. Across model scales from 135 million to 1.7 billion parameters, Mixture-of-Recursions establishes a new efficiency frontier: achieving improved language understanding (“lower perplexity”) at smaller model sizes with higher throughput compared to standard and existing recursive baselines (or faster processing rate). This research directly connects the weight sharing concept covered earlier in this article to modern architectural innovation.
Recent research also reveals that current large language models struggle to capture sequential dependencies in practical applications, reinforcing why dedicated sequential architectures remain essential. Kim et al.’s “Lost in Sequence: Do Large Language Models Understand Sequential Recommendation?” (February 2025, KAIST) demonstrated that LLM-based recommendation systems fail to leverage sequential information in user interaction histories. When researchers shuffled the order of items in purchase sequences, LLM performance remained largely unchanged, whereas traditional sequential recommenders (using recurrent architectures like GRU4Rec and attention based SASRec) suffered significant degradation. This finding validates the eCornell program’s emphasis that sequential data requires neural network model architectures specifically designed to capture temporal dependencies.
From a business strategy perspective, the resurgence of recurrent architectures represents a shift in where competitive advantage resides in the AI value chain. Drawing on economics and advanced business strategy studies from my two-year Cornell Johnson MBA journey, the directional pattern for product niches is clear.
As pure transformer inference costs grow quadratically with sequence length, enterprises face a direct constraint on profit margins for long-context workloads. Hybrid recurrent architectures like Nemotron-H and Mamba (mentioned earlier) address this by reducing inference costs while maintaining performance, effectively improving operational efficiency for firms deploying AI at scale.
At the same time, as foundational model architectures become increasingly commoditized and widely available, the source of durable competitive advantage shifts from the model itself to the proprietary data and specialized domain knowledge that train it.
The training data becomes the critical enabling asset that determines whether an AI deployment creates genuine business value or merely replicates what competitors can access through open-source alternatives.
For business leaders evaluating AI investments, the AI model architecture selection decision explored throughout this series is not merely a technical choice. It directly determines inference costs, deployment feasibility on constrained computational hardware (compute and memory), and ultimately the return on investments for AI initiatives when they address your personal/business/societal objectives.
That said, we must also consider external markets, regulations, customer preferences, collaborators and positioning to drive a fully consistent strategy – beyond product features and advantages.
Quantified Domain-Specific and Industry Applications
RNN-based and RNN-derived architectures deliver measurable business value across multiple industry verticals. The following industry applications and recent research demonstrate how sequential neural network model architectures continue to drive impact across financial services, healthcare, time-series forecasting, edge computing, and medical imaging.
Financial services and fraud detection: Recent research validates the specific RNN variant architectures that the eCornell program taught for sequential pattern detection. Damanik and Liu’s “Advanced Fraud Detection: Leveraging K-SMOTEENN and Stacking Ensemble” (2025) combined LSTM and GRU networks with an MLP meta-learner, achieving a perfect sensitivity (recall) of 1.00 and a specificity of 0.997 for credit card fraud detection. A separate study, “Enhanced Credit Card Fraud Detection Using Deep Hybrid CLST Model” (June 2025), achieved ROC-AUC of 0.9733 by using CNN for spatial features and LSTM for sequential transaction patterns. These results directly validate the eCornell program’s RNN versus LGBM AUC comparison at production scale. LSTM and bidirectional LSTM architectures also support “Enhanced Credit Card Fraud Detection Using Federated Learning, LSTM Models, and the SMOTE Technique” (ICAART 2025), enabling multiple financial institutions to collaboratively train fraud detection models without sharing sensitive transaction data, addressing the data privacy constraints discussed in my previous article on federated computing.
Healthcare and genomics: Georgia Tech researchers demonstrated improved heart failure prediction accuracy using RNN-based models that process sequential patient records, capturing temporal deterioration patterns that static models miss. With an 18-month observation window, their GRU model achieved an AUC of 0.883, outperforming the best traditional baseline by approximately 5%. Published in February 2026, “A Novel Recurrent Neural Network Framework for Prediction and Treatment of Oncogenic Mutation Progression” demonstrates RNNs being chosen over Transformers for cancer mutation progression prediction because Transformers require significantly larger datasets to train effectively, increasing model complexity and runtime for genomic sequence analysis. The researchers explicitly elected to use RNNs over Transformers for this genomic application, drawing a direct parallel between time-series data and mutation sequences — achieving accuracies greater than 60%, comparable to existing cancer diagnostics.
Time-series forecasting: Yunita et al.’s “Performance Analysis of Neural Network Architectures for Time Series Forecasting: A Comparative Study of RNN, LSTM, GRU, and Hybrid Models” (2025) evaluated nine RNN, LSTM, and GRU architectures across 100 Monte Carlo iterations. While the Friedman test found no statistically significant differences among architectures, consistent performance patterns emerged favouring LSTM-based hybrids — with LSTM-RNN excelling in sunspot and dissolved oxygen forecasting and standalone LSTM showing optimal performance for COVID-19 prediction. These findings validate the eCornell program’s emphasis on matching AI model architectures to data characteristics. Cherifi et al.'s "Autoencoder, LSTM, RNN, and SVM for Anomaly Detection, Prediction, and Localization in Industrial Systems" (2025) combines Autoencoders, LSTM, RNN, and One-Class SVMs to detect faults in real-time, estimate machine health for future time periods, and precisely localise errors in IoT-enabled industrial machines — demonstrating RNNs operating as part of broader multi-method systems for predictive maintenance.
Edge computing and real-time processing: For applications running on mobile devices and embedded systems, RNN variants offer critical advantages. Google/Google Research compressed their RNN-based speech recognition model from 450MB to 80MB for on-device processing in Gboard, achieving a 4x runtime speedup that eliminated cloud latency entirely. Feng et al.'s "Were RNNs All We Needed?" (arXiv:2410.01201, October 2024) introduced minimized RNN variants — minGRU and minLSTM — that are fully parallelizable during training, achieving up to 175x faster training than traditional GRUs at a sequence length of 512 tokens, while matching the performance of state-of-the-art models like Mamba. Unlike Transformers, which require quadratic memory as sequence length grows, these minimized RNNs maintain linear complexity, making them well-suited for resource-constrained environments (e.g., memory).
Regulatory compliance and explainability: RNN and LSTM neural network model architectures offer a more intuitive sequential processing structure compared to Transformers — inputs flow step-by-step through defined gates rather than through simultaneous attention across all tokens. While neither model architecture is fully transparent or explainable (as with neural networks), this sequential structure can simplify certain model audits and aligns more naturally with the kind of step-by-step traceability that regulators in healthcare, finance, and insurance increasingly seek for automated decision systems.
Medical imaging and radiology report generation: Messina et al.'s "A Survey on Deep Learning and Explainability for Automatic Report Generation from Medical Images" (arXiv:2010.10563, 2022) and Izhar et al.'s "Medical Radiology Report Generation: A Systematic Review of Current Deep Learning Methods, Trends, and Future Directions" (Artificial Intelligence in Medicine, 2025) together document how CNN-RNN hierarchical frameworks use CNNs to extract spatial features from medical images and RNN or LSTM decoders to generate sequential text reports — directly bridging the spatial and sequential architectures explored across this article series. While Transformer-based decoders have increasingly replaced LSTM decoders in more recent work, CNN-RNN frameworks established the foundational architecture for this application and remain relevant, particularly in resource-constrained clinical settings.
Frontier Research Developments in Recurrent Architectures
Innovation-driven Recurrent Neural Networks (IRNN) represent a recent research advance that enhances RNN prediction accuracy without substantial additional training or computational costs. Zhou et al.'s "IRNN: Innovation-driven Recurrent Neural Network for Time-Series Data Modeling and Prediction" (arXiv:2505.05916, 2025) adapts the concept of "innovation" from Kalman Filters to RNN architectures, using past prediction errors as additional input signals to update hidden states. This approach achieves remarkably improved prediction accuracy across real-world benchmark datasets for time-series modeling. The tailored training algorithm alternates between updating innovations and optimizing network parameters via gradient descent, demonstrating that the RNN paradigm continues to generate novel optimization techniques.
Albert Gu's "On the Tradeoffs of SSMs and Transformers" (Goomba Lab blog, July 2025) provides a framework for understanding how modern recurrent models (e.g., SSMs) relate to the classical RNNs. Gu observes that the selectivity mechanisms in modern state space models are closely related to the gating mechanisms introduced by LSTM in 1997 and refined by GRU. These are the same information flow controls that evolved to address the vanishing gradient and "forgetting" limitations of classical RNNs. The core challenge remains the same: how to maintain and selectively update a compressed representation of sequential history. The difference lies in how modern architectures parameterize and parallelize this recurrence for practical training efficiency on GPUs — as opposed to training classical RNNs one sequence at a time.
Hybrid deep learning models that combine CNN spatial features with LSTM temporal features represent a growing research trend across multiple domains. Hamdan et al.'s "Deep Learning-based Blockchain Framework for Fraud Detection using Multilevel Supervision in Hierarchical Generative Hashing" (International Journal of Machine Learning and Cybernetics, 2026) and Yu and Luo's "Financial Fraud Detection using a Hybrid Deep Belief Network and Quantum Optimization Approach" (Discover Applied Sciences, 2025) both demonstrate hybrid deep learning architectures combining CNN and LSTM components consistently outperforming single-architecture approaches for fraud detection. Notably, the Hamdan et al. framework uses a dual-pathway CNN and bidirectional LSTM, achieving 99.10% classification accuracy against eleven baseline approaches. This hybrid pattern directly connects the CNN concepts from Part 1 article through Part 3 article of this neural network architecture series with RNN and LSTM concepts explored here, validating the eCornell program's architectural mapping framework: different data characteristics demand different neural network architectures, and complex real-world problems often benefit from combining multiple architectural strengths.
Connecting to My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer Project
The Transformer architecture powering Meta Llama 3.2-1B in my project emerged as a response to the limitations that RNNs first exposed.
The recurrent loop was the foundational architectural solution for maintaining context across sequential steps, and the challenges it surfaced — how to capture long-range dependencies, how to represent sequential position, how to selectively retain relevant context — shaped every subsequent architectural innovation.
Attention mechanisms were first introduced as enhancements to RNN-based models before Transformers abandoned recurrence entirely in favour of self-attention to process all tokens simultaneously, enabling the parallelisation that made scaling to billions of parameters practical and efficient in modern large language models.
As the industry developments in this article demonstrate, the recurrent paradigm is not merely historical. It continues to evolve through state space models and hybrid architectures that combine recurrent and attention-based approaches, with neural network architecture selection depending on the specific tradeoffs each application or use case demands.
Looking Ahead
My next learning reflection will re-examine a critical limitation that emerges when RNNs process long sequences: the forgetting problem. While the recurrent loop creates "memory", that memory degrades as sequences grow longer. Gradients that must propagate through many time steps during training face the same vanishing gradient challenge that affects deep feedforward networks through layer depth — but in RNNs compounded by the temporal dimension rather than network depth. (I previously introduced vanishing gradients when discussing node activation functions, though the mechanism here operates across time steps rather than layers.)
In RNNs, vanishing gradients lead to forgetting. Over time, the neural network retains less and less of earlier information, making it progressively unable to connect distant elements in a sequence. This limitation motivated the development of Long Short-Term Memory networks, which a subsequent article will explore. Sepp Hochreiter and Jürgen Schmidhuber introduced LSTMs in their foundational 1997 paper "Long Short-Term Memory" (Neural Computation, 1997), which remains among the most cited papers in deep learning to this day. LSTMs replace the simple recurrent loop with sophisticated gate mechanisms that control what information to remember, forget, or output at each time step. The eCornell program framed LSTM as the field's first systematic architectural attempt to control what information to store, not merely how much to retain.
Sherstinsky's formal analysis in "Fundamentals of Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) Network" (arXiv:1808.03314, 2023) shows that the LSTM addresses the vanishing gradient problem by introducing what the original inventors named the "Constant Error Carousel" (CEC). In a standard RNN, the training signal that teaches the neural network to connect distant events "fades away" as it travels backward through many time steps — like a message getting quieter the further it has to travel. The CEC creates a dedicated pathway that keeps the training signal circulating at full strength when needed, by selectively holding the memory gate open while the other gates dynamically control whether new information enters or existing information is released — decisions the neural network learns to make from the data itself at every time step. This is what allows LSTMs to learn from events that occurred hundreds of steps earlier — something standard RNNs cannot sustain. A subsequent article will examine how the LSTM's three gate types — which Sherstinsky terms control state, control update, and control readout, corresponding to the forget, input, and output gates in standard literature — implement this architecture in practice.
The architectural evolution from RNN to LSTM to Transformer represents the field's progressive solutions to the sequential memory challenge. Each innovation addressed specific limitations of its predecessor while building upon its foundational concepts. Understanding this progression builds the analytical framework needed for the architecture selection decisions we will formalise in later articles in this series.
This article is Part 4 of an 8-part series on neural network architectures and their uses from the "Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks" module of Cornell University's eCornell "Designing and Building AI Solutions" program, instructed by Lutz Finger .

## Article #20

**Title:** Neural Network Architectures and Their AI Uses Part 5: RNNs — The Forgetting Problem

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/neural-network-architectures-ai-uses-part-5-rnns-forgetting-lim-mykec/

**Content:**

Editor's Note
This article is Part 5 of an eight-part series documenting my learning reflections about neural network model architectures and their practical applications. These reflections are drawn from fundamental learning from the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” program module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” certificate program and two-year Cornell Johnson MBA, both of which are instructed by Lutz Finger.
My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer project continues delivering production-ready results as I build out my personal News Analyst application. As the semantic layer of my personal News Analyst application, this v2 AI Industry News Enhancer is a locally deployed system which transforms high-volume AI news into structured metadata for deeper pattern analysis. The forgetting problem explored in this article explains why my project uses a transformer-based architecture rather than a Recurrent Neural Network (RNN), and why context window limitations remain a practical concern even in modern language models.
Reflecting on the AI fundamentals taught by Lutz Finger in the eCornell program, I see a pedagogical thread that connects the sequential neural network model architecture taught in the program. RNNs control how much information to store in memory. Long Short-Term Memory networks (LSTMs) make the first architectural attempt to decide what to store in memory. Transformers complete progression by computing what to store across all sequence (or token) positions simultaneously through attention. This article examines why basic RNNs cannot solve the “what to store” problem on their own, motivating the architectural evolution that follows.
Project repository: v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production): GitHub | Hugging Face
Introduction
Part 4 introduced Recurrent Neural Networks and their revolutionary memory mechanism: the recurrent loop that feeds information back into the network across time steps. This architectural innovation enables processing of sequential data where temporal order matters, from speech recognition to fraud detection. Hidden states accumulate context from previous inputs, allowing the network to make informed predictions based on sequential history. I covered the recurrent loop, hidden states, and the U, V, and W weight matrices in detail in my Part 4 article, and this article builds directly on those foundations.
However, this memory mechanism carries a critical limitation that emerges as sequences grow longer. The eCornell program examined this limitation, revealing that the same recurrent structure enabling memory also creates a pathway for progressive information loss. The longer the sequence, the less the network remembers from its beginning.
The forgetting problem in RNNs is not merely an engineering inconvenience but a fundamental neural network model architectural constraint. It drove the development of new network architectures and continues to influence design decisions in modern AI systems. Understanding why RNNs forget provides essential context for evaluating the LSTM solution (in my learning reflection piece) and the transformer architecture that powers today’s large language models (LLMs).
Vanishing Gradients in Sequential Processing
In RNNs, gradients must propagate not only through network depth but also backward through every time step in the sequence, creating an exponentially compounding challenge that the eCornell program described as an epic journey. This dual propagation requirement distinguishes the RNN vanishing gradient problem from the feedforward version that I examined in my published optimization series Part 3 on activation functions and gradient flow.
During training, the backpropagation algorithm (David Rumelhart, Geoffrey Hinton, and Ronald Williams' "Learning Representations by Back-Propagating Errors", Nature, 1986) must trace the influence of each parameter on the final output. In a standard feedforward network, gradients flow backward through layers of depth. In an RNN, gradients must additionally flow backward through time, traversing every time step between the current position and the point where relevant context first appeared. Using a sequence of one-hundred time steps as an example, gradients must traverse one hundred multiplicative operations through the recurrent weight matrix alone.
Each backward step through time multiplies the gradient by the recurrent weight values. When these values are less than one, the gradient shrinks at each step. After many multiplicative operations, the gradient approaches zero. This mathematical decay means that the network’s ability to learn from distant time steps diminishes exponentially with distance. By the time the gradient reaches early time steps in a long sequence, it has become so small that it produces negligible weight updates.
The practical consequence is that RNNs struggle to learn relationships between elements that are far apart in a sequence. A word at position five in a sentence can easily influence the prediction at position ten. But a word at position five in a long document may have virtually no influence on the prediction at position five hundred. The gradient signal connecting those distant positions has effectively vanished during backpropagation through the intervening time steps.
The eCornell program emphasized that this is not a minor technical issue but a fundamental property of the recurrent architecture. The same weight sharing that enables RNNs to process variable-length sequences also means that every time step introduces another multiplicative factor in the gradient chain. Longer sequences inevitably produce smaller gradients for earlier positions, creating an inherent tradeoff between sequence length and memory depth.
Forgetting Equals Vanishing Gradient
In the context of RNNs, vanishing gradient is equivalent to forgetting information, because the network progressively retains less and less of earlier inputs as sequences grow longer. The eCornell program made this connection explicit, framing the mathematical phenomenon of gradient decay as a practical memory failure.
When gradients vanish for early time steps, the network cannot adjust its weights to account for information presented at those positions. This means the network effectively ignores early inputs when making predictions later in the sequence. The hidden state, which Part 4 described as a compressed representation of the entire sequence history, becomes increasingly dominated by recent inputs while losing the influence of earlier ones.
Consider a practical example in language processing. A document begins with a critical definition that subsequent paragraphs reference throughout. An RNN processing this document would gradually lose the influence of that opening definition as it processes through hundreds of subsequent words. By the time it reaches the concluding paragraph, the opening definition has effectively been “forgotten” because the gradient connecting the conclusion to the opening has vanished through hundreds of multiplicative steps.
This forgetting behavior creates measurable performance degradation for tasks requiring long-range dependencies. Yoshua Bengio, Patrice Simard, and Paolo Frasconi’s “Learning Long-Term Dependencies with Gradient Descent is Difficult” (IEEE Transactions on Neural Networks, 1994) provided the foundational mathematical formalization of this challenge, demonstrating that learning long-term temporal dependencies through gradient descent becomes increasingly difficult as the time lag between relevant events grows. The network retains excellent short-term memory but progressively loses long-term context.
The eCornell program illustrated that forgetting is not selective but indiscriminate. The network does not choose to forget irrelevant information while retaining important context. Instead, all information from early time steps fades equally as gradients vanish. This indiscriminate behavior connects to a structural property that Lutz Finger emphasized in the program: in the basic RNN, all data flows through all nodes at each time step. The network controls how much information passes through the recurrent connection through weight magnitudes, but it cannot decide what passes through. Important context receives no special treatment.
Differentiation from Feedforward Vanishing Gradient
The vanishing gradient problem in RNNs shares mathematical roots with the feedforward version but manifests differently and proves more resistant to the solutions that worked for standard neural networks. My previous article re-examined vanishing gradients in the context of feedforward neural networks. Reflecting on the AI fundamentals taught by Lutz Finger in the eCornell program, I find that understanding the distinction between these two manifestations clarifies why RNNs required a fundamentally different solution.
In feedforward neural networks, vanishing gradient occurs across layers of depth. As a recap from my [Part 1 article], a neural network consists of sequential or stacked layers of artificial neurons, with information flowing from the input layer through one or more hidden layers to the output layer. Modern neural network model architectures stack many hidden layers between input and output, and the term "depth" refers to how many layers are stacked across the neural network. Each layer multiplies the gradient by its weight values and the derivative of its activation function. Deep neural networks with many layers compound this multiplication, causing gradients to shrink as they propagate from the output layer back to the input layer. The classic example involves the sigmoid activation function, whose maximum derivative of 0.25 guarantees gradient decay at every layer.
In RNNs, vanishing gradient occurs across time steps in addition to depth. The temporal dimension adds an entirely separate axis of multiplicative gradient decay. Even a shallow RNN with minimal depth can suffer severe vanishing gradient if the sequence is long enough, because the recurrent weight matrix multiplies the gradient at every time step. This temporal propagation compounds with any depth-based gradient decay, making the overall problem significantly worse.
The distinction matters because solutions for feedforward vanishing gradient do not adequately address the RNN version. Improved activation functions like ReLU effectively mitigated vanishing gradient in feedforward networks by replacing the sigmoid’s maximum derivative of 0.25 with a constant derivative of 1 for positive values. However, ReLU addresses only the depth component. The temporal component persists because it operates through the recurrent weight matrix rather than through derivatives of activation function.
Therefore, the activation function fix may be insufficient for recurrent networks. Changing the activation function reduces gradient decay through network depth, but the recurrent weight multiplication at each time step continues to compound. Eventually this temporal decay overwhelms any improvement from better activation functions, motivating the search for architectural rather than functional solutions.
Why Activation Functions Cannot Solve This
Reflecting further on the AI fundamentals taught by Lutz Finger in the eCornell program, I see why functional solutions fail and why architectural innovation becomes necessary for the RNN forgetting problem. This section examines the mechanism in more detail.
ReLU and its variants solve vanishing gradient by maintaining gradient magnitude through network depth. When the input is positive, ReLU passes the gradient through unchanged with a derivative of 1. This prevents the multiplicative shrinkage that sigmoid and tanh activation functions impose at every layer. For feedforward networks, this solution proved remarkably effective, enabling training of much deeper architectures than previously possible.
However, the RNN vanishing gradient problem has two components, and activation function improvements address only one. The depth component responds to activation function changes just as it does in feedforward networks. The temporal component, however, operates through the recurrent weight V that connects hidden states across time steps. This weight matrix multiplication occurs regardless of the activation function used, because it represents a fundamentally different operation than the nonlinear transformation that activation functions perform.
The temporal gradient decay follows from the weight sharing property that enables RNNs to process variable-length sequences. As Part 4 explained, the same V weight matrix applies at every time step. During backpropagation through time, the gradient is multiplied by V at every step. When the recurrent weights effectively shrink the gradient at each step, the gradient decays exponentially across long sequences. When they effectively amplify it, the gradient explodes. This narrow stability band proves extremely difficult to maintain across long sequences, a property formalized in Pascanu, Mikolov, and Bengio’s “On the Difficulty of Training Recurrent Neural Networks” (ICML, 2013), which extended the foundational vanishing gradient analysis and introduced gradient clipping as a partial mitigation for the exploding gradient case.
The recurrent loop itself creates the temporal gradient pathway, the route through which gradients flow backward across time steps. No activation function change can fix the gradient decay through this pathway. Activation functions operate at the neuron (or node) level while the gradient decay originates from the recurrent weight matrix. The latter is the root cause. Therefore the challenge becomes redesigning how information flows through the recurrent connection, not merely adjusting how individual neurons process their inputs.
The Need for Architectural Innovation
The inability of activation function improvements to solve RNN forgetting motivated researchers to develop entirely new architectural mechanisms for controlling information flow through recurrent connections. This insight from the eCornell program frames the transition from basic RNNs to more sophisticated sequential architectures.
The core problem traces back to the “how much” limitation that Part 4 examined in detail: basic RNNs control quantity through weight magnitudes but cannot select content. This lack of selectivity means that vanishing gradients affect important and unimportant information equally, creating the indiscriminate forgetting that earlier sections of this article described.
What sequential processing requires is a mechanism that decides what to store, not merely how much to store. The architectural innovation needs to provide selective memory: the ability to explicitly retain specific information across many time steps while allowing other information to decay naturally. This selective retention would prevent important long-range context from being overwhelmed by the accumulation of more recent but potentially less relevant inputs.
Lutz Finger’s pedagogical framing in the eCornell program connects this requirement to a three-step architectural progression that defines the rest of this series. Basic RNN model architectures answer the question “how much to store” through weight magnitudes alone. LSTMs introduce the first architectural attempt to answer “what to store, what to forget, and what to pass through” by adding a gate-based selection mechanism between input and recurrent hidden state. Transformers complete the progression by abandoning recurrence entirely and using attention mechanisms to compute what to store across all positions simultaneously, regardless of distance. Each step in this progression resolves a specific limitation of the previous one.
My next learning reflection will refresh my learning about the LSTM architecture in detail, and explore its use in real world applications such as quantitative hedge funds. The shift from “how much” to “what” represents the conceptual hinge of sequential modeling, and the LSTM gate structure operationalizes this shift through a memory cell that information can enter and exit selectively rather than indiscriminately.
Modern Research Perspectives on the Forgetting Problem
The forgetting problem identified in this article remains an active research frontier rather than a closed historical chapter. Three threads of research illustrate how the foundational insight from the eCornell program continues to shape contemporary AI architecture work.
Foundational mathematical research has shaped how the field reasons about temporal gradient flow for over three decades. Bengio, Simard, and Frasconi’s 1994 paper cited earlier in this article established why gradient-based training cannot reliably learn long-term temporal dependencies, while Pascanu, Mikolov, and Bengio’s 2013 work cited in the previous section formalized both vanishing and exploding gradient regimes and introduced gradient clipping as a partial mitigation. Together, these papers established the formal vocabulary that the field still uses to reason about training stability in any recurrent architecture.
Recent research demonstrates that the forgetting problem persists in modern transformer-based architectures, just in modified form. Liu et al.’s “Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts” (Transactions of the Association for Computational Linguistics, 2024) found that large language models reliably retrieve information at the start and end of long contexts, but performance degrades significantly when relevant information sits in the middle. The mathematical mechanism differs from RNN gradient decay, but the practical consequence of distance-based information loss persists.
Engineering responses to the forgetting problem complement the architectural research thread. Xiao et al.’s “Efficient Streaming Language Models with Attention Sinks” (ICLR, 2024) found that retaining the initial tokens of a sequence as “attention sinks” enables stable processing across millions of tokens, addressing the same distance-based information loss that Lost in the Middle documented. These engineering solutions complement rather than replace the architectural innovations like Mamba, demonstrating that the forgetting problem remains a multi-front research frontier.
Modern selective state space models revisit the “what to store” question through input-dependent parameters. Albert Gu and Tri Dao’s “Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces” (arXiv:2312.00752, 2023), which I discussed in Part 4 within the broader RNN renaissance, addresses selectivity directly by allowing model parameters to depend on the current input token. This input-dependent control enables per-token decisions about what to remember and what to forget, achieving the selective retention that basic RNNs lacked. The architectural question raised by RNN forgetting therefore continues to drive frontier research, validating the eCornell program’s emphasis on understanding fundamental limitations as the foundation for architectural innovation.
Connecting to My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer Project
Part 4 explained why I chose transformer-based Meta Llama 3.2-1B over an RNN for processing AI industry news. This article supplies the mechanistic justification for that choice: the forgetting problem makes RNNs structurally unable to maintain coherent understanding across the long-range dependencies that news articles require. An RNN would lose the influence of opening-paragraph context as it processed subsequent paragraphs, degrading metadata extraction quality for later sections of the same article.
Lutz Finger's eCornell instruction paints the field's evolving answer to one question — how should a neural network decide what from the past is relevant now? RNNs controlled how much context survived; LSTMs added gates to select what to remember or forget; Transformers abandoned sequential processing entirely to attend to all positions simultaneously. Rather than processing sequences one step at a time and maintaining memory through recurrent connections, transformers process all positions simultaneously and use attention mechanisms to directly connect any two positions regardless of distance. This eliminates the temporal gradient decay entirely because there is no recurrent pathway through which gradients must propagate. The forgetting problem that motivated LSTMs becomes structurally impossible.
However, the forgetting problem has not been fully eliminated even in transformer architectures. Context window limitations in modern language models represent a practical descendant of the RNN forgetting challenge, as the Lost in the Middle research demonstrates. My v2 project must consider input length constraints when processing news articles, ensuring that critical information falls within the model’s effective context window. The mathematical mechanisms differ from RNN gradient decay, but the practical consequence of information loss over distance persists in modified form.
Understanding the forgetting problem across architectural generations enables informed engineering decisions. When I configured my v2 project’s input processing pipeline, awareness of how different architectures handle long-range dependencies guided decisions about text chunking, context prioritization, and output validation. The principles that the eCornell program taught about RNN forgetting apply conceptually to any system that must maintain coherent understanding across extended input sequences.
Looking Ahead
Part 6 introduces Long Short-Term Memory networks, the architectural innovation that directly addresses the forgetting problem examined in this article. Sepp Hochreiter and Jürgen Schmidhuber introduced LSTMs in their foundational 1997 paper “Long Short-Term Memory” (Neural Computation, 1997), proposing a gating mechanism that decides what to forget, what to update, and what to pass through. This structure replaces the simple recurrent loop with one that gives the network explicit control over information flow.
The LSTM’s architectural solutions to the forgetting problem is what its creators named the “Constant Error Carousel.” As I described in my previous article through Sherstinsky’s formal analysis, the Constant Error Carousel is a dedicated cell state pathway that preserves gradient magnitude across long sequences, bypassing the repeated multiplicative decay through the recurrent weight matrix. Part 6 will walk through how the gates and cell state implement this mechanism in practice.
The LSTM innovation operationalizes the conceptual shift from “how much” to “what.” Where basic RNNs control information flow through weight magnitudes alone, LSTMs introduce dedicated mechanisms that make selective decisions about retention and disposal. This represents the first architectural answer to the question that this article has identified as the core limitation of basic RNNs.
Understanding why RNNs forget provides essential context for appreciating the LSTM solution. The architectural evolution from RNN to LSTM demonstrates how identifying the precise mechanism of failure guides the design of effective solutions, a principle that applies broadly across engineering disciplines.
This article is Part 5 of an 8-part series on neural network architectures and their uses from the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” program, instructed by Lutz Finger.

## Article #21

**Title:** Neural Network Architectures and Their AI Uses Part 6: LSTMs — Gates That "Remember"

**URL:** https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/neural-network-architectures-ai-uses-part-6-lstms-gates-lim-wnhzc/

**Content:**

Editor's Note
This article is Part 6 of an eight-part series documenting my learning reflections about neural network model architectures and their practical applications. These reflections are drawn from fundamental AI learning from the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” program module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” certificate program, instructed by Lutz Finger, who also taught the AI electives during my Cornell Johnson MBA.
What I Have Been Up To
The CoreAI newsletter went quiet for a few weeks while I returned to Singapore to spend time with friends, family and industry peers. The visit ran longer than two weeks, and I am grateful for the short pause. The series now resumes with this article on Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) neural networks.
Even during that family visit, I spent the time building a project for The Gemma 4 Good Hackathon, a global challenge run by Google DeepMind and Kaggle. Built on the new open-source family of Google Gemma 4 models, I entered the Global Resilience hackathon track with SolarHive, an open-source "intelligence" layer that helps local, rural or suburban communities of households coordinate their rooftop solar generation and shared storage. A community member can photograph the sky, ask a question, and receive a grounded recommendation that draws on live weather, solar irradiance, battery/fuel cell, and grid data, with the model running locally on modest hardware rather than in the cloud. The full project, including the dataset, the fine-tuned models, and a working mobile application, is open and available on Kaggle, GitHub, and Hugging Face.
The lesson that stayed with me is that grounding beats fluency. I held the system to one rule, namely that no quantitative figure in an answer comes from the AI model itself. That rule shaped the fine-tuning, since it meant training the model to rely on its native tool calling to fetch every number from a real data source, such as live weather and irradiance API services with national labs. The principle connects directly to the architectural story in this current newsletter series, because the question of what a model should compute itself versus what it should retrieve runs through the whole progression from recurrence to attention.
My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer project continues delivering production-ready results as I build out my personal News Analyst application. Serving as the semantic layer of that system, the v2 AI Industry News Enhancer is a locally deployed pipeline which transforms high-volume AI news into structured metadata for deeper pattern analysis. The gating mechanisms explored in this article represent a crucial evolutionary step toward the transformer architecture that powers the language model in my project.
Understanding LSTM gates illuminates how neural networks learned to make selective decisions about memory, a capability that proved foundational for modern sequential processing. The progression from simple recurrence to gated memory demonstrates how targeted architectural innovation solves specific computational problems.
Project repository: v2 AI Industry News Enhancer (Production): GitHub | Hugging Face
Introduction
In my previous learning reflection article, we examined the forgetting problem that limits basic Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN), where vanishing gradients cause progressive loss of early sequence information (or the information from the beginning of any sequences). We established that this limitation requires an architectural solution rather than merely an improved activation function, because the recurrent loop itself creates the temporal gradient pathway responsible for forgetting.
This personal learning reflection article introduces Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) neural networks, the model architectural innovation that directly addresses RNN's "forgetting problem" through a sophisticated system of gates. The eCornell program presented LSTMs as the first successful attempt to give neural networks selective control over what information to remember and what to discard. Rather than treating all sequential information identically, LSTMs make explicit decisions at each time step.
The fundamental shift that LSTMs introduce moves the central question from “how much” to “what.” Basic RNNs control only the quantity of information flowing through their recurrent connection. LSTMs determine the specific content that deserves preservation, the new information worth incorporating, and the relevant context worth communicating. This selective capability transformed sequential processing and enabled applications that simple RNNs could never achieve effectively.
The LSTM Architectural Innovation
Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) neural networks replace the simple recurrent loop with a gated structure that actively decides what information to forget, update, or pass through at each time step. The eCornell program described this innovation as the first architecture to address content selectivity rather than mere quantity control.
The name itself captures the architectural goal: maintaining both long-term and short-term memory within a single coherent structure. Basic RNNs excel at short-term memory but lose long-term context as sequences extend. LSTM designers specifically built the architecture to preserve important information across many time steps while still incorporating recent inputs appropriately.
The core architectural difference lies in how information flows through the neural network. Instead of looping everything back through a single recurrent connection, LSTMs introduce a gate or switch mechanism. At each time step, this mechanism can choose to forget existing information, update memory with new information, or pass information through unchanged. The eCornell program emphasized that this represented the first deliberate attempt to decide what to store rather than only how much.
One historical nuance deserves mention. The modern three-gate cell (1/ forget, 2/ input, 3/ output) described throughout this current newsletter series reflects the LSTM architecture as practitioners commonly teach and deploy it today. However, the original 1997 design by Sepp Hochreiter and Jürgen Schmidhuber contained only the input and output gates alongside the protected memory pathway. Felix Gers, Jürgen Schmidhuber, and Fred Cummins introduced the forget gate three years later in “Learning to Forget: Continual Prediction with LSTM” (Neural Computation, 2000), recognizing that a cell which can never reset its contents will eventually saturate. This refinement on top of the original architecture itself illustrates the article’s theme, namely that targeted innovation resolves a specific and well understood failure mode.
This "information" selectivity directly addresses the indiscriminate forgetting problem observed in basic RNN model architectures. Where a standard RNN allows all early information to fade equally as gradients vanish, an LSTM can explicitly preserve specific important context regardless of sequence length. The gates learn through training which information patterns deserve long-term retention and which can safely decay, providing the selective memory that sequential processing demands.
Cell State as Information Highway
The cell state provides a dedicated pathway for information to flow across many time steps with minimal alteration, functioning as an information highway that runs parallel to the network’s primary processing. The program materials visualize this architectural feature in the LSTM diagram, and it represents the key mechanism that solves the vanishing gradient problem.
This protected pathway is precisely what the LSTM’s creators named the Constant Error Carousel, the mechanism I previewed in my Part 5 article through Sherstinsky’s formal analysis. The Constant Error Carousel is the cell state itself, a dedicated route that keeps the training signal circulating at full strength across long sequences rather than letting it fade through repeated multiplication by the recurrent weight matrix. The sections that follow walk through how the gates and the cell state implement this mechanism in practice, closing the loop opened in Part 5.
Unlike the hidden state that undergoes transformation at every step, the cell state undergoes only carefully controlled modifications through the gates. Information can travel along the cell state across numerous time steps without the repeated multiplicative operations that cause gradient decay in basic RNNs.
The update itself combines two operations rather than relying on addition alone. The forget gate scales the previous cell state by a value between zero and one, which is a multiplicative operation, while the input gate adds new candidate information on top of the result. The gradient preservation that protects against vanishing comes from this additive structure working together with a forget gate that learns to stay near one, holding the cell state open so that the signal carries forward intact. Describing the cell state as purely additive captures the intuition, but the forget gate’s multiplicative role gives the network its selective control over how long information persists.
The mathematical significance of additive updates is hard to overstate. Basic RNNs suffer vanishing gradients because each time step multiplies the gradient by the recurrent weight matrix, causing exponential decay. The cell state, by contrast, carries information forward through an additive update. Addition preserves gradient magnitude far better than multiplication, allowing gradients to flow across many time steps without vanishing.
Sigmoid functions serve as each gate's activation function, and their zero-to-one output range is what makes them suitable for gating. A sigmoid output near zero closes a gate, blocking information flow, while an output near one opens a gate, permitting full information passage. This continuous control allows gates to make nuanced decisions rather than binary choices, regulating precisely how much information flows through each pathway. Tanh transformations complement the sigmoid gates by scaling values to a range between negative one and one, preparing information for storage or output.
How Gates Solve Vanishing Gradient
The gated architecture solves the vanishing gradient problem by creating a protected pathway through the cell state where gradients flow without the multiplicative decay that affects basic RNNs. This section examines the mechanism that enables LSTMs to maintain long-range dependencies where simple recurrent networks fail.
The forget gate can learn to remain open, preserving important information indefinitely. When the forget gate produces values near one, the cell state retains its contents across time steps. This allows critical context to persist regardless of sequence length, directly countering the indiscriminate forgetting that vanishing gradients impose on basic RNNs. The network learns which information deserves this protected preservation through training.
Gradients flowing backward through the cell state encounter addition operations rather than repeated weight multiplication. During backpropagation through time, the cell state pathway allows gradients to traverse many time steps while maintaining their magnitude. This preserved gradient signal enables the network to learn relationships between distant sequence elements, the precise capability that vanishing gradients destroy in basic RNNs.
The contrast with basic RNN memory mechanisms reveals the elegance of the gated solution. A standard RNN forces all information through a single recurrent pathway where each step multiplies the gradient, guaranteeing eventual decay. An LSTM provides multiple controlled pathways, with the cell state offering protected long-term storage while the gates manage selective updates. This architectural separation of concerns allows LSTMs to maintain both stable long-term memory and responsive short-term processing simultaneously.
LSTM Limitations
Despite solving the forgetting problem, LSTMs retain a fundamental architectural constraint that limits their efficiency: they must process inputs in strict temporal order, which prevents parallelization and blocks awareness of future context. The eCornell program identified these limitations as the motivation for the eventual development of transformer architectures.
Sequential processing prevents the parallelization that modern hardware enables. Because each LSTM time step depends on the hidden state and cell state from the previous step, the network cannot process multiple positions simultaneously. This sequential dependency creates a computational bottleneck, particularly for long sequences, because each step must complete before the next begins. Graphics processing units that excel at parallel computation cannot fully accelerate inherently sequential LSTM processing.
Current input receives context only from previous inputs, never from future ones. The eCornell program emphasized that LSTMs process inputs in strict temporal order, meaning each position has access to preceding context but remains blind to subsequent information. For many language tasks, understanding a word fully requires knowing what follows it, not just what precedes it. This unidirectional context limitation constrains LSTM performance on tasks requiring comprehensive sequence understanding.
These limitations persist even though LSTMs successfully solve the vanishing gradient problem. The gated architecture enables long-range memory but does nothing to address the sequential processing bottleneck or the lack of future context. Solving these remaining constraints required yet another architectural innovation, one that abandoned recurrence entirely in favor of a fundamentally different approach to sequential processing.
A Preview of Transformers
As discussed in previous articles, transformers eliminate this aforementioned sequential processing constraint and unidirectional context limitation by replacing LSTM's recurrent layer and gates with a dedicated attention mechanism. The eCornell program positioned transformers as the architecture that addresses the architectural constraints inherent in both RNNs and LSTMs.
Rather than using a time loop or gate structure, transformers employ a dedicated model for attention. This attention mechanism determines how much focus each element in a sequence should receive relative to every other element, and how long information needs to be retained. By computing relationships between all positions simultaneously, transformers process sequences in parallel rather than step by step.
The attention mechanism allows any position to directly access any other position regardless of distance. This eliminates the vanishing gradient problem entirely because no recurrent pathway exists through which gradients must propagate sequentially. It also provides bidirectional context, allowing each position to consider both preceding and following elements when forming its representation.
Modern large language models build upon the transformer architecture. The systems from OpenAI, Mistral, Meta Llama, and Google Gemini, along with Google's latest open-source Gemma 4 model family, all share this foundation. These models represent the culmination of the architectural evolution traced through this series, from basic RNNs through LSTMs to the attention-based transformers that power contemporary artificial intelligence. The conceptual lineage connects directly to the sequential processing challenges that the eCornell program examined.
Industry Developments: LSTM Gates in Modern Practice
Yet, the gating mechanisms examined in this article did not fade into history when transformers arrived; they continue to drive active research and remain deployed across demanding industry domains. The most direct evidence arrives from the architecture's own lineage. Maximilian Beck, Sepp Hochreiter, and colleagues revisited the design in “xLSTM: Extended Long Short-Term Memory” (NeurIPS, 2024), asking how far gated recurrence scales when equipped with modern techniques. The fact that one of the original 1997 LSTM authors returned to extend the architecture nearly three decades later signals that the gating principle retains real research value. xLSTM introduces exponential gating and a revised memory structure, yet it preserves the forget, input, and output gate logic at its core, demonstrating that the selective memory idea this article describes remains a living foundation rather than a closed chapter.
Quantitative finance, and quantitative hedge funds in particular, offers one of the clearest demonstrations of LSTM gates solving a real production problem. Financial markets generate sequential data with exactly the long-range dependencies and noisy short-term fluctuations that gated memory was designed to manage. The application domains below illustrate how the forget, input, and output gates translate into trading and risk infrastructure.
These financial industry applications extend the same lesson the eCornell program taught through its own sequential use cases. Lutz Finger illustrated gated and recurrent memory through fraud detection, where a model must read a sequence of transactions to recognize a pattern such as a fraudster who starts with small amounts and steadily increases them, alongside speech recognition and the program's deforestation example of identifying a chainsaw within a sequence of forest sounds. The trading and risk systems described above apply that identical principle, namely that sequential data carries meaning in its order, to a domain where the sequence is a stream of market events rather than sounds or transactions.
High-frequency trading desks apply LSTM gates to limit order book forecasting, where predicting short-term price movement requires sifting signal from extreme noise. Zihao Zhang, Stefan Zohren, and Stephen Roberts established the influential DeepLOB architecture in “DeepLOB: Deep Convolutional Neural Networks for Limit Order Books” (IEEE Transactions on Signal Processing, 2019), pairing convolutional layers with an LSTM layer so that the gates handle the temporal dimension of order flow while convolutions capture spatial structure across price levels. Antonio Briola and co-authors extended this line of work in “Deep limit order book forecasting: a microstructural guide” (Quantitative Finance, 2025), releasing an open framework that benchmarks these sequence models on heterogeneous NASDAQ stocks and cautions that strong forecasting accuracy does not automatically translate into profitable trading signals (open-access version on arXiv).
Risk management and derivatives desks use gated sequence models as fast surrogates for pricing and sensitivity calculation. The CFA Institute Research Foundation's 2025 treatment of deep learning in finance describes how LSTM and GRU models extract short-horizon signals from limit order books while neural surrogates recalibrate volatility surfaces and produce stable risk sensitivities in near real time. The same gating logic that preserves a definition across a long document here preserves market context across a long trading session, feeding intraday risk and valuation systems that must respond within milliseconds.
Financial asset portfolio construction and execution teams embed gated recurrence inside larger hybrid systems rather than discarding it. Across alpha research, portfolio allocation, and trade execution, practitioners increasingly combine LSTM layers with convolutional and attention components, letting each mechanism handle the part of the problem it solves best. This pattern echoes the broader industry move toward hybrid architectures, where gated recurrence contributes efficient sequential memory alongside the global context that attention provides. The same design appears well beyond finance. Seongho Hong, Taek-Kyu Chung, Byeong-Soo Yoo, and Sung-Ryul Kim’s “Innovative LSTM-transformer hybrid models for predicting consolidation settlement” (Acta Geotechnica, 2025) pairs an LSTM layer that captures temporal dependencies with a transformer that models long-range feature interactions, outperforming both a standalone transformer and a traditional engineering method on real port-construction data. The result reinforces a consistent theme, namely that gated recurrence and attention work better together than either does alone.
Recent large-scale benchmarks confirm that gated recurrence remains highly competitive on risk-adjusted financial performance. Adir Saly-Kaufmann and colleagues, including Stefan Zohren of the Oxford-Man Institute of Quantitative Finance, evaluated linear models, recurrent networks, transformers, and state space models on a daily futures dataset spanning commodities, equities, bonds, and foreign exchange from 2010 through 2025 in “Deep Learning for Financial Time Series: A Large-Scale Benchmark of Risk-Adjusted Performance” (arXiv, 2026). A hybrid combining Variable Selection Networks with an LSTM achieved the highest overall Sharpe ratio, while the extended LSTM demonstrated the largest breakeven transaction cost buffer, indicating strong robustness to real trading frictions. That the same researcher behind the DeepLOB architecture now finds gated recurrence leading a modern benchmark underscores how durable the approach has proven.
A complementary study cautions that simpler gated units can outperform their more elaborate successors under market noise. Benchmarking the scalar and matrix LSTM units against the full extended architecture and the TimesNet model across six financial datasets, researchers found that the core scalar and matrix units consistently delivered superior forecasting accuracy, surpassing even the more structurally complex configuration (Mathematics, 2026). This result reinforces a theme worth carrying through the series, namely that added architectural complexity does not guarantee better performance when the underlying data is noisy and non-stationary.
Beyond financial application, gated recurrence remains the deployed choice wherever inference must run continuously under tight memory and latency budgets. Healthcare monitoring is the clearest example. Ankit Gupta, Ruchi Chauhan, and colleagues developed SepsisAI in “Improving sepsis prediction in intensive care with SepsisAI” (PLOS Digital Health, 2024), an LSTM-based clinical decision support system that monitors a patient’s vital signs in real time and predicts the onset of hospital-acquired sepsis hours in advance, with an alert layer tuned to reduce the false alarms that drive clinician fatigue. The same properties that recommend gated recurrence here, efficient sequential inference and a modest memory footprint, are the ones Lutz Finger emphasized when describing short-term memory tasks, and they explain why LSTMs continue to run inside real-time systems even as transformers dominate the research headlines.
Modern Research Perspectives on Gated Memory
The gates described in this article remain an active research frontier, with several threads extending, refining, and re-examining the original design. Four lines of work illustrate how the selective memory principle continues to shape contemporary architecture research.
Historical research on the forget gate clarifies that the modern LSTM emerged through iterative refinement rather than a single act of invention. Felix Gers, Jürgen Schmidhuber, and Fred Cummins introduced the forget gate in “Learning to Forget: Continual Prediction with LSTM” (Neural Computation, 2000), three years after the original architecture appeared. Their work recognized that a memory cell which can never reset its own contents will eventually saturate and lose its ability to discriminate, and the forget gate gave the network explicit control over when to clear stale context. This refinement is the same gate that now sits at the center of every standard LSTM diagram, a reminder that targeted architectural innovation often resolves a specific and well understood failure mode.
Forecasting research demonstrates that gated recurrence still competes with transformers and state space models on sequential prediction tasks. Musleh Alharthi and Ausif Mahmood adapted the extended architecture for long-term time series prediction in “xLSTMTime: Long-Term Time Series Forecasting with xLSTM” (arXiv, 2024), pairing exponential gating with a revised memory structure to handle longer horizons. Maurice Kraus and colleagues advanced this further in “xLSTM-Mixer: Multivariate Time Series Forecasting by Mixing via Scalar Memories” (NeurIPS, 2025), which refines a shared linear forecast through stacked gated blocks that mix temporal and cross-variate information. Yaxuan Kong, Stefan Zohren, and colleagues took a complementary route in “Unlocking the Power of LSTM for Long Term Time Series Forecasting” (AAAI, 2025), proposing P-sLSTM, which adapts the scalar memory unit for forecasting by adding patching and channel independence to overcome its short memory limitation, reaching state-of-the-art results. That the senior author behind the DeepLOB work also leads this forecasting advance reinforces how continuous the gated-memory research thread has been. These results matter for the financial forecasting applications discussed above, where gated memory must track many interacting series at once.
Scaling research re-examines whether gated recurrence can rival transformers at the largest model sizes. The xLSTM line of work directly tests how gated memory behaves when scaled to billions of parameters, reporting competitive performance against state-of-the-art transformers and state space models on both quality and efficiency. Maximilian Beck and colleagues sharpened this comparison in “xLSTM Scaling Laws: Competitive Performance with Linear Time-Complexity” (arXiv:2510.02228, ICLR 2026), studying model sizes from eighty million to seven billion parameters and training budgets up to two trillion tokens. The authors report that xLSTM is pareto-dominant over dense transformers in their setup, meaning that for a fixed compute budget the gated models reach lower loss, and for a fixed loss they require fewer operations, while retaining linear complexity in context length where attention scales quadratically. The same team demonstrated the practical payoff in “xLSTM 7B: A Recurrent LLM for Fast and Efficient Inference” (arXiv:2503.13427, ICML 2025), a seven-billion-parameter model that pairs the architecture’s linear compute scaling with constant memory usage, delivering faster inference than comparable transformer-based models. This investigation reopens a question many considered settled, namely whether recurrence or attention represents the more durable foundation for sequence modeling, and it suggests the answer may be more nuanced than the transformer’s current dominance implies.
The newest frontier work returns directly to the forget gate as the conceptual ancestor of test-time memory. Ali Behrouz and colleagues at Google introduced Titans in “Learning to Memorize at Test Time” (arXiv, 2025), a family of architectures that pairs short-term attention with a separate neural long-term memory module updated while the model runs. The design mirrors the very logic this article describes, treating attention as short-term memory and a dedicated module as long-term memory, an echo of the name Long Short-Term Memory itself. The authors explicitly frame their memory decay mechanism as a generalization of the forgetting mechanism in modern recurrent models, drawing a straight line from the LSTM forget gate to a 2025 architecture that scales to context windows beyond two million tokens. The gate that Gers and colleagues added in 2000 thus reappears, decades later, at the center of one of the most ambitious recent attempts to extend machine memory.
This active research frontier validates the eCornell program's framing of architecture as an evolving toolkit rather than a settled hierarchy. Lutz Finger presented RNNs and LSTMs as strong structures for sequential data where a neural network must hold part of the information in short-term memory, while pointing ahead to attention as the mechanism that would lift their remaining limits. The continued refinement of gated memory, from the forget gate through xLSTM, shows that the question the program posed, namely how a neural network should decide what to store and for how long, remains genuinely open rather than closed by the arrival of transformers.
Connecting to My v2 AI Industry News Enhancer Project
My v2 project uses Meta Llama 3.2-1B, a transformer-based architecture whose conceptual foundations trace directly through the LSTM innovations examined in this article. Understanding LSTM gates provides essential context for appreciating how modern language models manage information across long text sequences.
The selective memory capability that LSTMs introduced through gates evolved into the attention mechanisms my project relies upon. Where LSTM gates decide what to remember and forget at each time step, transformer attention decides which parts of the input deserve focus when processing each element. Both approaches solve the same fundamental challenge of selective information processing, though through different architectural mechanisms.
Recognizing this evolutionary pathway informs practical decisions about how my project handles sequential text. The principles of selective retention, controlled information flow, and managed context that LSTMs pioneered continue to influence how I configure input processing, manage context windows, and validate outputs in my transformer-based system. The architectural history provides conceptual grounding for contemporary engineering choices.
Looking Ahead
My next reflection article will transition from examining individual architectures to synthesizing a comprehensive framework for matching neural network designs to specific business objectives. Having explored CNNs for spatial data and the RNN to LSTM progression for sequential data, we now possess the foundation to develop systematic guidance for architecture selection.
In this next reflection article, the central principle will establish that AI model architecture selection must begin with the business objective. The eCornell program emphasized that understanding what you want to achieve determines which architectural configuration serves your needs. Without clear objectives, selecting among the available architectures becomes guesswork rather than informed engineering. I will refresh our previous newsletter series to visualize a complete use case mapping that connects common business problems to appropriate architectures. From image classification to clustering, regression to forecasting, and recommendation to natural language processing, each problem type aligns with specific architectural strengths. This mapping transforms the fundamental learning about AI model architectures into actionable knowledge for successful, outcome-driven AI deployments.
This article is Part 6 of an 8-part series on neural network architectures and their uses from the “Expanding AI Power and Value Through Neural Networks” module of Cornell University’s eCornell “Designing and Building AI Solutions” program, instructed by Lutz Finger.
